(d) Award (M1)(A1) for recomputing the overall rate as $0.04$, the (A1) for the conditional rate $0.04$, and the (R1)(AG) for observing that the two are equal. Follow through from an incorrect part (a) is allowed provided the equality is correctly tested.

23. A logarithmic inequality proved from the sign of a derivative

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.6-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.6 (derivative of x^n, n in Q; derivative of sin x, cos x, e^x and ln x); AHL 5.13 (evaluation of limits) · difficulty 5

(a) $h(1)=\ln 1-\dfrac{2(1-1)}{1+1}=0-0=0$. (A1) For the derivative, differentiate the rational term by the quotient rule:
$$\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{2(x-1)}{x+1}\right)=\frac{2(x+1)-2(x-1)}{(x+1)^{2}}=\frac{4}{(x+1)^{2}}$$ (M1)(A1)
so, since $\dfrac{d}{dx}\ln x=\dfrac{1}{x}$, (M1)
$$h'(x)=\frac{1}{x}-\frac{4}{(x+1)^{2}}=\frac{(x+1)^{2}-4x}{x(x+1)^{2}}=\frac{x^{2}-2x+1}{x(x+1)^{2}}=\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{x(x+1)^{2}}$$ (A1)(AG)
The numerator completes to a perfect square, which is the fact the rest of the question depends on.

(b) For $x>0$ we have $x>0$ and $(x+1)^{2}>0$, so the denominator $x(x+1)^{2}$ is positive, while $(x-1)^{2}\ge 0$ for every real $x$. Hence $h'(x)\ge 0$ on the whole domain, with equality only at $x=1$. (M1)(A1) Therefore $h$ is increasing on $(0,\infty)$. (R1) Since $h(1)=0$, it follows that
$$x>1 \Rightarrow h(x)>0 \Rightarrow \ln x>\frac{2(x-1)}{x+1},\qquad 0<x<1 \Rightarrow h(x)<0 \Rightarrow \ln x<\frac{2(x-1)}{x+1}.$$ (A1)(A1)
The inequality reverses at $x=1$, and that reversal is the point: the two expressions are equal at $x=1$ and the comparison changes direction there rather than holding one way throughout.

(c) As $x\to\infty$, the rational term satisfies $\dfrac{2(x-1)}{x+1}=\dfrac{2-2/x}{1+1/x}\to 2$, whereas $\ln x\to\infty$. (M1)(A1) Hence $h(x)\to\infty$. (A1) The bound is therefore true but increasingly weak: it never exceeds $2$, so for large $x$ it says only that $\ln x$ is bigger than a quantity that has stopped growing. (R1) What makes the inequality interesting is the opposite end. At $x=1$ the two sides are equal and, since $h'(1)=0$, their first derivatives agree as well, so near $x=1$ the rational expression approximates $\ln x$ to second order and the bound is excellent; the same statement degrades steadily as $x$ moves away from $1$.

(d) Put $x=2$, which is greater than $1$, so the first case of part (b) applies: (M1)
$$\ln 2>\frac{2(2-1)}{2+1}=\frac{2}{3}.$$ (A1)
Hence $\ln 2$ is the larger of the two. (A1) No decimal value is needed, and none is available without a calculator, which is exactly why the inequality is worth having; the conclusion is rigorous even though the gap is only about $0.026$. (R1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The quotient rule must be shown rather than quoted, because the simplification to a single square is where the mark is earned. Award (A1) for $4/(x+1)^{2}$ and do not accept an unsimplified $\big[(x+1)^{2}-4x\big]$ numerator as final. (b) The sign argument must name both factors: positivity of the denominator and non-negativity of the square. A candidate who says only 'the derivative is positive' has skipped the case $x=1$ and should not receive the final mark. Award the reversal in full only if both directions are given; a candidate who states the $x>1$ case alone has done half the question. (c) Accept any correct evaluation of the limit; the comment mark is for noticing that a bound that tends to $2$ cannot track a function that tends to infinity. Credit the observation about agreement of value and gradient at $x=1$ as an alternative acceptable comment. (d) The word 'hence' is load-bearing: the mark is for substituting $x=2$ into the part (b) result, not for quoting a decimal for $\ln 2$. A candidate who writes $\ln 2 \approx 0.693$ and compares has not answered the question as set and should be credited only for the conclusion.

24. P3 extended problem: parameter range for an inequality

[16 marks]MATH-P3-005

Topic 5: Calculus · AHL 5.13 (evaluation of limits), AHL 5.14 (implicit differentiation, related rates), SL 5.8 (local maximum and minimum points) · difficulty 5

(a) Differentiating, using the product rule on $x\ln x$:
$$f'(x) = \ln x + x\cdot\frac{1}{x} - a = \ln x + 1 - a$$ (M1)(A1)
Setting $f'(x)=0$ gives $\ln x = a-1$, so $x = e^{a-1}$. Since $\ln x$ is strictly increasing on $x>0$, there is exactly one solution for any real $a$, and it is positive, so it lies in the domain. (A1)
$$f''(x) = \frac{1}{x} > 0 \quad \text{for all } x>0$$
so the single stationary point is a minimum. (A1)

(b) At $x = e^{a-1}$ we have $\ln x = a-1$, so (M1)
$$f\bigl(e^{a-1}\bigr) = e^{a-1}(a-1) - a\bigl(e^{a-1}-1\bigr)$$ (A1)
$$= e^{a-1}\bigl[(a-1)-a\bigr] + a$$ (M1)
$$= -e^{a-1} + a = a - e^{a-1}$$ (A1)(AG)

(c) Because $f$ has a single minimum and $f(x)\to\infty$ as $x\to\infty$, the condition $f(x)\ge 0$ for all $x>0$ is equivalent to the minimum being at least zero. (M1) (The left-hand endpoint needs a separate check: as $x\to0^{+}$, $x\ln x\to0$ and $-a(x-1)\to a$, so $f(x)\to a$, and so we also need $a\ge 0$.) (A1)

So we require
$$g(a) = a - e^{a-1} \ge 0.$$
Now treat $g$ as a function of $a$ and maximise it. (M1)
$$g'(a) = 1 - e^{a-1}, \qquad g''(a) = -e^{a-1} < 0$$
so $g'(a) = 0$ at $a = 1$ and this is a maximum. (M1)(A1)
$$g(1) = 1 - e^{0} = 1 - 1 = 0$$
Therefore $g(a) \le 0$ for every real $a$, with equality only at $a=1$. (A1)

Since we need $g(a)\ge 0$ and $g(a)\le 0$ always, the only possibility is $g(a)=0$, which forces $a=1$. This also satisfies the endpoint condition $a\ge0$. (A1)

Answer: $a=1$ is the only value. For this value $f(x) = x\ln x - x + 1 \ge 0$ for all $x>0$, which is the standard inequality $x\ln x \ge x-1$ with equality at $x=1$.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the product rule on $x\ln x$. The uniqueness argument needs the monotonicity of $\ln$, not merely a sketch; award the final (A1) only if $f''>0$ or an equivalent sign argument is given. (b) Follow-through applies from an incorrect stationary point. The subtraction in the second line is where most errors occur: award (M1) for collecting the $e^{a-1}$ terms even if the bracket is wrong. (c) This part is where the question is won. Award (M1) for recognising that a single minimum reduces the condition to one inequality, and a further (A1) for the separate $x\to0^{+}$ check, which is easy to miss because the minimum does not occur at the endpoint. The crucial step is maximising $g$; a candidate who solves $g(a)=0$ numerically and reports $a=1$ without showing it is the only root has not justified uniqueness. Note that $a=1$ also satisfies $a\ge0$, so the endpoint condition is consistent rather than additional. Accept a graphical argument for the maximisation provided the shape of $g$ is established analytically.

25. P3 extended problem: generating functions

[16 marks]MATH-P3-006

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.11 (partial fractions), SL 1.8 (sum of infinite convergent geometric sequences), AHL 1.10 (counting principles) · difficulty 5

(a) A strip of one cell can only be covered by a square, in $2$ colours, so $u_1 = 2$. (A1) A strip of two cells is either two squares ($2\times2 = 4$ ways) or one domino ($3$ ways), so $u_2 = 4+3 = 7$. (A1)

For $n\ge2$, look at the first cell. (M1) If it is covered by a square, there are $2$ choices of colour and the remaining $n-1$ cells can be tiled in $u_{n-1}$ ways, giving $2u_{n-1}$. If it is covered by a domino, that domino covers the first two cells, there are $3$ colour choices, and the remaining $n-2$ cells can be tiled in $u_{n-2}$ ways, giving $3u_{n-2}$. (A1) These cases are exhaustive and disjoint, so $u_n = 2u_{n-1} + 3u_{n-2}$. (AG)

(b) Multiply the recurrence by $x^n$ and sum from $n=2$ to $\infty$: (M1)
$$\sum_{n\ge2} u_n x^n = 2\sum_{n\ge2} u_{n-1}x^n + 3\sum_{n\ge2} u_{n-2}x^n$$
The left side is $F(x) - u_0 - u_1 x = F(x) - 1 - 2x$. (A1) The first sum on the right is
$$2x\sum_{n\ge2} u_{n-1}x^{n-1} = 2x\sum_{m\ge1} u_m x^m = 2x\bigl(F(x)-1\bigr)$$
and the second is
$$3x^{2}\sum_{n\ge2} u_{n-2}x^{n-2} = 3x^{2}F(x).$$ (A1)(A1)
Hence
$$F - 1 - 2x = 2x(F-1) + 3x^{2}F$$
$$F\bigl(1 - 2x - 3x^{2}\bigr) = 1 - 2x + 2x = 1$$
$$F(x) = \frac{1}{1-2x-3x^{2}}$$ (A1)(AG)

(c) Factorising, $1-2x-3x^{2} = (1-3x)(1+x)$. (M1) Write
$$\frac{1}{(1-3x)(1+x)} = \frac{A}{1-3x} + \frac{B}{1+x}$$
$$1 = A(1+x) + B(1-3x)$$
Putting $x = -1$: $1 = B(1+3) = 4B$, so $B = \tfrac14$. (A1)
Putting $x = \tfrac13$: $1 = A(1+\tfrac13) = \tfrac43 A$, so $A = \tfrac34$. (A1)
Therefore
$$F(x) = \frac{3/4}{1-3x} + \frac{1/4}{1+x} = \frac34\sum_{n\ge0}(3x)^{n} + \frac14\sum_{n\ge0}(-x)^{n}$$
and comparing coefficients, $u_n = \tfrac34\,3^{n} + \tfrac14(-1)^{n}$, that is (A1)
$$u_n = \frac{3^{n+1} + (-1)^{n}}{4}.$$

(d) $u_{10} = \dfrac{3^{11} + 1}{4} = \dfrac{177147 + 1}{4} = \dfrac{177148}{4} = 44287$. (M1)(A1)
$$\frac{u_n}{3^{n}} = \frac34 + \frac{(-1)^{n}}{4\cdot3^{n}} \to \frac34 \quad \text{as } n\to\infty$$ (M1)(A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The recurrence mark is for the case analysis on the first cell; a candidate who merely asserts the recurrence without the two cases earns nothing. Award the $u_2$ mark even if obtained from the recurrence rather than directly, provided $u_0$ and $u_1$ were established first. (b) The index-shifting is the technical hurdle: award (M1) for setting up the summed equation and the (A1)s for correctly expressing each of the three sums in terms of $F$. A common error is to write the first right-hand sum as $2xF$ instead of $2x(F-1)$; condone only if corrected later. Convergence need not be discussed at this level, but a candidate who notes that the series is being treated formally should not be penalised. (c) Award (M1) for the factorisation, which is the step students most often fail to see. Accept any valid route to $A$ and $B$, including equating coefficients. The final (A1) is for reading off the coefficient, which requires recognising both geometric series. (d) Accept $44287$; the limit mark is for noticing that the sign-alternating term is divided by $3^n$ and so vanishes, leaving the coefficient of the dominant term.

26. P3 extended problem: binomial identities by coefficient extraction

[16 marks]MATH-P3-009

Topic 1: Number and algebra · SL 1.9 (the binomial theorem), AHL 1.10 (counting principles), SL 1.6 (simple deductive proof) · difficulty 5

(a) Consider $(1+x)^{2n}$. By the binomial theorem the coefficient of $x^{n}$ is $\dbinom{2n}{n}$. (M1)(A1)

On the other hand $(1+x)^{2n} = (1+x)^{n}(1+x)^{n}$, and expanding each factor separately, (M1)
$$(1+x)^{n}(1+x)^{n} = \left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}x^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}x^{j}\right).$$
The coefficient of $x^{n}$ in the product comes from all pairs with $i+j=n$, so $j = n-i$ and (M1)
$$[x^{n}] = \sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}\binom{n}{n-i}.$$
Using $\dbinom{n}{n-i} = \dbinom{n}{i}$, this is $\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}^{2}$. (A1)

The two expressions are coefficients of $x^{n}$ in the same polynomial, so they are equal:
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}^{2} = \binom{2n}{n}.$$ (AG)

(b) Let $S = \displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n} k\binom{n}{k}^{2}$. (M1)
Substitute $k \mapsto n-k$; as $k$ runs from $0$ to $n$ so does $n-k$, and $\dbinom{n}{n-k} = \dbinom{n}{k}$, so (M1)(A1)
$$S = \sum_{k=0}^{n}(n-k)\binom{n}{n-k}^{2} = \sum_{k=0}^{n}(n-k)\binom{n}{k}^{2}.$$
Adding the two expressions for $S$: (M1)
$$2S = \sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl(k + n - k\bigr)\binom{n}{k}^{2} = n\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}^{2} = n\binom{2n}{n},$$
using part (a). Hence (A1)
$$S = \frac{n}{2}\binom{2n}{n}.$$

(c) Consider $(1-x)^{n}(1+x)^{n} = (1-x^{2})^{n}$. (M1)
Expanding the two factors separately and extracting the coefficient of $x^{n}$, exactly as in (a): (M1)
$$[x^{n}]\,(1-x)^{n}(1+x)^{n} = \sum_{k=0}^{n}\left[(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}\right]\binom{n}{n-k} = \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}^{2},$$
which is the required sum. (A1)

On the other hand, $(1-x^{2})^{n} = \displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}(-1)^{j}x^{2j}$, which contains only even powers of $x$. (M1)

If $n$ is odd, the coefficient of $x^{n}$ is therefore $0$. (A1)

If $n$ is even, say $n = 2m$, the coefficient of $x^{2m}$ is $(-1)^{m}\dbinom{n}{m} = (-1)^{n/2}\dbinom{n}{n/2}$. (A1)

Hence
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}^{2} = \begin{cases} 0, & n \text{ odd},\[4pt] (-1)^{n/2}\dbinom{n}{n/2}, & n \text{ even}.\end{cases}$$

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1)s are for writing the single power as a product of two expansions and for identifying the pairs with $i+j=n$; the symmetry $\binom{n}{n-i}=\binom{n}{i}$ must be stated. A candidate who tries induction will find the inductive step intractable and should be credited only for the base case. (b) The substitution $k\mapsto n-k$ is the whole idea and earns (M1); the second (M1) is for adding the two forms. Award the final (A1) only if part (a) is used, since the question says 'hence'. (c) The recognition that $(1-x)^n(1+x)^n=(1-x^2)^n$ earns (M1) and is the crux. The parity split must be explicit: a candidate who gives only the even case has lost half the marks. Check the sign of the even case carefully, since $(-1)^{n/2}$ is positive when $n/2$ is even and negative when $n/2$ is odd; a candidate who writes $(-1)^n$ has the wrong period. Candidates may verify small cases, and doing so is encouraged. Numerical checking is a legitimate route to the answer in (c) but never to the proof: for $n=2$ the sum is $1-4+1=-2$, which is $-\binom{2}{1}$, and for $n=3$ it is $1-9+9-1=0$; a candidate who spots the parity from these two cases but gives no argument for general $n$ has guessed, not shown. In (b) the final value is $\frac{n}{2}\binom{2n}{n}$, which is an integer, and a candidate who reports $\frac{n}{2}$ times the part (a) sum without simplifying has not used 'hence' as instructed. Do not penalise index notation differences between $i$, $j$ and $k$.

27. The product of the chords of a regular polygon

[16 marks]MATH-P3-011

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.13 (modulus-argument (polar) form); AHL 1.14 (complex conjugate roots of quadratic and polynomial equations) · difficulty 5

(a) The sum is a finite geometric series with first term $1$, common ratio $\omega$ and $n$ terms, and $\omega \neq 1$ because $n \ge 2$: (M1)
$$1 + \omega + \cdots + \omega^{n-1} = \frac{1 - \omega^{n}}{1 - \omega}$$
Since $\omega^{n} = e^{2\pi i} = 1$, the numerator is zero and the denominator is not, so the sum is $0$. (A1)(A1) Geometrically, the $n$ vertices are $n$ equal vectors whose sum is zero: the polygon closes up. Equivalently, the centre of mass of the vertices, treated as unit masses, is at the origin. (A1)

(b) The $n$ numbers $1, \omega, \ldots, \omega^{n-1}$ are precisely the $n$ roots of $z^{n} - 1$, so (M1)
$$z^{n} - 1 = \prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\bigl(z - \omega^{k}\bigr) = (z-1)\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl(z - \omega^{k}\bigr)$$
Dividing by $z - 1$ and using the standard factorisation $z^{n} - 1 = (z-1)(z^{n-1} + \cdots + z + 1)$, valid for all $z$, (M1)
$$z^{n-1} + z^{n-2} + \cdots + 1 = \prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl(z - \omega^{k}\bigr)$$
Both sides are polynomials in $z$, so they are equal for every $z$, including $z = 1$ even though the division by $z-1$ is not valid there. (M1)(A1) Substituting $z = 1$ gives $n$ on the left, from the $n$ terms, and the required product on the right: (A1)(AG)
$$\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl(1 - \omega^{k}\bigr) = n$$

(c) With $\omega^{k} = \cos\frac{2k\pi}{n} + i\sin\frac{2k\pi}{n}$, (M1)
$$\bigl|1 - \omega^{k}\bigr|^{2} = \left(1 - \cos\frac{2k\pi}{n}\right)^{2} + \sin^{2}\frac{2k\pi}{n} = 2 - 2\cos\frac{2k\pi}{n}$$
Using $1 - \cos 2\theta = 2\sin^{2}\theta$ with $\theta = \dfrac{k\pi}{n}$, this is $4\sin^{2}\dfrac{k\pi}{n}$, so $|1 - \omega^{k}| = 2\sin\dfrac{k\pi}{n}$, which is positive for $1 \le k \le n-1$. (A1) Taking the modulus of the result in (b), and noting that the modulus of a product is the product of the moduli, (M1)
$$n = \left|\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl(1 - \omega^{k}\bigr)\right| = \prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\left|1 - \omega^{k}\right| = \prod_{k=1}^{n-1} 2\sin\frac{k\pi}{n} = 2^{\,n-1}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\sin\frac{k\pi}{n}$$
so $\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\sin\frac{k\pi}{n} = \frac{n}{2^{\,n-1}}$. (A1) Since $|1 - \omega^{k}|$ is the distance from the vertex at $1$ to the vertex at $\omega^{k}$, part (b) says that the product of the $n-1$ chord lengths from any one vertex of a regular $n$-gon inscribed in the unit circle is exactly $n$, independent of everything except the number of vertices.

(d) Taking $n = 7$, (M1)
$$\prod_{k=1}^{6}\sin\frac{k\pi}{7} = \frac{7}{2^{6}} = \frac{7}{64}$$ (A1)
Evaluating, $\dfrac{7}{64} = 0.109375$. As a check, $\sin\frac{\pi}{7}\sin\frac{2\pi}{7}\sin\frac{3\pi}{7} = \frac{\sqrt{7}}{8}$ and the remaining three factors are the same three values in reverse order, so the full product is $\dfrac{7}{64}$, which agrees. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) Accept the geometric-series argument or the observation that multiplying the sum by $\omega$ permutes its terms, so $S = \omega S$ and hence $S = 0$; the geometric interpretation must say something about the vertices or the centre of mass and not merely restate the equation. (b) Award (M1) for the factorisation of $z^n - 1$ over its roots and (M1) for cancelling the factor $(z-1)$; the subtle step is the substitution $z = 1$, and a candidate who says the identity is only valid for $z \neq 1$ has missed that both sides are polynomials and should lose one mark. (c) The identity $|1-\omega^k| = 2\sin(k\pi/n)$ must be derived rather than quoted; accept the route through $|1-\omega^k|^2 = (1-\omega^k)(1-\omega^{-k})$ as an alternative. The final remark about the product of chord lengths is a separate mark and should be credited even if the algebraic product is muddled. (d) Accept $7/64$ or $0.109375$; the pairing check is optional but shows the candidate has noticed the symmetry $\sin(k\pi/7) = \sin((7-k)\pi/7)$.

28. 5.16 A substitution that reproduces the integral instead of evaluating it

[15 marks]MATH-AHL5.16-102

Topic 5: Calculus · AHL 5.16 (integration by substitution) and AHL 5.19 (Maclaurin series context) · difficulty 5

(a) With $u = \dfrac{\pi}{2} - x$, $dx = -du$; when $x = 0$, $u = \dfrac{\pi}{2}$, and when $x = \dfrac{\pi}{2}$, $u = 0$ (M1). Also $\sin x = \sin\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2} - u\right) = \cos u$ and $\cos x = \cos\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2} - u\right) = \sin u$ (A1). Hence
$$I = \int_{\pi/2}^{0} \frac{\cos u}{\cos u + \sin u}(-du) = \int_0^{\pi/2} \frac{\cos u}{\cos u + \sin u}\,du \quad (A1),$$
and renaming the dummy variable $u$ as $x$ gives the required result.

(b) Adding the two expressions for $I$ (M1),
$$2I = \int_0^{\pi/2} \frac{\sin x + \cos x}{\sin x + \cos x}\,dx = \int_0^{\pi/2} 1\,dx = \frac{\pi}{2} \quad (A1),$$
so $I = \dfrac{\pi}{4}$ (A1).

(c) Apply the same substitution, $u = \dfrac{\pi}{2} - x$, to $K$ (M1). Since $\sin x$ and $\cos x$ are exchanged, and the expression is symmetric in its two arguments in the sense that the denominator is their sum,
$$K = \int_0^{\pi/2} \frac{f(\cos x)}{f(\cos x) + f(\sin x)}\,dx \quad (A1).$$
Adding the two expressions for $K$ (M1),
$$2K = \int_0^{\pi/2} \frac{f(\sin x) + f(\cos x)}{f(\sin x) + f(\cos x)}\,dx = \int_0^{\pi/2} 1\,dx = \frac{\pi}{2} \quad (A1),$$
the numerator and denominator being identical for every $x$. Hence $K = \dfrac{\pi}{4}$ (A1). The hypotheses matter: positivity of $f$ guarantees that the denominator is never zero, so the integrand is defined throughout the interval, and continuity guarantees that $K$ exists. Nothing about the particular form of $f$ was used, which is why the result holds for every such $f$.

(d)(i) Write $\tan^3 x = \dfrac{\sin^3 x}{\cos^3 x}$, so (M1)
$$\frac{1}{1 + \tan^3 x} = \frac{1}{1 + \frac{\sin^3 x}{\cos^3 x}} = \frac{\cos^3 x}{\cos^3 x + \sin^3 x}.$$
This is the integrand of part (c) with $f(t) = t^3$, which is positive and continuous on $(0, 1)$ (A1), so by part (c) the integral equals $\dfrac{\pi}{4}$ (A1).

(d)(ii) It is less than $\dfrac{\pi}{4}$. For $0 < x < \dfrac{\pi}{2}$ both $\sin x$ and $\cos x$ are positive, so adding $1$ to the denominator strictly decreases the integrand: $\dfrac{\sin x}{\sin x + \cos x + 1} < \dfrac{\sin x}{\sin x + \cos x}$ pointwise on the interval of integration (M1), and therefore the integral is strictly less than $I = \dfrac{\pi}{4}$ (A1). The result of part (c) does not apply here, because the denominator is not the sum of $f(\sin x)$ and $f(\cos x)$ for any $f$ -- the extra term $1$ is not of that form -- so the symmetry argument that gives $\pi/4$ is unavailable. Numerically the integral is approximately $0.4388$, against $\dfrac{\pi}{4} \approx 0.7854$.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the substitution with correctly transformed limits (or for reversing the limits and absorbing the minus sign), (A1) for $\sin x \leftrightarrow \cos u$ and $\cos x \leftrightarrow \sin u$, (A1) for the final integral. Candidates who omit the change of limits but otherwise proceed correctly may earn (M1)(A1) only.
(b) Award (M1) for adding the two integrals -- this is the idea of the question, and no credit is available for attempting the antiderivative. (A1) for the integrand simplifying to 1 and the value $\pi/2$, (A1) for $I = \pi/4$.
(c) Award (M1) for applying the substitution to the general integrand, (A1) for the exchanged form, (M1) for adding, (A1) for the numerator equalling the denominator, (A1) for the conclusion. Credit a correct argument that observes $g(x) + g(\pi/2 - x) = 1$ for the integrand $g$, so that the average value of $g$ over the interval is $1/2$; this is equivalent and should be marked in full. Do not penalise a candidate who fails to restate the role of the hypotheses, but do not award the final (A1) to a candidate who applies the result of part (b) to a particular $f$ and claims a general proof.
(d)(i) Award (M1) for rewriting in terms of sine and cosine, (A1) for identifying $f(t) = t^3$ and checking that it satisfies the hypotheses, (A1) for the value $\pi/4$.
(d)(ii) Award (M1) for a valid comparison of the two integrands, (A1) for the strict inequality and the conclusion 'less than'. A candidate who states 'less than' with the comparison earns both marks; a candidate who states 'equal to $\pi/4$' by misapplying part (c) earns neither.

29. 3.8 The number of solutions of sin 2x = k cos x on 0 <= x <= 2pi as a function of k: the count is four while 0 < |k| < 2, five at k = 0 because the branch sin x = 0 reaches both endpoints, and two once |k| >= 2 because that branch loses its solutions or merges into the other one

[15 marks]MATH-AHL3.8-201

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · AHL 3.8 (further trigonometric equations; the double angle identity sin 2x = 2 sin x cos x) with SL 3.5 (the equation sin x = c and its solutions on a stated interval) · difficulty 5

(a) Using the double angle identity $\sin 2x = 2\sin x\cos x$, the equation becomes
$$2\sin x\cos x = k\cos x \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \cos x\,(2\sin x - k) = 0.$$
This is an identity, not an approximation: the two equations have exactly the same solution set, because subtracting the right side from the left gives $\cos x\,(2\sin x - k)$ identically (M1). The factorisation is what makes the problem finite, since a product is zero exactly when one of its factors is (A1).
Setting $\cos x = 0$ gives $x = \dfrac{\pi}{2}$ and $x = \dfrac{3\pi}{2}$ on $0 \le x \le 2\pi$ (A1). These two values do not depend on $k$ at all, and they are solutions of the original equation for every value of $k$, because when $\cos x = 0$ the left side $\sin 2x = 2\sin x\cos x$ is also zero.
(b) The second branch is $2\sin x = k$, that is $\sin x = k/2$. For $0 < |k| < 2$ the number $k/2$ lies strictly between $-1$ and $1$, so $\sin x = k/2$ has exactly two solutions in one full period (M1). On $0 \le x \le 2\pi$ they are
$$x = \arcsin\!\left(\frac{k}{2}\right) \quad\text{and}\quad x = \pi - \arcsin\!\left(\frac{k}{2}\right).$$
Write $\alpha = \arcsin(k/2)$, so that $0 < |\alpha| < \pi/2$ (A1). If $k > 0$ then $\alpha \in (0, \pi/2)$ and $\pi - \alpha \in (\pi/2, \pi)$; if $k < 0$ then $\alpha \in (-\pi/2, 0)$, which puts the first solution at $2\pi + \alpha \in (3\pi/2, 2\pi)$ and the second at $\pi - \alpha \in (\pi, 3\pi/2)$. In both cases the two values avoid $\pi/2$ and $3\pi/2$ strictly: coincidence would need $\sin(\pi/2) = 1 = k/2$ or $\sin(3\pi/2) = -1 = k/2$, that is $|k| = 2$, which part (b) excludes (A1). So the two branches contribute disjoint sets, and $S(k) = 2 + 2 = 4$ (A1).
(c) At $k = 0$ the second branch reads $\sin x = 0$. On the closed interval $0 \le x \le 2\pi$ this has three solutions, $x = 0$, $x = \pi$ and $x = 2\pi$ (M1), because the equation is satisfied at both endpoints as well as in the interior. Combining with the two roots of $\cos x = 0$ gives
$$S(0) = 5, \qquad x \in \left\{0,\ \frac{\pi}{2},\ \pi,\ \frac{3\pi}{2},\ 2\pi\right\} \;(A1).$$
The reason the count rises is not that the branch gains a root in any general sense, but that the two roots of $\sin x = k/2$ move onto the endpoints of the interval as $k \to 0$: $\alpha \to 0$ and $\pi - \alpha \to \pi$, and the endpoint $2\pi$ is reached from below by $2\pi + \alpha$ when $k$ approaches zero through negative values (A1). Nothing new appears; the same two roots sit on the boundary, where they still count, and the point $x = 2\pi$ now coincides with a root that was previously outside the interval (A1).
(d) At $k = 2$ the branch is $\sin x = 1$, whose only solution is $x = \pi/2$; that value is already one of the roots of $\cos x = 0$, so it adds nothing. At $k = -2$ the branch is $\sin x = -1$, whose only solution is $x = 3\pi/2$, again already counted (A1). Hence $S(2) = S(-2) = 2$ (A1).
For $|k| > 2$ the number $k/2$ lies outside $[-1, 1]$ and $\sin x = k/2$ has no solution at all (A1), so the solution set is exactly $\{\pi/2,\ 3\pi/2\}$ and $S(k) = 2$ (A1). Geometrically the two roots of the branch move towards $\pi/2$ and $3\pi/2$ as $k$ increases to $2$, meet them at $k = 2$, and then leave the interval $[-1,1]$ altogether, which is why the count is unchanged from $k = 2$ onwards.
Collecting the regimes: $S(k) = 5$ at $k = 0$; $S(k) = 4$ for $0 < |k| < 2$; and $S(k) = 2$ for $|k| \ge 2$.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for applying the double angle identity to $\sin 2x$ and factorising, (A1) for $\cos x\,(2\sin x - k) = 0$, (A1) for the pair $\pi/2$ and $3\pi/2$. A candidate who divides both sides by $\cos x$ has destroyed the branch that carries the whole question and cannot score the final (A1); dividing by $\cos x$ is the single most damaging move available here, and it also silently discards the roots $\pi/2$ and $3\pi/2$.
(b) (M1) for solving $\sin x = k/2$ on the stated interval, (A1) for naming the two solutions $\alpha$ and $\pi - \alpha$, (A1) for the argument that they cannot coincide with $\pi/2$ or $3\pi/2$ unless $|k| = 2$, (A1) for $S(k) = 4$. The justification mark requires the candidate to say *why* the four values are distinct, not merely to assert it: an answer that writes the four values and adds four is worth (M1)(A1) and nothing more. Accept any correct argument, including comparing the ranges of $\alpha$ and $\pi - \alpha$ or observing that $\sin(\pi/2) = 1$.
(c) (M1) for solving $\sin x = 0$ and finding three roots on the closed interval, (A1) for $S(0) = 5$ with the solution set listed, (A1) for explaining that the two roots of the branch have moved onto the endpoints, (A1) for identifying $x = 2\pi$ as the value that was outside the interval for every other $k$. A candidate who answers $4$ has counted the roots of $\sin x = 0$ on $0 < x < 2\pi$ instead of on the closed interval; that answer scores the first (M1) only. A candidate who answers $6$ has double-counted $x = 0$ as both a root of $\cos x = 0$ and a root of $\sin x = 0$, which is false, and scores nothing beyond (M1).
(d) (A1) for $S(2) = 2$ with the reason that $\sin x = 1$ gives only $\pi/2$, (A1) for $S(-2) = 2$ with the reason that $\sin x = -1$ gives only $3\pi/2$, (A1) for $S(k) = 2$ when $|k| > 2$ with the reason that $\sin x = k/2$ has no solution, (A1) for the geometric description of the two roots merging and then leaving $[-1,1]$. Condonation: a candidate who writes a single case $|k| \ge 2$ and gives the reason for each of the three sub-cases inside it scores all four marks.
Follow-through: a candidate who mislabels the regime boundaries in (b) and then works consistently in (c) and (d) keeps the method marks in those parts. Forfeits: treating the interval as open and answering $S(0) = 4$; answering $S(k) = 4$ for all $k$ by adding two roots from each factor without checking that they are distinct; and claiming that $\sin x = k/2$ has two solutions for $|k| > 2$.

30. 1.13 The sum of 1/(1 - w^k) over the non-trivial n-th roots of unity: every term has real part exactly one half, the terms pair into conjugates that each sum to one, and the imaginary parts cancel in pairs

[15 marks]MATH-AHL1.13-201

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.13 (modulus-argument form, conjugate roots and the geometry of the complex plane) with AHL 1.12 (roots of unity and de Moivre's theorem) · difficulty 5

(a) Put $w^{k} = e^{i\theta}$ with $\theta = 2\pi k/n$. Then
$$\frac{1}{1 - e^{i\theta}} = \frac{1 - e^{-i\theta}}{(1 - e^{i\theta})(1 - e^{-i\theta})} = \frac{1 - \cos\theta + i\sin\theta}{2 - 2\cos\theta} \;(M1).$$
The denominator used is the squared modulus $|1 - e^{i\theta}|^{2} = 2 - 2\cos\theta$, which is real and non-zero for $1 \le k \le n-1$ (A1). The real part is therefore
$$\frac{1 - \cos\theta}{2 - 2\cos\theta} = \frac{1}{2},$$
because $2 - 2\cos\theta = 2(1 - \cos\theta)$ and the numerator is exactly half of it (A1). This is independent of $k$ and of $n$: every single term of $T_{n}$ has real part $\tfrac{1}{2}$. The imaginary part is $\dfrac{\sin\theta}{2 - 2\cos\theta} = \dfrac{\sin\theta}{4\sin^{2}(\theta/2)} = \dfrac{2\sin(\theta/2)\cos(\theta/2)}{4\sin^{2}(\theta/2)} = \dfrac{1}{2}\cot\dfrac{\theta}{2} = \dfrac{1}{2}\cot\dfrac{\pi k}{n}$.
(b) Let $z$ be of modulus $1$ and $z \ne 1$. Since $\bar{z} = 1/z$ we have $1 - \bar{z} = 1 - 1/z = (z - 1)/z$, so
$$\frac{1}{1 - \bar{z}} = \frac{z}{z - 1} = -\frac{z}{1 - z} \;(M1).$$
Therefore
$$\frac{1}{1 - z} + \frac{1}{1 - \bar{z}} = \frac{1}{1 - z} - \frac{z}{1 - z} = \frac{1 - z}{1 - z} = 1 \;(A1),$$
which is exactly one for every such $z$ and does not depend on $z$ at all (A1). Now the map $k \mapsto n - k$ pairs the integers $1, \ldots, n-1$, and $w^{n-k} = \overline{w^{k}}$ (A1), so each pair $\{k, n-k\}$ contributes exactly $1$ to $T_{n}$.
(c) If $n$ is odd then $n - 1$ is even and the pairing is complete, with $(n-1)/2$ pairs and no term left over, so
$$T_{n} = \frac{n-1}{2} \quad (n \text{ odd}) \;(A1).$$
If $n$ is even, the index $k = n/2$ is paired with itself, because $n - n/2 = n/2$ (M1). At that index $w^{k} = w^{n/2} = e^{i\pi} = -1$, and the term is
$$\frac{1}{1 - (-1)} = \frac{1}{2},$$
which is exactly the real part every term has, so nothing is lost (A1). The remaining $n - 2$ indices form $(n-2)/2$ pairs contributing $1$ each, so
$$T_{n} = \frac{n-2}{2}\cdot 1 + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{n-1}{2} \quad (n \text{ even}) \;(A1).$$
Both cases therefore give $T_{n} = \dfrac{n-1}{2}$.
(d) From part (a), $\operatorname{Im}\dfrac{1}{1 - w^{k}} = \dfrac{1}{2}\cot\dfrac{\pi k}{n}$, and from part (b) the imaginary parts of a conjugate pair cancel, since $\overline{w^{k}} = w^{n-k}$ implies $\operatorname{Im}(1/(1 - w^{n-k})) = -\operatorname{Im}(1/(1 - w^{k}))$ (M1). Reading that cancellation in terms of the cotangents, $\cot\dfrac{\pi(n-k)}{n} = \cot\left(\pi - \dfrac{\pi k}{n}\right) = -\cot\dfrac{\pi k}{n}$, so every cotangent with $1 \le k < n/2$ is cancelled by its partner (A1). When $n$ is even the middle index $k = n/2$ gives $\cot(\pi/2) = 0$, so it contributes nothing (A1). Hence
$$\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\cot\frac{k\pi}{n} = 0 \quad\text{for every } n \ge 2 \;(A1).$$
For $n = 5$ the sum is $\cot(\pi/5) + \cot(2\pi/5) + \cot(3\pi/5) + \cot(4\pi/5)$, and since $\cot(3\pi/5) = -\cot(2\pi/5)$ and $\cot(4\pi/5) = -\cot(\pi/5)$ this is $0$ exactly. As a check, $T_{5} = 5 \cdot \tfrac{1}{2} + 0 \cdot i = \tfrac{5}{2}$, which is $(n-1)/2$ for $n = 5$.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for multiplying numerator and denominator by the conjugate $1 - e^{-i\theta}$, (A1) for identifying the denominator as $2 - 2\cos\theta$ and noting it is non-zero, (A1) for the value $1/2$. The cotangent form is not required for the marks but must be correct if offered: $\tfrac{1}{2}\cot(\pi k/n)$, not $\cot(\pi k/n)$ and not $\tfrac{1}{2}\cot(2\pi k/n)$.
(b) (M1) for using $\bar{z} = 1/z$ to rewrite the second denominator, (A1) for the algebraic cancellation giving $1$, (A1) for recognising that the value does not depend on $z$, (A1) for the statement that $k$ and $n-k$ index conjugate roots. A candidate who proves the identity for a specific $z$ and asserts it in general scores (M1)(A1) only. A candidate who argues geometrically -- that $1 - z$ and $1 - \bar{z}$ are mirror images, so the two reciprocal vectors have equal and opposite imaginary parts and real parts summing to one -- scores full marks, and this is the more illuminating route.
(c) (A1) for $T_{n} = (n-1)/2$ for odd $n$, (M1) for identifying the self-paired index $k = n/2$, (A1) for the value $1/2$ of that term, (A1) for $T_{n} = (n-1)/2$ for even $n$. A candidate who writes $(n-2)/2$ for even $n$ has forgotten the middle term and scores (M1) only in that branch; a candidate who writes $(n-1)/2$ but claims all terms pair into twos has the right answer by a wrong route and scores (A1) for the odd case, (A1) for the even value, and loses the (M1) for the middle term.
(d) (M1) for reading the imaginary parts off part (a), (A1) for the cotangent reflection identity $\cot(\pi - x) = -\cot x$ applied to the pairs, (A1) for noting that the middle cotangent is zero when $n$ is even, (A1) for the value $0$. Accept any correct numerical check such as the $n = 5$ case. A candidate who answers $\pi/2$ or who confuses this sum with $\sum \cot^{2}$ scores nothing in this part.
Follow-through: a candidate whose part (a) cotangent is out by a factor of two but who then argues the cancellation consistently keeps the method marks in (d). Forfeits: attempting to put the $n-1$ fractions over a common denominator; using $|1 - e^{i\theta}| = 1 - \cos\theta$ instead of the squared modulus; and treating $w^{k}$ as if its conjugate were $w^{-k}$ while also claiming $k$ and $n - k$ are different indices when $k = n/2$.

31. 4.10 The regression line of x on y against the rearranged regression line of y on x: two different predictions because the two lines minimise residuals in different directions

[15 marks]MATH-4.10-301

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.10 (equation of the regression line of x on y) with SL 4.4 (linear correlation of bivariate data) · difficulty 5

(a) The slope of the regression line of $y$ on $x$ is
$$b_{yx} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}} = \frac{960}{480} = 2 \;(M1)(A1),$$
and the line passes through $(\bar{x},\,\bar{y}) = (12,\,62)$, so
$$y - 62 = 2(x - 12) \quad\text{that is}\quad y = 2x + 38 \;(A1).$$
(b) The product-moment correlation coefficient is
$$r = \frac{S_{xy}}{\sqrt{S_{xx}S_{yy}}} = \frac{960}{\sqrt{480 \times 3000}} = \frac{960}{\sqrt{1\,440\,000}} = \frac{960}{1200} = 0.80 \;(M1)(A1).$$
This is positive, so higher revision times are associated with higher marks, and at $0.80$ it indicates a strong but by no means perfect linear association: roughly $64\%$ of the variation in marks is accounted for by variation in revision time $\bigl(r^{2} = 0.64\bigr)$ (A1).
(c) The slope of the regression line of $x$ on $y$ is
$$b_{xy} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}} = \frac{960}{3000} = 0.32 \;(M1)(A1),$$
and the line again passes through $(12,\,62)$, so
$$x - 12 = 0.32(y - 62) \quad\text{that is}\quad x = 0.32y - 7.84 \;(A1).$$
(Check: at $y = 62$ this gives $x = 19.84 - 7.84 = 12$, as it must.)
(d) The mean mark is $62$ and $70 > 62$, so this student exceeded the mean (A1). Using the line of $x$ on $y$ from part (c), the predicted revision time is
$$x = 0.32(70) - 7.84 = 22.40 - 7.84 = 14.56 \;\text{hours} \;(M1)(A1).$$
Inverting the line $y = 2x + 38$ instead gives
$$x = \frac{y - 38}{2} = \frac{70 - 38}{2} = \frac{32}{2} = 16.00 \;\text{hours} \;(A1),$$
which exceeds the correct prediction by $16.00 - 14.56 = 1.44$ hours, an overstatement of almost $10\%$ of the value being predicted (A1). Note that both predictions lie inside the observed range of the data, so the difference is not an artefact of extrapolation.
(e) The regression line of $y$ on $x$ is the line $y = a + bx$ that minimises $\sum \bigl(y_{i} - a - bx_{i}\bigr)^{2}$, the sum of squared *vertical* residuals, because it is $y$ that is to be predicted and the error that matters is the error in the predicted $y$ (M1). The regression line of $x$ on $y$ minimises $\sum \bigl(x_{i} - c - dy_{i}\bigr)^{2}$, the sum of squared *horizontal* residuals (A1). Writing one line as $x = (y - a)/b$ evaluates it backwards along a least-squares fit that was never optimised in that direction, so it does not minimise anything meaningful and systematically over-predicts how far from the mean explanatory variable corresponds to a value far from the mean response -- which is why the error grows as the value of $y$ moves away from $\bar{y}$ (A1). Formally, the product of the two slopes is
$$b_{yx}\,b_{xy} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}} \cdot \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}} = \frac{S_{xy}^{2}}{S_{xx}S_{yy}} = r^{2} \;(M1),$$
and here $2 \times 0.32 = 0.64 = 0.80^{2}$, verifying the identity (A1). The two lines both pass through the mean point, so they coincide exactly when their slopes are reciprocals, that is when $r^{2} = 1$, hence when $r = \pm 1$ (A1). Only when every point lies on a single straight line do the two residual directions amount to the same thing.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for correctly identifying $S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ as the slope, (A1) for $b_{yx} = 2$, (A1) for $y = 2x + 38$. Accept the intercept as $38.00$. Condoned: omitting the units on the coefficients. Do not accept a line obtained from $S_{yy}/S_{xy}$, which inverts the roles of the two variables; that mistake is followed through into (c) but forfeits the accuracy mark there as well.
(b) (M1) for the correct formula, (A1) for $r = 0.80$, (A1) for an interpretation that says more than 'they are correlated': it must note the positive direction, or the strength, or quote $r^{2} = 0.64$ as the proportion of variation explained. 'Strong positive correlation' alone earns this third mark only if both adjectives are justified by the numerical value given before it. A candidate who writes $r = 0.8$ without working scores (A1) only. Note that the sign matters: a negative value here is a computational slip, not a valid alternative reading.
(c) (M1) for $S_{xy}/S_{yy}$, (A1) for $0.32$, (A1) for $x = 0.32y - 7.84$. The check that the line passes through $(12,\,62)$ is not required but may be credited in place of the final mark if the intercept is otherwise unaccounted for.
(d) (A1) for noting $70 > 62$, (M1) for substituting into the $x$-on-$y$ line, (A1) for $14.56$ hours, (A1) for the inverted-line value $16.00$ hours, (A1) for the difference $1.44$ hours. Follow-through applies from a wrong line in (a) or (c): a candidate whose two lines are both correct in form but whose arithmetic slips keeps every subsequent mark provided the difference is computed consistently with the two lines they hold. Forfeits: predicting with the $y$-on-$x$ line and treating the answer as a mark rather than a revision time, which is a unit error and loses the final three marks; and quoting only one of the two predictions, which loses the marks for the comparison.
(e) (M1) for naming what the $y$-on-$x$ line minimises, (A1) for naming what the $x$-on-$y$ line minimises, (A1) for the consequence that inverting does not recover the second line and that the error grows away from the mean, (M1) for forming the product $b_{yx}b_{xy}$ from the two slope formulae, (A1) for showing it equals $r^{2}$ here, (A1) for concluding that coincidence requires $r = \pm 1$. The algebraic identity $b_{yx}b_{xy} = r^{2}$ is the cleanest route but any argument reaching $r^{2} = 1$ earns the last two marks; in particular a candidate may argue geometrically that identical lines would have to make both sets of residuals zero, which forces every point onto one line.

32. 1.13 A finite sum of sines obtained by summing a geometric progression of complex numbers and taking imaginary parts, with the integral of k recovered as a limit

[15 marks]MATH-AHL1.13-301

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.13 (modulus-argument (polar) form) with SL 1.3 (geometric sequences and series) · difficulty 5

(a) With $z = e^{i\theta}$, the terms form a geometric progression with first term $z$ and common ratio $z$, so for $z \neq 1$
$$\sum_{k=1}^{n} z^{k} = \frac{z(1 - z^{n})}{1 - z} \;(M1)(A1).$$
Applying $1 - e^{i\phi} = -2i\,e^{i\phi/2}\sin\tfrac{\phi}{2}$ to the numerator with $\phi = n\theta$ and to the denominator with $\phi = \theta$,
$$\frac{1 - z^{n}}{1 - z} = \frac{-2i\,e^{in\theta/2}\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}}{-2i\,e^{i\theta/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}} = e^{i(n-1)\theta/2}\,\frac{\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}}{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}} \;(M1),$$
and multiplying by the leading factor of $z = e^{i\theta}$,
$$\sum_{k=1}^{n} z^{k} = e^{i(n+1)\theta/2}\,\frac{\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}}{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}} \;(A1),$$
as required. Writing the left-hand side as $\sum \cos(k\theta) + i\sum\sin(k\theta)$ and taking imaginary parts of the right-hand side, whose multiplier is the real number $\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}/\sin\frac{\theta}{2}$,
$$S = \sum_{k=1}^{n}\sin(k\theta) = \frac{\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}\,\sin\frac{(n+1)\theta}{2}}{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}} \;(A1).$$
(b) With $n = 100$ and $\theta = \tfrac{\pi}{3}$, we have $\tfrac{n\theta}{2} = \tfrac{50\pi}{3}$ and $\tfrac{(n+1)\theta}{2} = \tfrac{101\pi}{6}$. Now $\tfrac{50\pi}{3} = 16\pi + \tfrac{2\pi}{3}$, so $\sin\tfrac{50\pi}{3} = \sin\tfrac{2\pi}{3} = \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$; and $\tfrac{101\pi}{6} = 16\pi + \tfrac{5\pi}{6}$, so $\sin\tfrac{101\pi}{6} = \sin\tfrac{5\pi}{6} = \tfrac{1}{2}$ (M1). Hence
$$S = \frac{\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}}{\sin\tfrac{\pi}{6}} = \frac{\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{4}}{\tfrac{1}{2}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \;(A1).$$
Verification by periodicity: the values $\sin\tfrac{k\pi}{3}$ repeat with period six, and over one period
$$\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + 0 - \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + 0 = 0 .$$
Since $100 = 16 \times 6 + 4$, only the first four terms of a fresh period survive, namely $\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + 0 - \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ (A1), which agrees.
(c) From the closed form, using $\sin u \sim u$ as $u \to 0$,
$$\frac{1}{\theta}\,S = \frac{1}{\theta}\cdot\frac{\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}\,\sin\frac{(n+1)\theta}{2}}{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}} \;\longrightarrow\; \frac{1}{\theta}\cdot\frac{\tfrac{n\theta}{2}\cdot\tfrac{(n+1)\theta}{2}}{\tfrac{\theta}{2}} = \frac{n(n+1)}{2} \;(M1)(A1),$$
the factors of $\theta$ cancelling exactly. Independently, since $\sin(k\theta) \sim k\theta$ as $\theta \to 0$, the same limit is by definition $\sum_{k=1}^{n} k$, so
$$\sum_{k=1}^{n} k = \frac{n(n+1)}{2} \;(A1),$$
which for $n = 100$ gives $\tfrac{100 \times 101}{2} = 5050$.
(d) Taking real parts in part (a) instead, $C = \Re\bigl(e^{i(n+1)\theta/2}\bigr)\,\frac{\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}}{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}}$ with the real factor carried through unchanged, since only the exponential is complex $(M1)$. Because $\Re(e^{i\alpha}) = \cos\alpha$ with $\alpha = \tfrac{(n+1)\theta}{2}$,
$$C = \sum_{k=1}^{n}\cos(k\theta) = \frac{\sin\frac{n\theta}{2}\,\cos\frac{(n+1)\theta}{2}}{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}} \;(A1).$$
With $n = 100$ and $\theta = \tfrac{\pi}{3}$ we use $\sin\tfrac{50\pi}{3} = \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ as before, together with $\cos\tfrac{101\pi}{6} = \cos\tfrac{5\pi}{6} = -\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \;(A1)$, giving
$$C = \frac{\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\cdot\bigl(-\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\bigr)}{\tfrac{1}{2}} = \frac{-\tfrac{3}{4}}{\tfrac{1}{2}} = -\tfrac{3}{2} \;(A1).$$
(Over one period the cosines sum to $\tfrac{1}{2} - \tfrac{1}{2} - 1 - \tfrac{1}{2} + \tfrac{1}{2} + 1 = 0$, and the four surviving terms give $\tfrac{1}{2} - \tfrac{1}{2} - 1 - \tfrac{1}{2} = -\tfrac{3}{2}$, in agreement.)
(e) Both closed forms divide by $\sin\tfrac{\theta}{2}$, which vanishes exactly when $\tfrac{\theta}{2}$ is an integer multiple of $\pi$, that is when $\theta$ is an integer multiple of $2\pi$ (M1). In that case every term is $\cos(2\pi m k) = 1$ and $\sin(2\pi m k) = 0$, so $C = n$ and $S = 0$; both numerators vanish too, so the forms give the indeterminate $0/0$ rather than the answers (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for quoting or deriving the finite geometric sum, (A1) for the correct expression $\frac{z(1-z^{n})}{1-z}$, (A1) for applying the half-angle identity to both numerator and denominator with the two different values of $\phi$ and deducing the closed form for $S$. Do not accept a candidate who writes $1 - e^{i\phi} = -2ie^{i\phi/2}\sin(\phi/2)$ without deriving or at least quoting its source: the identity is supplied in the stem but using it correctly, with $\phi$ taking the two different values $n\theta$ and $\theta$, is the substantive step. Follow-through applies downstream from any error confined to the exponent of the exponential factor, provided part (b) is answered consistently with it.
(b) (M1) for reducing the two angles modulo $2\pi$ correctly, (A1) for $\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$, (A1) for the independent periodicity check. The check is not optional decoration: a candidate whose closed form is wrong will generally find that the two methods disagree, and the disagreement is the intended diagnostic. Accept either order of working.
(c) (M1) for replacing sines of small arguments by their arguments, (A1) for $\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}$, (A1) for $5050$ when $n = 100$. Accept a candidate who reaches $\sum k = \tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}$ from the second route alone, but only the numerical value earns a mark if the limit has not been connected to either expression. The cancellation of $\theta$ must be exhibited at least once; 'the limit is obviously the sum of the first n integers' without showing the small-angle replacement scores at most one.
(d) (M1) for recognising that the real part is required and identifying the correct right-hand side, (A1) for the closed form, (A1) for evaluating $\sin\tfrac{50\pi}{3}$ and $\cos\tfrac{101\pi}{6}$, (A1) for $-\tfrac{3}{2}$. Note that the only change from part (b) is $\sin \to \cos$ on the second numerator factor; a candidate who carries the sine across unchanged has not taken the real part at all and scores nothing here.
(e) (M1) for identifying the vanishing denominator and its condition, (A1) for $C = n$ and $S = 0$. Condoned: omitting the remark about $0/0$, provided both values are stated. Do not accept 'the formulae give infinity': the numerators vanish simultaneously and the forms are undefined rather than divergent.

33. 3.18 Three planes whose normals are coplanar: no two planes are parallel and yet no point lies on all three, so the configuration is a triangular prism

[15 marks]MATH-3.18-301

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · AHL 3.17 (vector equations of a plane: r = a + λb + μc, and ax + by + cz = d) with AHL 3.18 (intersections of: a line with a plane; two planes; three planes) and AHL 3.16 (the definition of the vector product of two vectors) · difficulty 5

(a) The normal vectors are $\mathbf{n}_{1} = (1,\,1,\,1)$, $\mathbf{n}_{2} = (2,\,-1,\,1)$ and $\mathbf{n}_{3} = (3,\,0,\,2)$, and
$$\mathbf{n}_{1} + \mathbf{n}_{2} = (1 + 2,\; 1 - 1,\; 1 + 1) = (3,\,0,\,2) = \mathbf{n}_{3} \;(M1).$$
The three normals are therefore linearly dependent, so the determinant of the coefficient matrix is zero and the system cannot have a unique solution $(A1)$. That leaves two possibilities only: the planes share a common line, or there is no point lying on all three $(A1)$.\n(b) A direction vector of the line of intersection is $\mathbf{d} = \mathbf{n}_{1} \times \mathbf{n}_{2} = (2,\,1,\,-3)$ $(M1)$, and $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{1} = 2 + 1 - 3 = 0$, $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{2} = 4 - 1 - 3 = 0$, so $\mathbf{d}$ is parallel to both planes as required $(A1)$. Putting $z = 0$ in the first two equations gives $x + y = 4$ and $2x - y = 5$, so $x = 3$, $y = 1$, and the line is
$$\mathbf{r} = \begin{pmatrix}3\\1\\0\end{pmatrix} + t\begin{pmatrix}2\\1\\-3\end{pmatrix}, \qquad t \in \mathbb{R} \;(A1).$$
Substituting this into the left-hand side of $\Pi_{3}$: $3(3 + 2t) + 2(-3t) = 9 + 6t - 6t = 9$, which is constant and never equal to $10$, so the line is parallel to $\Pi_{3}$ $(M1)$ and no point of it lies in $\Pi_{3}$ $(A1)$.\n(c) For $\Pi_{1}$ and $\Pi_{3}$: the point $\left(\tfrac{10}{3},\,\tfrac{2}{3},\,0\right)$ satisfies both, so the line is
$$\mathbf{r} = \begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{10}{3}\\[2pt]\tfrac{2}{3}\\[2pt]0\end{pmatrix} + s\begin{pmatrix}2\\1\\-3\end{pmatrix} \;(M1)(A1).$$
For $\Pi_{2}$ and $\Pi_{3}$: the point $\left(\tfrac{10}{3},\,\tfrac{5}{3},\,0\right)$ satisfies both, so the line is
$$\mathbf{r} = \begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{10}{3}\\[2pt]\tfrac{5}{3}\\[2pt]0\end{pmatrix} + u\begin{pmatrix}2\\1\\-3\end{pmatrix} \;(A1).$$
All three lines have direction $(2,\,1,\,-3)$ and are therefore parallel $(A1)$. They are distinct because the three points $(3,1,0)$, $\left(\tfrac{10}{3},\tfrac{2}{3},0\right)$ and $\left(\tfrac{10}{3},\tfrac{5}{3},0\right)$ all have $z = 0$ and differ in $x$ or $y$, while $(2,1,-3)$ has non-zero $z$-component, so the displacement between any two of the points cannot be a multiple of the common direction.\n(d) Take $\mathbf{v} = \left(\tfrac{10}{3} - 3,\;\tfrac{2}{3} - 1,\;0\right) = \left(\tfrac{1}{3},\,-\tfrac{1}{3},\,0\right)$ $(M1)$. Then
$$\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{d} = \begin{pmatrix}1\\[2pt]1\\[2pt]1\end{pmatrix}, \qquad |\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{d}| = \sqrt{3}, \qquad |\mathbf{d}| = \sqrt{14},$$
so the shortest distance is $\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{14}} = \sqrt{\tfrac{3}{14}} = 0.463$ $(M1)(A1)$. The distance is not zero, so the two parallel lines are distinct and no point lies on all three planes; the number of such points is $0$ $(A1)$. This is visible in the equations too: adding $\Pi_{1}$ and $\Pi_{2}$ gives $3x + 2z = 9$, which contradicts $\Pi_{3}$.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for adding the two normals componentwise, (A1) for the conclusion that the determinant is zero so there is no unique point, (A1) for naming the two remaining possibilities. A candidate who says 'the planes are parallel' here has not read the stem and earns nothing: no two normals are multiples of one another.\n(b) (M1) for a cross product of the two normals, (A1) for $(2,1,-3)$ or any multiple, (A1) for a correct point and a correct vector equation, (M1) for substituting the general point of the line into $\Pi_{3}$ and obtaining the constant $9$, (A1) for concluding that the line is parallel to and outside the plane. Award no mark for 'the point $(3,1,0)$ does not satisfy $\Pi_{3}$' on its own: one point proves nothing until the whole line has been substituted.\n(c) (M1) for using the cross product or an equivalent elimination in each case, (A1) for each of the two lines (a correct point and the correct direction in each), (A1) for observing that all three directions are the same and that the three fixed points are different. Accept any correct parametrisation, including one obtained by setting $x$ or $y$ rather than $z$ to zero, provided the resulting line is verified against both of its planes.\n(d) (M1) for a displacement vector between a point on each line, (M1) for the formula $|\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{d}|/|\mathbf{d}|$, (A1) for $\sqrt{3/14}$ or $0.463$, (A1) for the conclusion that no point lies on all three planes, supported either by the non-zero distance or by the contradiction $3x + 2z = 9$ against $3x + 2z = 10$. Note that $\sqrt{3/14}$ is exact; a decimal answer stated without working scores at most two.

34. 5.9 Two moving objects that are level twice: the second meeting is a repeated root, so the cubic has three roots and the objects meet on two occasions

[15 marks]MATH-5.9-301

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.9 (kinematic problems involving displacement s, velocity v, acceleration a and time t) with SL 5.8 (local maximum and minimum points) and SL 2.7 (solution of quadratic equations) · difficulty 5

(a) $v_{A}(t) = s_{A}'(t) = 3t^{2} - 12t + 9 = 3(t - 1)(t - 3)$ $(M1)$, so $v_{A}(t) = 0$ at $t = 1$ and $t = 3$; these are the two times at which $A$ reverses $(A1)$. $s_{A}(1) = 1 - 6 + 9 = 4$, so at the first reversal $A$ is $4$ m from the origin $(A1)$.\n(b) Let $D(t) = s_{A}(t) - s_{B}(t)$. Being level at $t = \tfrac{5}{2}$ means $D\left(\tfrac{5}{2}\right) = 0$. If the root were simple, $D$ would change sign there, so $A$ would pass $B$ or $B$ would pass $A$; since neither overtakes the other, $D$ keeps the same sign on both sides, which happens exactly when the root is of even multiplicity $(M1)$ — here a repeated (double) root, so also $D'\left(\tfrac{5}{2}\right) = 0$ $(A1)$. Now
$$D(t) = t^{3} - 6t^{2} + 9t - \alpha - \beta t, \qquad D'(t) = 3t^{2} - 12t + 9 - \beta.$$
The two conditions give $D\left(\tfrac{5}{2}\right) = \tfrac{125}{8} - \tfrac{75}{2} + \tfrac{45}{2} - \alpha - \tfrac{5\beta}{2} = \tfrac{5}{8} - \alpha - \tfrac{5\beta}{2} = 0$ and $D'\left(\tfrac{5}{2}\right) = \tfrac{75}{4} - 30 + 9 - \beta = -\tfrac{9}{4} - \beta = 0$, so $\beta = -\tfrac{9}{4} = -2.25$ $(M1)$, and then $\alpha = \tfrac{5}{8} - \tfrac{5}{2}\left(-\tfrac{9}{4}\right) = \tfrac{5}{8} + \tfrac{45}{8} = \tfrac{25}{4} = 6.25$ $(A1)$. Hence $s_{B}(t) = 6.25 - 2.25t$: $B$ starts $6.25$ m from the origin and moves towards it at $2.25$ m s$^{-1}$.\n(c) With these values,
$$D(t) = t^{3} - 6t^{2} + 11.25t - 6.25 = (t - 1)\left(t - \tfrac{5}{2}\right)^{2} \;(M1)(A1),$$
so the equation $D(t) = 0$ has roots $t = 1$ and $t = \tfrac{5}{2}$ (twice). The objects are therefore level on two occasions in $0 \le t \le 5$, not three: the double root is a single instant at which $B$ draws level with $A$ and immediately falls behind again $(A1)$. When $t = 4$, $s_{A}(4) = 64 - 96 + 36 = 4$ and $s_{B}(4) = 6.25 - 9 = -2.75$, so the distance between them is $4 - (-2.75) = 6.75$ m $(A1)$.\n(d) The separation is $|D(t)|$ with $D(t) = (t - 1)\left(t - \tfrac{5}{2}\right)^2$ $(M1)$. $D'(t) = 3t^{2} - 12t + 11.25 = 3(t - 1.5)(t - 2.5)$, so the only stationary points in the interval are $t = 1.5$ and $t = 2.5$ $(A1)$. Evaluating, $D(0) = -6.25$, $D(1.5) = 0.5$, $D(2.5) = 0$ and $D(5) = 125 - 150 + 56.25 - 6.25 = 25$, so $|D|$ attains its greatest value at the endpoint $t = 5$ $(M1)$, where the separation is $25$ m $(A1)$.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for differentiating, (A1) for $t = 1$ and $t = 3$, (A1) for $4$ m. Accept the factorised velocity $3(t-1)(t-3)$ as the justification for the reversal times.\n(b) (M1) for explaining that 'no overtaking' forces the difference not to change sign, and hence a repeated root, (A1) for the condition $D'(5/2) = 0$ as well as $D(5/2) = 0$, (M1) for solving the two equations, (A1) for $\beta = -2.25$ and $\alpha = 6.25$. A candidate who uses only $D(5/2) = 0$ and the earlier observation $D(1) = 0$ obtains the same two equations by a different route, and must be credited fully for the algebra; but such a candidate earns the first mark only on confirming that $t = 5/2$ is in fact a double root of the difference they have found, since otherwise they have not shown that their motion is consistent with the observation they were given.\n(c) (M1) for forming $D$ and factorising, (A1) for the factorisation, (A1) for the answer two occasions with the reason that the double root is one instant, (A1) for $6.75$ m. Do not credit 'three times' however carefully the cubic is solved; the question asks for occasions, and this is the item's point.\n(d) (M1) for writing the separation as $|D(t)|$, (A1) for $D'(t) = 3(t-1.5)(t-2.5)$, (M1) for checking the endpoints as well as the stationary points, (A1) for $25$ m at $t = 5$. A candidate who maximises $D$ without the modulus and reports $25$ m by luck should be asked for the endpoint check; award the final mark only if $t = 0$ was considered.

35. An induction that only closes once the hypothesis is strengthened

[15 marks]MATH-AHL1.15-001

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.15 (proof by mathematical induction) · difficulty 5

(a) For $k\ge1$,
$$\frac{1}{k}-\frac{1}{k+1}=\frac{(k+1)-k}{k(k+1)}=\frac{1}{k(k+1)}.$$ (M1) Now $(k+1)^{2}+1=k^{2}+2k+2$ and $k(k+1)=k^{2}+k$, so $$(k+1)^{2}+1-k(k+1)=k+2>0,$$ hence $(k+1)^{2}+1>k(k+1)$ and therefore $$\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}<\frac{1}{k(k+1)}=\frac1k-\frac1{k+1}.$$ (A1)(A1) *(AG)*

(b) With the hypothesis $S_k<\frac32$ the step gives $$S_{k+1}=S_k+\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}<\frac32+\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}.$$ (M1)(A1) The right-hand side is strictly greater than $\frac32$ because $\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}>0$, so this does not establish $S_{k+1}<\frac32$. (R1) No rearrangement of a hypothesis of the form $S_k<\frac32$ can remove a positive increment, so the hypothesis has to be strengthened to one that leaves a gap larger than the increment. (A1)

(c) Claim: $S_n\le\frac32-\frac1n$ for all $n\ge1$. Base case $n=1$: $S_1=\frac12$ and $\frac32-1=\frac12$, so the claim holds, with equality. (M1) Inductive step: assume $S_k\le\frac32-\frac1k$. Then $$S_{k+1}=S_k+\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}\le\frac32-\frac1k+\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}.$$ (M1) By part (a), $\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}\le\frac1k-\frac1{k+1}$, so $$S_{k+1}\le\frac32-\frac1k+\frac1k-\frac1{k+1}=\frac32-\frac1{k+1}.$$ (A1) This is the claim with $n=k+1$, so by induction the claim holds for every $n\ge1$. (A1) *(AG)* Finally $\frac1n>0$, so $S_n\le\frac32-\frac1n<\frac32$ for every $n\ge1$. (A1)

(d) We want the largest $c$ with $S_n\le\frac32-\frac{c}{n}$ for all $n\ge1$. (M1) The case $n=1$ reads $\frac12\le\frac32-c$, that is $c\le1$, so no $c>1$ is admissible; part (c) shows that $c=1$ is admissible. (A1) Therefore the greatest such constant is $c=1$. (A1) For example, $c=\frac{3}{2}$ would demand $S_1\le0$, which is false because $S_1=\frac12$, so values above $1$ genuinely fail and the answer is not merely the largest value tested.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for combining the right-hand side into the single fraction $\frac{1}{k(k+1)}$; the two (A1) marks are for the comparison $(k+1)^{2}+1>k(k+1)$ and for concluding the inequality. A candidate who checks several values of $k$ without proving the general comparison earns at most (M1): the point is the identity $(k+1)^{2}+1-k(k+1)=k+2>0$. Accept the equivalent form $\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}\le\frac{1}{k(k+1)}$ with no further work required.

(b) The step must be written out with the positive increment visible. Award (M1) for the decomposition $S_{k+1}=S_k+\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2}+1}$, (A1) for substituting the hypothesis, (R1) for observing that the resulting bound exceeds $\frac32$, and the final (A1) for the structural remark that a strictly positive increment cannot be absorbed by a bound with no slack. A candidate who writes only 'it does not work' with no inequality earns just the first two marks.

(c) The strengthened statement must be stated, not assumed. The base case is checked with equality, which is acceptable. Award (M1) for the base case, (M1) for the inductive hypothesis and the decomposition, (A1) for applying the inequality from (a), (A1) for the algebra reaching $\frac32-\frac1{k+1}$, and (A1) for the concluding deduction $S_n<\frac32$. Follow-through is allowed: a candidate who carries an incorrect constant from (a) but applies it consistently keeps the method marks.

(d) The base case gives $c\le1$ and part (c) gives admissibility at $c=1$. A candidate who simply states $c=1$ without the inequality $\frac12\le\frac32-c$ loses the (M1). Condone a brief note that smaller values of $c$ give a weaker, hence also true, inequality; the question asks for the greatest constant.

36. The cubic whose odd symmetry is hidden by a translation

[15 marks]MATH-AHL2.12-001

Topic 2: Functions · AHL 2.12 (polynomial functions, their graphs and equations), AHL 2.14 (odd and even functions) · difficulty 5

(a) Put $g(t)=f(2+t)-2$. Then $$g(t)+g(-t)=\bigl(f(2+t)-2\bigr)+\bigl(f(2-t)-2\bigr)=4-4=0,$$ (M1) using the given condition with the same $t$. Hence $g(-t)=-g(t)$ for all real $t$, so $g$ is odd. (A1)

Since $f$ is a cubic, $g$ is a cubic in $t$. An odd polynomial contains only odd powers of $t$, so $g(t)=at^{3}+bt$ for constants $a$ and $b$. (R1) Writing $t=x-2$ gives $f(x)-2=a(x-2)^{3}+b(x-2)$, that is $$f(x)=2+a(x-2)^{3}+b(x-2).$$ (A1) *(AG)*

(b) The leading coefficient of $f$ is $1$, so $a=1$. (A1) With $y=x-2$, the equation $f(x)=0$ becomes $$y^{3}+by+2=0.$$ (M1) A cubic $y^{3}+py+q$ has a repeated root exactly when its discriminant $-4p^{3}-27q^{2}$ is zero. (M1) Here $p=b$ and $q=2$, so $-4b^{3}-108=0$, giving $b^{3}=-27$ and hence $b=-3$. (A1) Therefore $$f(x)=(x-2)^{3}-3(x-2)+2.$$ (A1) *(AG)*

(c) Expanding, $(x-2)^{3}=x^{3}-6x^{2}+12x-8$ and $-3(x-2)=-3x+6$, so $$f(x)=x^{3}-6x^{2}+9x=x\left(x^{2}-6x+9\right)=x(x-3)^{2}.$$ (M1)(A1) The roots are $x=0$ and the repeated root $x=3$. (A1) The point of inflection is the centre of symmetry $(2,2)$, since $f(2)=2$; equivalently $f''(x)=6x-12$ vanishes at $x=2$. (A1) As a check on the symmetry, the half-turn about $(2,2)$ carries the point $(0,0)$ to $(4,4)$, and indeed $f(4)=4$.

(d) Differentiating, $f'(x)=3x^{2}-12x+9=3(x-1)(x-3)$. (M1) Hence $f$ has a local maximum at $x=1$ with $f(1)=4$ and a local minimum at $x=3$ with $f(3)=0$. (A1) These are reflections of each other in the point of symmetry $(2,2)$, since $2\cdot2-1=3$ and $2\cdot2-4=0$. (A1) Reading the graph: for $k<0$ there is one solution; for $k=0$ there are two distinct solutions, $x=0$ and the repeated root $x=3$; for $0<k<4$ there are three solutions; for $k=4$ there are two distinct solutions; and for $k>4$ there is one solution. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the translation $g(t)=f(2+t)-2$ and the addition of the two shifted equations; the (A1) is for recognising that $g(t)+g(-t)=0$ is precisely the definition of an odd function. The (R1) is for the statement that an odd polynomial has only odd powers, which is what converts oddness into the shape of $f$. A candidate who writes $f(x)=2+a(x-2)^3+b(x-2)^2+c(x-2)$ without eliminating the even powers has not used oddness and forfeits the (R1). Accept $b$ in place of the linear coefficient; the names are not important.

(b) The (A1) for $a=1$ follows from the stated leading coefficient. The repeated-root condition must be converted into a condition on $b$: award (M1) for the substitution $y=x-2$ and (M1) for invoking the discriminant, then (A1) for $b=-3$ and (A1) for the assembled cubic. A candidate who instead sets $f(x)=(x-r)^{2}(x-s)$ and matches coefficients should be fully credited, since it reaches the same unique answer. Condone an unsimplified form of $f$.

(c) Accept any correct factorisation route, including division by $x$ after noticing the zero constant term. The point of inflection may be found from the symmetry given in the stem or from $f''(x)=0$; either is acceptable.

(d) The (M1) is for locating the turning points, the (A1) for their values, and the (A1) for linking them by the central symmetry. The final (A1) requires all five cases, including the degenerate values $k=0$ and $k=4$ where a double root reduces the number of distinct solutions; a candidate who gives three solutions at $k=0$ or $k=4$ loses the final mark.

37. A compound-angle equation whose squared version invents solutions

[15 marks]MATH-AHL3.10-001

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · AHL 3.10 (compound angle identities), AHL 3.11 (relationships between trigonometric functions) · difficulty 5

(a) By the compound-angle formula, $$\sqrt{2}\cos\left(x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\sqrt2\left(\cos x\cos\frac{\pi}{4}-\sin x\sin\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\sqrt2\cdot\frac{\sqrt2}{2}(\cos x-\sin x)=\cos x-\sin x.$$ (M1)(A1) Also, by the double-angle formula and the difference of two squares, $$\cos 2x=\cos^{2}x-\sin^{2}x=(\cos x-\sin x)(\cos x+\sin x).$$ (A1) *(AG)*

(b) Substituting the results of (a), $$(\cos x-\sin x)(\cos x+\sin x)=\cos x-\sin x,$$ so $$(\cos x-\sin x)\bigl(\cos x+\sin x-1\bigr)=0.$$ (M1)

Case 1: $\cos x=\sin x$, that is $\tan x=1$, giving $x=\frac{\pi}{4}$ or $x=\frac{5\pi}{4}$ in $[0,2\pi)$. (A1)

Case 2: $\cos x+\sin x=1$. Writing the left side as $\sqrt2\cos\left(x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$ gives $\cos\left(x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt2}$, so $x-\frac{\pi}{4}=\pm\frac{\pi}{4}+2k\pi$. (M1) Hence $x=0$ or $x=\frac{\pi}{2}$ in $[0,2\pi)$. (A1)

The solutions are $x=0$, $x=\frac{\pi}{4}$, $x=\frac{\pi}{2}$ and $x=\frac{5\pi}{4}$.

(c) Squaring both sides gives $\cos^{2}2x=2\cos^{2}\left(x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$. (M1) Using $\cos^{2}2x=1-\sin^{2}2x$ and $2\cos^{2}\theta=1+\cos2\theta$ with $\theta=x+\frac{\pi}{4}$, the right-hand side is $$1+\cos\left(2x+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=1-\sin2x.$$ (A1) Hence $1-\sin^{2}2x=1-\sin2x$, that is $\sin^{2}2x=\sin2x$, or $$\sin2x\,(\sin2x-1)=0.$$ (A1)

The squared equation therefore admits $x=0,\frac{\pi}{2},\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}$ from $\sin2x=0$ and $x=\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{5\pi}{4}$ from $\sin2x=1$. (A1) Of these, $x=\pi$ and $x=\frac{3\pi}{2}$ do not solve the original equation: at $x=\pi$ the left side is $1$ and the right side is $-1$, while at $x=\frac{3\pi}{2}$ the left side is $-1$ and the right side is $1$. They are extraneous and must be rejected.

(d) From (b), $\cos x=\sin x$ gives $x=\frac{\pi}{4}+k\pi$ for $k\in\mathbb{Z}$. (M1) Also $\cos x+\sin x=1$ gives $x=2k\pi$ or $x=\frac{\pi}{2}+2k\pi$ for $k\in\mathbb{Z}$. (A1)

In $[-2\pi,2\pi]$ the family $\frac{\pi}{4}+k\pi$ contributes $k=-2,-1,0,1$, that is $-\frac{7\pi}{4},-\frac{3\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{5\pi}{4}$, four values; the family $2k\pi$ contributes $-2\pi,0,2\pi$, three values; and the family $\frac{\pi}{2}+2k\pi$ contributes $-\frac{3\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}$, two values. (M1) These nine values are all distinct, so there are $9$ solutions. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the compound-angle expansion and the (A1) for evaluating $\cos\frac{\pi}{4}=\sin\frac{\pi}{4}=\frac{\sqrt2}{2}$; the second (A1) is for the double-angle factorisation. A candidate who quotes $\cos2x=1-2\sin^{2}x$ instead of the factorised form has not produced the factor needed in (b), so the second (A1) should not be given unless the factorisation is also shown.

(b) The (M1) is for setting the two expressions equal and factorising. A candidate who divides by $(\cos x-\sin x)$ and so loses $x=\frac{\pi}{4}$ and $x=\frac{5\pi}{4}$ forfeits the first (A1). The second case needs the compound-angle rewrite $\cos x+\sin x=\sqrt2\cos\left(x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$; award (M1) for this and (A1) for the two roots. Accept any order of the four solutions.

(c) All four marks depend on the algebra being shown. The (A1) for $2\cos^{2}\left(x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=1-\sin2x$ requires the double-angle identity applied with the correct angle; a common slip is to write $\cos\left(2x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$ instead of $\cos\left(2x+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)$. The final (A1) requires the two extraneous values to be named and tested against the original equation; merely saying that squaring can introduce roots earns nothing.

(d) The general solutions must carry the $k\in\mathbb{Z}$ parameter; accept $\tan x=1$ written as $x=\frac{\pi}{4}+k\pi$. The count of $9$ is earned only if the three families are enumerated over the closed interval. A candidate who takes the four solutions of (b) and doubles them, or who omits the endpoints $x=-2\pi$ and $x=2\pi$, loses the final (A1).

38. The false root invented by multiplying through a vanishing denominator

[15 marks]MATH-AHL3.9-001

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · AHL 3.9 (definition of the reciprocal trigonometric ratios sec, csc, cot) · difficulty 5

(a) $f$ is undefined where $\sin x=0$, because $\csc x=\frac{1}{\sin x}$ and $\cot x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}$ both require $\sin x\ne0$. (A1) In $[0,2\pi)$ these are $x=0$ and $x=\pi$. (A1) Both reciprocal functions have period $2\pi$, so $f$ has period $2\pi$. (A1)

(b) Putting the two terms over the common denominator, $$\csc x-\cot x=\frac{1}{\sin x}-\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}=\frac{1-\cos x}{\sin x}.$$ (M1) The half-angle identity $\tan\frac{x}{2}=\frac{1-\cos x}{\sin x}$ holds for every $x$ with $\sin x\ne0$. (M1) Hence $f(x)=\tan\frac{x}{2}$ on the whole domain of $f$. (A1) *(AG)*

(c) Multiplying $f(x)=1$ by $\sin x$ gives $1-\cos x=\sin x$, that is $$\sin x+\cos x=1.$$ (M1)(A1) Writing the left side as $\sqrt2\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$ gives $\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt2}$, so $x+\frac{\pi}{4}=\frac{\pi}{4}$ or $\frac{3\pi}{4}$ in the first period. (M1) The solutions in $[0,2\pi)$ are $x=0$ and $x=\frac{\pi}{2}$. (A1) At $x=0$, however, $\sin x=0$, so $f$ is undefined and the multiplication by zero has created a false root; only $x=\frac{\pi}{2}$ solves the original equation. (R1)(A1)

(d) On the domain $f(x)=\tan\frac{x}{2}$ with $\frac{x}{2}\in\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\cup\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi\right)$. (M1) On $\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ the tangent rises from $0$ to $+\infty$, so each $k>0$ is attained exactly once and no $k<0$ is attained. (A1) On $\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi\right)$ it rises from $-\infty$ to $0$, so each $k<0$ is attained exactly once and no $k>0$ is attained. (A1) For $k=0$, $\tan\frac{x}{2}=0$ would require $x=0$ or $x=2\pi$, both excluded, so there are no solutions. (R1) Hence $f(x)=k$ has exactly one solution for every $k\ne0$ and none for $k=0$. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) Both (A1) marks are for the two excluded values; accept $0$ and $\pi$, or $n\pi$ with $n=0,1$, within the interval. The third (A1) is for the period $2\pi$; a candidate who says $\pi$ has confused the period of $\tan\frac{x}{2}$ with that of $\tan x$.

(b) The first (M1) is for combining the reciprocals over the single denominator $\sin x$, the second (M1) for the half-angle form. A candidate who uses $\tan\frac{x}{2}=\frac{\sin x}{1+\cos x}$ should be fully credited, since the two forms agree on the domain. The (A1) requires the identity to be asserted on the domain, not at the excluded points.

(c) The (M1)(A1) are for the multiplication and the resulting linear equation, the next (M1) for solving it, and the (A1) for the two roots in the interval. The (R1) is for testing $x=0$ against the domain and naming it extraneous; merely listing $x=0$ earns nothing. The final (A1) requires the reduced solution set.

(d) The (M1) is for rewriting the equation as $\tan\frac{x}{2}=k$ on the two branches. Each branch contributes one (A1); the (R1) is for treating $k=0$ separately, since the tangent vanishes only at the excluded endpoints.

39. A join where continuity and differentiability impose independent relations

[15 marks]MATH-AHL5.12-001

Topic 5: Calculus · AHL 5.12 (informal understanding of continuity and differentiability), AHL 5.15 (derivatives of tan x, sec x, csc x, cot x, a^x, log_a x) · difficulty 5

(a) At $x=1$ the left limit is $a^{1}+\log_a1+p=a+p$, while the right-hand value is $q\tan0+r=r$. (M1)(A1) These agree exactly when $a+p=r$. (AG) At $x=1+\frac{\pi}{4}$ the left limit is $q\tan\frac{\pi}{4}+r=q+r$ and the right-hand value is $s\csc\frac{\pi}{4}+t=s\sqrt2+t$; these agree exactly when $q+r=s\sqrt2+t$. (M1)(A1) *(AG)*

(b) The first branch has derivative $a^{x}\ln a+\frac{1}{x\ln a}$, equal at $x=1$ to $a\ln a+\frac{1}{\ln a}$; the second has derivative $q\sec^{2}(x-1)$, equal at $x=1$ to $q$. (M1)(A1) Differentiability at $x=1$ needs continuity and equality of these one-sided derivatives, giving $a+p=r$ and $q=a\ln a+\frac{1}{\ln a}$. (A1) At $x=1+\frac{\pi}{4}$ the second branch has derivative $q\sec^{2}\frac{\pi}{4}=2q$, and the third has derivative $-s\csc(x-1)\cot(x-1)$, equal there to $-s\sqrt2$. (M1) So differentiability at that join requires $q+r=s\sqrt2+t$ together with $2q=-s\sqrt2$, that is $s=-\sqrt2\,q$. (A1) *(AG)*

(c) Continuity at $x=1$ is the single condition $a+p=r$; differentiability adds the independent condition $q=a\ln a+\frac{1}{\ln a}$. (M1) Hence $f$ is continuous but not differentiable at $x=1$ precisely when $a+p=r$ and $q\ne a\ln a+\frac{1}{\ln a}$, for any admissible $a$. (A1) The reverse cannot occur: a function differentiable at a point must be continuous there, since a differentiable function has a tangent line and cannot break. (R1)(A1)

(d) With $a=e$ we have $\ln a=1$, so $q=e+1$, then $s=-\sqrt2(e+1)$ and $t=q+r-s\sqrt2=3(e+1)$. (M1) On the third branch $f'(x)=-s\csc(x-1)\cot(x-1)=\sqrt2(e+1)\csc(x-1)\cot(x-1)$, which vanishes when $\cot(x-1)=0$, that is at $x=1+\frac{\pi}{2}$. (A1) There $f=s\csc\frac{\pi}{2}+t=s+t=(3-\sqrt2)(e+1)$, so the stationary point is $\left(1+\frac{\pi}{2},\,(3-\sqrt2)(e+1)\right)$. Since $f'$ is positive just left of $x=1+\frac{\pi}{2}$ and negative just right, the point is a local maximum. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for evaluating each one-sided limit and the (A1) for the resulting relation; accept $\log_a1=0$ used without comment. A candidate who checks continuity by substituting $x=1$ into one branch only, without comparing the branches, earns at most the (M1).

(b) The derivative marks depend on the correct rules: $\frac{d}{dx}a^{x}=a^{x}\ln a$, $\frac{d}{dx}\log_a x=\frac{1}{x\ln a}$, $\frac{d}{dx}\tan u=\sec^{2}u$ and $\frac{d}{dx}\csc u=-\csc u\cot u$, with $u=x-1$. Award (A1) for $a\ln a+\frac{1}{\ln a}$ and for $-s\sqrt2$. Condone writing $\frac{1}{\ln a}$ as $\log_a e$. Differentiability must be stated to require continuity as well; a candidate who equates the derivatives but omits $a+p=r$ loses the final (A1).

(c) The (M1) is for separating the continuity relation from the differentiability relation; the answer must be given as a family, not one choice. The (R1) is for the implication differentiable implies continuous, and the final (A1) for completing the argument. Accept an equivalent remark that the differentiability condition is strictly stronger.

(d) The (M1) is for substituting $a=e$ and propagating $q$, $s$ and $t$; follow-through is allowed from values carried consistently from (b). The (A1) needs both coordinates, and the second (A1) needs the sign argument, not merely the assertion that the point is a maximum.

40. The descent that only works because the modulus is three

[15 marks]MATH-AHL1.6-001

Topic 1: Number and algebra · SL 1.6 (simple deductive proof, numerical and algebraic; proof by contradiction and by counterexample) · difficulty 5

(a) Every integer is of the form $3k$, $3k+1$ or $3k+2$. (M1) Squaring these gives $9k^{2}$, $9k^{2} + 6k + 1$ and $9k^{2} + 12k + 4$. (M1) The first is divisible by $3$, while the second and third leave remainder $1$, so every square leaves remainder $0$ or $1$. (A1)

(b) By part (a), $a^{2}$ and $b^{2}$ each leave remainder $0$ or $1$ modulo $3$, so $a^{2} + b^{2}$ leaves remainder $0$, $1$ or $2$. (M1) The right-hand side $3c^{2}$ is divisible by $3$, so the left-hand side must leave remainder $0$. (M1) Remainder $0$ forces $0 + 0$, so $a^{2}$ and $b^{2}$ are both divisible by $3$. (A1) A square divisible by $3$ forces its root to be divisible by $3$, so $3 \mid a$ and $3 \mid b$. (A1)

(c) From part (b), write $a = 3a_1$ and $b = 3b_1$. (M1) Substituting gives $9a_1^{2} + 9b_1^{2} = 3c^{2}$, so $3(a_1^{2} + b_1^{2}) = c^{2}$. (M1) Therefore $3 \mid c^{2}$ and hence $3 \mid c$. (A1) Writing $c = 3c_1$ and dividing by $3$ gives $a_1^{2} + b_1^{2} = 3c_1^{2}$. (M1) So $(a_1, b_1, c_1)$ is another positive integer solution with $a_1 < a$. (A1) Repeating without end produces a strictly decreasing sequence of positive integers, which is impossible, so no such triple exists. (R1)

(d) Modulo $2$, every square leaves remainder $0$ or $1$. (M1) Both $0 + 0$ and $1 + 1$ leave remainder $0$ modulo $2$, so the congruence does not force $a$ and $b$ even. (A1) Indeed $a = b = c = 1$ gives $1 + 1 = 2 = 2 \times 1$, a genuine solution. (R1)

Markscheme notes

Part (a): the two (M1) marks are for the residue classes and for squaring them, and the (A1) for the conclusion. A candidate may tabulate the three cases instead of using algebra. Part (b): the first (M1) is for listing the possible remainders of $a^{2} + b^{2}$ and the second for matching the remainder $0$ forced by the right-hand side. The first (A1) is for concluding $3 \mid a$ and $3 \mid b$ from the remainders, and the second (A1) is for the step that a square divisible by $3$ forces its root to be divisible by $3$; this must be justified, not merely asserted. Part (c): the (M1) marks are for the substitutions $a = 3a_1$ and $b = 3b_1$ and for reaching $c^{2} = 3(a_1^{2} + b_1^{2})$. The (A1) is for $3 \mid c$. The final (M1)(A1) are for producing the smaller solution and identifying it as smaller, and the (R1) is for naming the impossibility of an infinite strictly decreasing sequence of positive integers. Part (d): the (M1) is for the two residue classes modulo $2$, the (A1) for the observation that $1 + 1$ is congruent to $0$, and the (R1) for an explicit counterexample such as $a = b = c = 1$.

41. P3 extended problem: exponential Diophantine equation

[15 marks]MATH-P3-007

Topic 1: Number and algebra · SL 1.6 (simple deductive proof), AHL 1.15 (proof by mathematical induction), SL 1.5 (laws of exponents) · difficulty 5

(a) If $m=1$ then $2^{1}+1 = 3 = 3^{1}$, giving $(m,n) = (1,1)$. (A1)
If $m=2$ then $2^{2}+1 = 5$, which is not a power of $3$, so there is no solution with $m=2$. (A1)
So far: $(m,n) = (1,1)$. (A1)

(b) Assume $m\ge3$. Then $2^{m}$ is divisible by $8$, so $2^{m}\equiv 0 \pmod 8$ and the equation gives (M1)
$$3^{n} = 2^{m}+1 \equiv 1 \pmod 8.$$
Now compute powers of $3$ modulo $8$: (M1)
$$3^{1}\equiv 3, \quad 3^{2} = 9 \equiv 1, \quad 3^{3} \equiv 3, \quad 3^{4}\equiv 1 \pmod 8.$$
Since $3^{2}\equiv1$, the pattern has period $2$: $3^{n}\equiv 1 \pmod 8$ when $n$ is even and $3^{n}\equiv 3 \pmod 8$ when $n$ is odd. (A1)(A1)
Therefore $n$ must be even. (A1)

(c) Write $n = 2k$ with $k$ a positive integer. Then (M1)
$$2^{m} = 3^{2k} - 1 = \bigl(3^{k}-1\bigr)\bigl(3^{k}+1\bigr).$$
Both factors are positive integers whose product is a power of $2$, so each factor is itself a power of $2$: (M1)(A1)
$$3^{k}-1 = 2^{a}, \qquad 3^{k}+1 = 2^{b}, \qquad b > a \ge 1.$$
Subtracting, (M1)
$$2 = 2^{b} - 2^{a} = 2^{a}\bigl(2^{\,b-a} - 1\bigr).$$
The second factor is odd, so it must divide $2$; being odd and positive it equals $1$, giving $2^{b-a}-1 = 1$, so $b-a = 1$. Then $2^{a}\cdot 1 = 2$, so $a = 1$ and $b = 2$. (A1)
Hence $3^{k}-1 = 2$, so $3^{k} = 3$ and $k = 1$. Then $n = 2$ and $2^{m} = 3^{2}-1 = 8$, so $m = 3$. (A1)

Checking: $2^{3}+1 = 9 = 3^{2}$. Therefore the complete set of solutions in positive integers is (A1)
$$\boxed{(m,\,n) = (1,\,1)\ \text{ and }\ (m,\,n) = (3,\,2).}$$

Markscheme notes

(a) Award the marks for the two substitutions; the conclusion mark is for listing $(1,1)$ only. (b) The (M1)s are for reducing the equation modulo $8$ and for computing the cycle of powers of $3$ modulo $8$. A candidate who uses modulo $4$ instead will find that $3^n\equiv1\pmod4$ also forces $n$ even when $m\ge2$, which is valid and should be credited in full; modulo $3$ gives nothing useful and earns nothing. Award the final (A1) only if the parity conclusion is stated. (c) The difference-of-two-squares step is essential and earns (M1). The inference that each factor is a power of $2$ needs justification: it follows because the only divisors of a power of two are powers of two.
