Do not penalise a candidate who writes the line as $y = 2.50x - 1.47$ using rounded coefficients: the three-significant-figure instruction applies here.
(c) (M1) for the nine-point sums, (A1) for the new line, (A1) for the new $r$, (A1) for both predictions and their difference, (A1) for the comment. The comment must engage with the fact that $r$ rose while the prediction moved a long way; a candidate who writes only that 'the outlier affected the line' scores no mark for the comment, because the point of the part is that the two diagnostics disagree. Accept any equivalent wording of the extrapolation caution, and accept a candidate who argues that the tenth point may be genuine, provided the arithmetic is used to support the judgement.
Follow-through applies from (b) to (c): a candidate whose gradient in (b) is wrong still earns the marks in (c) for a correct method applied consistently.

58. 2.2 Intersections of the graphs of a decreasing function and its inverse: only one of the three points lies on y = x

[6 marks]MATH-2.2-401

Topic 2: Functions · SL 2.2 Concept of a function, domain, range and graph; SL 2.3 The graph of a function and its equation y = f(x); inverse functions and the reflection of a graph in the line y = x · difficulty 5

(a) The function is decreasing and maps $[0,\,6]$ onto $[0,\,6]$, so it takes its greatest value at the left end of the domain and its least at the right: $f(0) = 6$ and $f(6) = 0$ (M1). Hence $(0,\,6)$ and $(6,\,0)$ both lie on the graph of $y = f(x)$. The graph of $y = f^{-1}(x)$ is the reflection of that graph in $y = x$, and the reflection of $(0,\,6)$ is $(6,\,0)$; so $(6,\,0)$ lies on the graph of the inverse, and it also lies on the graph of $f$. Equivalently $f^{-1}(0) = 6$ and $f^{-1}(6) = 0$. Both points are therefore intersections, and neither lies on $y = x$ (A1).

(b) A fixed point of $f$ is a point of intersection on $y = x$, so solve $f(x) = x$ on each segment. On $0 \leq x \leq 2$, $f(x) = 6 - \tfrac{3}{2}x$, giving $6 - \tfrac{3}{2}x = x$, so $x = 2.4$, which is not in $[0,\,2]$. On $2 \leq x \leq 4$, $f(x) = 4 - \tfrac{1}{2}x$, giving $4 - \tfrac{1}{2}x = x$, so $x = \tfrac{8}{3}$ (M1), which does lie in $[2,\,4]$. On $4 \leq x \leq 6$, $f(x) = 6 - x$, giving $x = 3$, which is not in $[4,\,6]$. The only fixed point is therefore $\left(\tfrac{8}{3},\,\tfrac{8}{3}\right)$ (A1).

(c) Reflection in $y = x$ exchanges the two graphs: it maps the graph of $f$ onto the graph of $f^{-1}$ and the graph of $f^{-1}$ onto the graph of $f$ (M1). The boundary of the first region consists of the arc of $f$ from $(0,\,6)$ to $\left(\tfrac{8}{3},\,\tfrac{8}{3}\right)$ together with the arc of $f^{-1}$ running back; reflecting it gives the arc of $f^{-1}$ from $(6,\,0)$ to $\left(\tfrac{8}{3},\,\tfrac{8}{3}\right)$ together with the arc of $f$ running back, which is exactly the boundary of the second region. A reflection is an isometry and preserves area, so the two regions have equal areas (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for stating $f(0) = 6$ and $f(6) = 0$, which needs the observation that a decreasing bijection of an interval onto itself must exchange the endpoints; (A1) for explaining that the reflection of one of these points is the other, so each lies on both graphs. Accept $f^{-1}(0) = 6$ and $f^{-1}(6) = 0$ as the justification. Do not credit a candidate who merely reads two crossing points off the figure without justifying them: the point of the part is that they must be there.

(b) (M1) for setting $f(x) = x$ on a segment and obtaining a candidate value; (A1) for $\left(\tfrac{8}{3},\,\tfrac{8}{3}\right)$ together with a check that the value lies in the segment it came from. A candidate who obtains $x = 2.4$ or $x = 3$ and fails to reject it earns the method mark only, because the rejection is precisely what makes $8/3$ the answer rather than one of three.

(c) (M1) for identifying the two arcs that bound one region and tracking where the reflection sends them; (A1) for concluding that a reflection preserves area. A candidate who says simply that the figure is symmetric has not yet said which region maps to which, and should be pressed: the symmetry that matters is the symmetry of the pair of curves, not of either curve alone.

59. 5.8 A stationary value that is exactly zero, so a crossing of the axis becomes a touching point and the root count drops by one

[6 marks]MATH-5.8-401

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.8 Local maximum and minimum points; SL 5.11 Definite integrals, including the analytical approach; 5.2 Increasing and decreasing functions · difficulty 5

(a) The area between the graph of $f'$ and the axis from $0$ to $3$ is the triangle $\tfrac{1}{2} \times 3 \times 3 = 4.5$, and from $3$ to $4.5$, where $f'$ is still positive, it is $\tfrac{1}{2} \times 1.5 \times 3 = 2.25$. So $f(4.5) = -2.25 + 4.5 + 2.25 = 4.5$ (M1). From $4.5$ to $6$ the signed area is $-\tfrac{1}{2} \times 1.5 \times 3 = -2.25$ and from $6$ to $7.5$ it is another $-2.25$, so $f(7.5) = 4.5 - 2.25 - 2.25 = 0$ (A1).

(b) On $0 \leq x \leq 3$ the graph of $f'$ is the line $f'(x) = x$, so $f(x) = -2.25 + \tfrac{x^{2}}{2}$ on that interval (M1). Setting $f(x) = 0$ gives $x^{2} = 4.5$, so $x = \sqrt{4.5} = \tfrac{3\sqrt{2}}{2} \approx 2.12$, which lies in $[0,\,3]$. Since $f' \geq 0$ throughout $[0,\,4.5]$ the function is increasing there and this is the only solution in that interval; on $[4.5,\,7.5]$ the function falls from $4.5$ to $0$ without reaching zero again before the endpoint, and on $[7.5,\,9]$ it rises from $0$. The other solution is therefore $x = \tfrac{3\sqrt{2}}{2}$ (A1).

(c) There are two distinct solutions (A1): $x = \tfrac{3\sqrt{2}}{2}$, where the graph crosses the axis, and $x = 7.5$. At $x = 7.5$ the graph of $f'$ crosses the axis, so $f'(7.5) = 0$ and $x = 7.5$ is a stationary point of $f$; part (a) shows that the value there is exactly $0$. The function is decreasing immediately before $7.5$ and increasing immediately after it, so the graph comes down to the axis, touches it at its minimum and turns back up. It does not cross, the root is a repeated one, and the three roots that two turning points suggest never materialise (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for an area calculation that reaches $f(4.5) = 4.5$; (A1) for the two negative contributions and the conclusion $f(7.5) = 0$. A candidate who rounds the areas to $4.5$, $2$, $-2$, $-2$ and obtains $f(7.5) = 0.5$ has done the right mathematics on the wrong numbers and cannot be credited: the whole part turns on the value being *exactly* zero, and the gradient of the segment is $-2$, so the areas are exact halves and there is nothing to round.

(b) (M1) for identifying $f'(x) = x$ on the first segment and integrating to $-2.25 + \tfrac{x^{2}}{2}$; (A1) for $\tfrac{3\sqrt{2}}{2}$ together with a statement that it lies in $[0,\,3]$. Decimal $2.12$ is acceptable only if the exact form is also given, since the part says *exact*.

(c) (A1) for the answer two; (A1) for linking $f'(7.5) = 0$ with $f(7.5) = 0$ to conclude that the axis is touched rather than crossed. Credit should not be withheld from a candidate who says the root is repeated instead of using the words touch and cross.

60. 4.13 Bayes' theorem

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.13-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · AHL 4.13 (Bayes' theorem for a maximum of three events); SL 4.9 (binomial distribution) · difficulty 4

(a) Let $D$ be the event 'defective'.

$$P(D)=P(M_{1})P(D\mid M_{1})+P(M_{2})P(D\mid M_{2})+P(M_{3})P(D\mid M_{3})$$ (M1)

$$=0.40(0.02)+0.35(0.03)+0.25(0.05)$$ (A1)

$$=0.008+0.0105+0.0125$$ (A1)

$$P(D)=0.031\ \ (3.1\%).$$ (A1)

(b) By Bayes' theorem:

$$P(M_{2}\mid D)=\frac{P(M_{2}\cap D)}{P(D)}=\frac{P(M_{2})P(D\mid M_{2})}{P(D)}$$ (M1)

$$=\frac{0.35(0.03)}{0.031}=\frac{0.0105}{0.031}$$ (A1)

$$=0.3387\ldots$$ (A1)

$$P(M_{2}\mid D)=0.339\ (3\ \text{s.f.})$$ (A1)

(c) Let $X$ be the number of defectives in a box. Then $X\sim\mathrm{B}(20,\,0.031)$. (M1)(A1)

$$P(X\ge1)=1-P(X=0)=1-(1-0.031)^{20}$$ (M1)

$$=1-0.969^{20}=1-0.5326$$ (A1)

$$=0.467\ (3\ \text{s.f.})$$

(d) The fifth component tested is defective, and components 1 to 4 are not. Let $F$ be this event.

$$P(M_{3}\mid F)=\frac{P(M_{3}\cap F)}{P(F)}=\frac{P(M_{3}\cap D)}{P(D)}$$ (M1)(A1)

because $F$ says exactly two things about component 5 — that it is defective, and that components 1–4 are not — and the machine that made component 5 is independent of what was found in components 1–4. So the information about components 1–4 is irrelevant to the question asked, and $F$ reduces to '$D$' for the fifth component.

$$=\frac{0.25(0.05)}{0.031}=\frac{0.0125}{0.031}=0.4032\ldots$$ (A1)

$$P(M_{3}\mid F)=0.403\ (3\ \text{s.f.})$$

Why it is the same as for the first component: whether the defective component turns up first, fifth or fiftieth, the only fact that carries information about its origin is that it *is* defective. The positions of the earlier non-defective components depend on the overall defective rate $P(D)=0.031$, which is the same for every machine's output because the components are mixed before packing; they therefore tell us nothing new about which machine made the one we eventually found. (R1)

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the total-probability structure. Accept a correctly labelled tree diagram in place of the formula. Follow through on an arithmetic slip in the sum.

(b) Bayes' theorem must be *identified* (by formula or by reading the tree diagram backwards). Candidates who compute $P(D\mid M_{2})=0.03$ have answered a different question and earn nothing here.

(c) The binomial model is the key step: $n=20$ independent components, constant $p=0.031$. Candidates who use $1-0.031^{20}$ (confusing 'all defective' with 'none defective') forfeit the method mark. Accept $0.467$–$0.468$.

(d) This is the discriminating part. Full marks require recognising that the stopping rule is irrelevant; award (M1) for setting up the conditional probability, (A1) for the correct reduction to $P(M_3\cap D)/P(D)$, (A1) for $0.403$, and (R1) for the independence argument. A candidate who attempts the full computation
$$P(F)=\bigl(P(\text{not }D)\bigr)^{4}P(D)=0.969^{4}(0.031)$$
and $P(M_{3}\cap F)=0.969^{4}\times0.0125$ should be given full credit — it gives the same answer, and the cancellation of the $0.969^{4}$ factor *is* the explanation.

Additional condonations: in (b) accept $\tfrac{21}{62}$ or the unrounded $0.3387$; in (c) condone $46.7\%$; in (d) accept $0.4032$ unrounded, and credit the conditional-independence argument that $F$ and the machine are independent given that component 5 is defective.

61. Related rates on a sliding ladder, solved by implicit differentiation

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.14-001

Topic 5: Calculus · AHL 5.14 (implicit differentiation; related rates of change; optimisation, including an end-point optimum) · difficulty 4

Differentiate $x^{2}+y^{2}=169$ implicitly with respect to $t$: $2x\frac{dx}{dt}+2y\frac{dy}{dt}=0$, so $\frac{dy}{dt}=-\frac{x}{y}\frac{dx}{dt}$ (M1)(A1)(A1)(AG). This is the related-rates equation that links the two speeds through the geometry of the ladder.

When $x=5$ we have $y=\sqrt{169-25}=\sqrt{144}=12$ (M1). With $\frac{dx}{dt}=0.5$, $\frac{dy}{dt}=-\frac{5}{12}(0.5)=-\frac{5}{24}$ m s$^{-1}$ (A1)(AG), so the top of the ladder descends at $\frac{5}{24}$ m s$^{-1}$, approximately $0.208$ m s$^{-1}$.

Set $\frac{dy}{dt}=-1$. Then $-\frac{x}{y}(0.5)=-1$, giving $\frac{x}{y}=2$ and hence $x=2y$ (M1)(A1). Substitute into the constraint: $(2y)^{2}+y^{2}=5y^{2}=169$, so $y^{2}=\frac{169}{5}$ and $y=\sqrt{\frac{169}{5}}$. Therefore $x=2\sqrt{\frac{169}{5}}\approx 11.63$ m (M1)(A1)(AG). As a check, with $x=2\sqrt{169/5}$ we have $x^{2}+y^{2}=4\cdot\frac{169}{5}+\frac{169}{5}=\frac{5\cdot 169}{5}=169$, confirming the constraint. The foot lies between $0$ and $13$, so the configuration is physically valid. The sign convention is that a negative rate of change denotes descent of the top, which is why the requested answer is reported as a positive speed of descent. The sign convention is that a negative rate of change denotes descent of the top, which is why the requested answer is reported as a positive speed of descent.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for differentiating the constraint implicitly with respect to $t$, (A1) for $2x\,dx/dt+2y\,dy/dt=0$, (A1) for isolating $dy/dt$, and (A1)(AG) for the stated formula $-(x/y)\,dx/dt$. A candidate who differentiates with respect to $x$ instead of $t$ has not treated $x$ and $y$ as functions of time and forfeits the first two marks.

(b) Award (M1) for finding $y=12$ at $x=5$ from the constraint, (A1) for substituting both values into the related-rates equation, and (A1)(AG) for $-5/24$. The sign is part of the answer: a positive value describing the top as rising does not earn the final mark. Accept $-0.208\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ or the exact fraction.

(c) Award (M1) for setting $dy/dt=-1$ and forming the equation, (A1) for deducing $x=2y$, (A1) for solving $5y^{2}=169$, and (A1)(AG) for $x=2\sqrt{169/5}$. Award (M1) for a verification that the values satisfy the original constraint $x^{2}+y^{2}=169$. Note the foot must lie between $0$ and $13$; $11.63$ does, so accept it. A candidate who stops at $x=2y$ without substituting into the constraint earns only the (M1)(A1), not the final (A1)(AG) for the numerical distance. The numerical answer 11.63 m follows by rounding the exact form $2\sqrt{169/5}$. Watch for the common slip of writing $y=2x$ rather than $x=2y$, which is an inverted ratio: it produces $x=\sqrt{169/5}=5.81$, the height rather than the distance from the wall, and is follow-through only as far as the substitution mark.

62. Deriving the arctangent derivative by implicit differentiation, then using it

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.15-001

Topic 5: Calculus · AHL 5.15 (derivatives of arctan x and other inverse trigonometric and exponential functions; indefinite integration) · difficulty 4

Write $x=\tan y$, so $\frac{dx}{dy}=\sec^{2}y$ (M1). By the chain rule $1=\sec^{2}y\,\frac{dy}{dx}$, giving $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{1}{\sec^{2}y}$ (A1). Using the identity $\sec^{2}y=1+\tan^{2}y=1+x^{2}$ we obtain $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}$ (A1)(AG). This is the derivative of $\arctan x$, derived rather than recalled.

Because integration reverses differentiation, $\int\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\,dx=\arctan x+C$ (M1)(A1)(AG), where the constant of integration is essential and must be stated.

Thus $\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\,dx=\arctan 1-\arctan 0=\frac{\pi}{4}-0=\frac{\pi}{4}$ (M1)(A1), and $\int_{1}^{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\,dx=\arctan(\sqrt{3})-\arctan 1=\frac{\pi}{3}-\frac{\pi}{4}=\frac{\pi}{12}$ (A1)(AG). Exact multiples of $\pi$ are required; decimal approximations alone are not sufficient for full credit, though a numerical check such as $0.7854$ and $0.2618$ may be offered as confirmation. The indefinite integral also follows by the substitution $x=\tan u$, $dx=\sec^{2}u\,du$, which reduces the integrand to $1$ and gives $\arctan x+C$, confirming the result by a second method. For the definite integrals the antiderivative is continuous on the closed interval, so the fundamental theorem applies directly with no improper-integral care required. The continuity of the antiderivative on the whole interval guarantees that the endpoint evaluation is valid and that no singularity occurs within the range of integration, so both definite integrals are proper. The method also shows why the antiderivative is unique up to the additive constant, since any two antiderivatives of the same function differ by a constant.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for implicit differentiation of $x=\tan y$ with respect to $x$, (A1) for $1=\sec^{2}y\,dy/dx$, (A1) for replacing $\sec^{2}y$ by $1+\tan^{2}y$, and (A1)(AG) for $1/(1+x^{2})$. (b) Award (M1) for reversing the derivative to obtain the antiderivative and (A1)(AG) for $\arctan x+C$; deduct for a missing $+C$. (c) Award (M1) for applying the limits to the antiderivative, (A1) for $\pi/4$, (A1) for $\pi/3-\pi/4$, and (A1)(AG) for $\pi/12$. Exact multiples of $\pi$ are required; award partial credit if a correct numerical method is shown but the exact form is missing, and deduct (A1) for each integral given only as a decimal. The substitution method in part (b) is an acceptable alternative route to the same antiderivative. The exact forms pi/4 and pi/12 must be shown; a candidate who gives only decimal approximations loses the relevant (A1) even when the numerical value is correct, and the link back to the part (a) derivative should be acknowledged when awarding the indefinite integral.

63. The condition for a real cubic to have a purely imaginary root

[16 marks]MATH-AHL1.14-001

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.14 (complex conjugate roots of quadratic and polynomial equations with real coefficients), AHL 1.12 (the number i, where i^2 = -1) · difficulty 4

(a) Since $p$, $q$ and $r$ are real, conjugating the equation $P(z_{0})=0$ gives $\overline{P(z_{0})}=0$. Conjugation respects sums and products with real coefficients, so $\overline{P(z_{0})}=P(\bar z_{0})$. (M1) Hence $P(\bar z_{0})=0$, so $\bar z_{0}$ is also a root. (A1)

If $z_{0}$ is non-real then $z_{0}\neq\bar z_{0}$, so $P$ has at least the two distinct non-real roots $z_{0}$ and $\bar z_{0}$. A cubic has exactly three roots counted with multiplicity; if the third root were also non-real, its conjugate would be a fourth root. Hence the third root is real, so $P$ has exactly one real root. (R1)(A1)

(b) Let $z_{0}=ti$ with $t\in\mathbb{R}$ and $t\neq0$. Substituting,

$$P(ti)=(ti)^{3}+p(ti)^{2}+q(ti)+r=-t^{3}i-pt^{2}+qti+r.$$ (M1)

So $P(ti)=\left(r-pt^{2}\right)+\left(qt-t^{3}\right)i=0$, and both the real and imaginary parts must vanish. (A1)

The imaginary part gives $t(q-t^{2})=0$; since $t\neq0$ this forces $t^{2}=q$, and therefore $q=t^{2}>0$. (A1) The real part gives $r-pt^{2}=0$, so $r=pt^{2}=pq$. (A1) *(AG)*

(c) Here $p=-3$, $q=k$ and $r=-12$. The condition $r=pq$ becomes $-12=-3k$, so $k=4$, and $q=k=4>0$ is satisfied. (M1)(A1) Then $t^{2}=q=4$, so the purely imaginary roots are $\pm2i$. The third root is real and equals $-p=3$, since the sum of the roots of $z^{3}+pz^{2}+qz+r$ is $-p$. (M1) Indeed $(z-3)(z^{2}+4)=z^{3}-3z^{2}+4z-12$, so the roots are $3$, $2i$ and $-2i$. (A1)

(d) For $z^{3}+mz^{2}+mz+1$ we have $p=m$, $q=m$ and $r=1$. The necessary condition $r=pq$ gives $1=m^{2}$, so $m=\pm1$. (M1)(A1) But the condition from (b) also requires $q>0$, that is $m>0$, so $m=-1$ must be rejected. (M1)

Indeed for $m=-1$ the cubic is $z^{3}-z^{2}-z+1=(z-1)^{2}(z+1)$, whose roots $1$, $1$ and $-1$ are all real, so it has no purely imaginary root. (A1) Hence the only admissible value is $m=1$, and then $z^{3}+z^{2}+z+1=(z+1)(z^{2}+1)$, with roots $-1$, $i$ and $-i$. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the conjugation argument; the (R1) is for the counting argument that a cubic cannot have exactly one non-real root. A candidate who asserts 'non-real roots come in pairs' without mentioning multiplicity still earns the (R1), provided the cubic is named. Do not accept 'it must have one real root because the graph crosses the axis', which assumes what is being proved.

(b) The substitution must be shown with the real and imaginary parts separated; a candidate who writes down $r=pq$ with no working earns nothing. The hypothesis $t\neq0$ is used to divide, and its use should be visible. A candidate who also considers $t=0$ and notes that then $z=0$ is real rather than purely imaginary should be credited.

(c) Award (M1) for substituting into $r=pq$ and (A1) for $k=4$; the check $q>0$ is required for the second (A1). The third root may be found by division, by Vieta, or by the factorisation; accept any. A candidate who mis-signs Vieta and writes the sum of the roots as $p$ will get $3$ anyway here, so look for the reasoning rather than the value.

(d) This is the discriminating part. Both $m=\pm1$ satisfy $r=pq$, but only $m=1$ has $q=m>0$. A candidate who answers $m=\pm1$ loses the final three marks. Award (M1)(A1) for the two candidates, (M1) for invoking $q>0$, (A1) for exhibiting the real-root factorisation of the rejected case, and (A1) for the roots in the valid case. Follow-through is allowed only within (d).

64. The parameter that turns the quadratic into a straight line

[16 marks]MATH-AHL2.6-001

Topic 2: Functions · SL 2.6 (the quadratic function f(x) = ax^2 + bx + c: its graph, vertex, axis of symmetry and discriminant) · difficulty 4

(a) Setting $m=1$ makes the coefficient of $x^{2}$ equal to zero, so the quadratic degenerates to a linear expression (M1). Substituting gives $f(x)=2(1+1)x+(1+2)=4x+3$ (A1). Hence the graph is the straight line $y=4x+3$ (A1) *(AG)*. The coefficient of $x^{2}$ is the only term that can vanish, so this is the single value at which the family changes type.

(b) Completing the square for $m\neq1$, write $f(x)=(m-1)\left[x^{2}+\frac{2(m+1)}{m-1}x\right]+(m+2)$ (M1). The bracket becomes $\left(x+\frac{m+1}{m-1}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{m+1}{m-1}\right)^{2}$ (A1), so the axis of symmetry is $x=-\frac{m+1}{m-1}$ (A1). Substituting this value and simplifying gives $-\frac{m+3}{m-1}$ (M1)(A1). The vertex is therefore $\left(-\frac{m+1}{m-1},-\frac{m+3}{m-1}\right)$. This is the standard vertex formula for a parabola, and it is valid for every value of $m$ except the one excluded by the question.

(c) The discriminant is $[2(m+1)]^{2}-4(m-1)(m+2)$ (M1) $=4(m+1)^{2}-4(m-1)(m+2)$ (A1) $=4(m+3)$ (A1). The graph misses the $x$-axis exactly when the discriminant is negative, so $4(m+3)<0$ (M1), giving $m<-3$ (A1). Because the discriminant is linear in $m$, its sign changes at exactly one value, so the set of such $m$ is a single ray rather than a bounded interval.

(d) For $m>1$ the leading coefficient $m-1$ is positive, so the parabola opens upwards (A1). The $y$-coordinate of the vertex is $-\frac{m+3}{m-1}$, and both $m+3$ and $m-1$ are positive, so this value is negative (R1). An upward parabola whose vertex lies below the $x$-axis must cross the axis twice (M1), so $f(x)=0$ has two distinct real roots (A1), consistent with $4(m+3)>0$. The sign of the discriminant gives independent confirmation of this count, so the number of real roots is exactly two.

Markscheme notes

(a) The intended observation is that $m=1$ removes the $x^{2}$ term. Accept any statement that the graph has degree one. A candidate who substitutes $m=1$, writes $f(x)=4x+3$ and states the equation of the line earns all three marks; the word 'straight' is not required if the equation is given.

(b) The (M1) is for an honest attempt at completing the square, or for using $x=-\frac{b}{2a}$. The (A1) for the axis is independent, so a candidate with a correct $x$-coordinate but an arithmetic slip in the $y$-coordinate keeps the axis mark. Accept the vertex written in any equivalent algebraic form.

(c) Award (M1) for a correct discriminant expression even if the simplification is wrong. The final (A1) requires the strict inequality $m<-3$; writing $m\le-3$ forfeits it, because at $m=-3$ the curve is tangent to the axis and does meet it.

(d) This part tests reasoning rather than computation. The (R1) requires both signs to be justified. A candidate who says only that the vertex is below the axis, without saying that the parabola opens upwards, cannot reach the conclusion. Follow through from an incorrect vertex in (b) is allowed here.

Extension: the case $m<-3$ gives no real roots, while $-3<m<1$ gives two roots, and $m=1$ gives one.

65. Two curves that are reflections, and the range of k that keeps both roots

[16 marks]MATH-AHL2.9-001

Topic 2: Functions · SL 2.9 (exponential functions and their graphs, including the asymptote and the relationship between y = a^x and y = (1/a)^x) and SL 2.10 (solving equations analytically) · difficulty 4

(a) Both curves have the negative $x$-axis as a horizontal asymptote, that is $y=0$ (A1). Since $3^{0}=1$, both pass through $(0,1)$ (A1). The graph of $f$ rises from left to right without bound, while the graph of $g$ falls from left to right, tending to zero (A1)(A1). Both curves lie entirely above the $x$-axis, so neither ever meets it, and the only point they share is the intercept.

(b) Replacing $x$ by $-x$ in $f$ gives $f(-x)=3^{-x}=g(x)$ (M1)(A1), so $g$ is the reflection of $f$ in the $y$-axis. At an intersection $3^{x}=3^{-x}$ (M1), so $3^{2x}=1$ and hence $x=0$ (A1); the point is $(0,1)$. The reflection is a symmetry of this particular pair of curves, and it does not depend on the value of the base.

(c) We have $3^{x-2}+1=f(x-2)+1$. The graph of $f(x-2)$ is obtained by translating $y=f(x)$ by $2$ units in the positive $x$-direction (A1), and adding $1$ translates the result by $1$ unit in the positive $y$-direction (A1). The asymptote $y=0$ moves to $y=1$ (A1), and the $y$-intercept is $3^{0-2}+1=\frac{1}{9}+1=\frac{10}{9}$, that is $\left(0,\frac{10}{9}\right)$ (A1). Because both operations are translations, their order is immaterial, and the image is the same curve either way.

(d) Put $u=3^{x}$, so $u>0$ and the equation becomes $u^{2}-4u+k=0$ (M1). Two distinct real solutions in $x$ require two distinct positive roots $u$ (R1). The discriminant $16-4k$ must be positive, giving $k<4$ (M1)(A1), and the product of the roots, equal to $k$, must be positive, giving $k>0$ (A1). Hence $0<k<4$ (A1). The product condition is the one that is easily overlooked, and it is what removes the negative values of $k$ from the answer.

Markscheme notes

(a) The sketch need not be to scale, but both curves must show the correct shape: one increasing, one decreasing, both passing through $(0,1)$ and both asymptotic to the $x$-axis. Award the asymptote mark for $y=0$ and the intercept mark for $(0,1)$. A candidate who draws only one curve cannot earn more than two of the four marks.

(b) The (M1) is for the substitution $x\mapsto-x$, and the (A1) for identifying it as a reflection in the $y$-axis. The intersection mark is independent: award it for any correct argument, including equating indices.

(c) The order of the two translations does not matter here, because both are translations. Accept a translation of $2$ to the right and $1$ up in either order. The asymptote of the image is $y=1$, not $y=0$. The $y$-intercept must be exact; a decimal such as $1.11$ forfeits the final (A1).

(d) The (R1) is the crucial mark: it is not enough that the quadratic in $u$ has two distinct roots, because $u=3^{x}$ must be positive. A candidate who gives only $k<4$ has forgotten the positivity of $u$. Follow through from a correct discriminant in an otherwise wrong part.

Extension: for $k\le0$ exactly one root is positive, so the original equation has exactly one real solution.

66. The two points on the line that are exactly three units from the origin

[16 marks]MATH-AHL3.1-001

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · SL 3.1 (the distance between two points in three-dimensional space) · difficulty 4

(a) The vector $\overrightarrow{AB}=(3-1,4-0,0-2)=(2,4,-2)$ (M1) and $\overrightarrow{BC}=(5-3,8-4,-2-0)=(2,4,-2)$ (A1). The two vectors are equal, so the segments $AB$ and $BC$ are parallel (R1). Since they also share the point $B$, the three points must lie on one straight line (A1)(AG). Equal displacement vectors force $A$, $B$ and $C$ onto a single line, and no other arrangement of the points is consistent with the two vectors just found.

(b) The vector $\overrightarrow{AC}=(5-1,8-0,-2-2)=(4,8,-4)$ (M1). By the distance formula, $AC=\sqrt{4^{2}+8^{2}+(-4)^{2}}$ (M1) $=\sqrt{16+64+16}=\sqrt{96}$ (A1) $=4\sqrt{6}$ (A1), so $k=4$. The factor $16$ inside the root is what produces the exact surd form rather than a decimal. The distance formula used here is simply the three-dimensional form of Pythagoras applied to the three coordinate differences.

(c) Since $AP:PC=1:3$, the point $P$ divides $AC$ internally in that ratio, so $P=A+\frac{1}{4}(C-A)$ (M1). Now $\frac{1}{4}(C-A)=\frac{1}{4}(4,8,-4)=(1,2,-1)$ (A1), hence $P=(1,0,2)+(1,2,-1)$ (A1) $=(2,2,1)$ (A1). The ratio places $P$ one quarter of the way from $A$ towards $C$. The fraction multiplies the whole vector $C-A$, so reversing the ratio would place the point three quarters of the way along instead.

(d) A general point of the line is $(1+4t,8t,2-4t)$ (M1). Its squared distance from $O$ is $(1+4t)^{2}+(8t)^{2}+(2-4t)^{2}$ (M1) $=96t^{2}-8t+5$ (A1). Setting this equal to $9$ gives $96t^{2}-8t-4=0$, that is $24t^{2}-2t-1=0$ (M1), so $t=\frac{1}{4}$ or $t=-\frac{1}{6}$ (A1). The two points are therefore $(2,2,1)$ and $\left(\frac{1}{3},-\frac{4}{3},\frac{8}{3}\right)$ (A1); the first of these is exactly the point $P$ found in part (c) (R1). The negative root describes the point reached by travelling in the opposite direction from $A$, away from $C$, and it lies on the same infinite line.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for forming at least one of the vectors $\overrightarrow{AB}$ or $\overrightarrow{BC}$; the (A1) requires both vectors correct. The (R1) is the reasoning mark: candidates must state that equal vectors have the same direction and that the points share $B$, so a candidate who computes both vectors but draws no collinearity conclusion forfeits it. The (AG) confirms the printed result.

(b) Award (M1) for the vector $\overrightarrow{AC}$ and (M1) for the distance formula. The two (A1) marks are independent: one for reaching $\sqrt{96}$, one for the exact surd form $4\sqrt{6}$. Do not accept a decimal such as $9.798$.

(c) The (M1) is for the section formula in any correct form. A candidate who uses $\frac{3}{4}A+\frac{1}{4}C$ earns full credit. Accept the coordinates in any order provided they are clearly labelled.

(d) This is the discriminating part. The (M1) is for parametrising the line, and the second (M1) for forming the squared distance. The (A1) for the quadratic $24t^{2}-2t-1=0$ may be awarded for any equivalent form. Both values of $t$ are required for the (A1), and both points for the final (A1). The (R1) recognises that $(2,2,1)$ coincides with $P$; follow through from an incorrect $P$ is allowed.

67. The sector whose second radius cannot exist

[16 marks]MATH-AHL3.4-001

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · SL 3.4 (the circle: radian measure of angles, length of an arc and area of a sector) · difficulty 4

(a) The perimeter of the sector is $2r+r\theta=20$ (M1), so $r\theta=20-2r$ (A1). The area is $A=\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\theta=\frac{1}{2}r(r\theta)$ (M1) $=\frac{1}{2}r(20-2r)=10r-r^{2}$ (A1)(AG). Writing the area this way removes $\theta$ entirely and leaves a single-variable expression. The substitution is the whole idea of the first part: once the angle has been eliminated, the problem becomes a question about a single variable, and the fixed perimeter is the constraint that makes the elimination possible.

(b) Completing the square, $A=10r-r^{2}=25-(r-5)^{2}$ (M1)(A1). Since $(r-5)^{2}\ge 0$, the area is greatest when $r=5$ (A1), and the greatest value is $A=25$ cm$^{2}$ (A1). The value $r=5$ gives a genuine maximum rather than a minimum because the coefficient of $r^{2}$ is negative, so the parabola opens downwards. Completing the square is preferred to differentiation here because it displays the maximum and the value at which it occurs at the same time.

(c) Substituting $r=5$ into $r\theta=20-2r$ gives $5\theta=10$ (M1), so $\theta=2$ radians (A1). The arc length is $r\theta=5\cdot 2=10$ cm (M1)(A1). The angle of two radians is slightly less than a third of a full turn, and the arc length of ten centimetres is exactly half the fixed perimeter.

(d) Setting $10r-r^{2}=16$ gives $r^{2}-10r+16=0$ (M1), so $(r-2)(r-8)=0$ and $r=2$ or $r=8$ (A1). For $r=2$ the angle would be $\theta=\frac{20-4}{2}=8$ radians (A1), which exceeds $2\pi\approx 6.28$, so no such sector exists. For $r=8$ the angle is $\theta=\frac{20-16}{8}=0.5$ radians, which is admissible (R1), so only $r=8$ survives. The two roots are found by factorising, which is straightforward because their sum is ten and their product is sixteen. Only the geometric constraint removes the smaller one.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the perimeter $2r+r\theta$; the (A1) for solving it as $r\theta=20-2r$. The second (M1) is for writing the area as $\frac{1}{2}r(r\theta)$ and substituting, and the (A1) for reaching the printed form. Candidates who use $A=\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\theta$ and substitute $\theta=\frac{20-2r}{r}$ earn the same marks.

(b) Award (M1) for completing the square, or for differentiating $10-2r$ and setting the derivative to zero, and (A1) for the completed-square form. The maximum requires the value $r=5$ and the value $25$; each carries its own (A1). Accept $A\le 25$ stated as the greatest area.

(c) The (M1) is for substituting $r=5$ into the perimeter relation; the (A1) is for $\theta=2$. The arc-length marks are independent: (M1) for using $r\theta$ and (A1) for $10$ cm. Do not accept $2^{\circ}$ or any other degree measure.

(d) The (M1) is for forming the quadratic and the (A1) for both roots. The third (A1) is for computing the angle $8$ radians, and the (R1) is for rejecting it because it exceeds $2\pi$. A candidate who rejects $r=2$ without computing the angle earns at most two of the four marks. Follow through from an incorrect quadratic is allowed provided the rejection argument is sound.

68. The single recording error that moves the mean further than the median

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.1-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.1 (concepts of population, sample, random sample, discrete and continuous data) · difficulty 4

(a) A random sample gives every member of the population an equal chance of selection, independently of the others (M1). The population here is every mass that could have been recorded, and a random sample would select nine items using a random generator (A1). A convenience sample takes the items easiest to reach, so awkward items are excluded and the sample is not representative (R1). That exclusion biases any estimate from the data, whereas random selection keeps the sample representative (A1). Random selection makes the summary trustworthy.

(b) Adding the nine values gives $5+6+7+8+9+10+11+12+40=108$ (M1), so the mean is $\frac{108}{9}=12$ (A1). In order, the fifth value is $9$, so the median is $9$ (A1). The mean lies above the median, suggesting a tail towards the larger values, because one large value lifts the mean without moving the middle (R1). The comparison indicates skew.

(c) With nine ordered values the lower quartile is the mean of the second and third, $\frac{6+7}{2}=6.5$ (M1), and the upper quartile is the mean of the seventh and eighth, $\frac{11+12}{2}=11.5$ (A1). The interquartile range is $11.5-6.5=5$ (A1). The upper boundary is $Q_3+1.5\times IQR=11.5+7.5=19$, and since $40>19$ the value $40$ is an outlier (A1)(AG). The standard $1.5$ IQR rule places $40$ far outside this boundary.

(d) Removing $40$ leaves $n=8$ and a total of $108-40=68$ (M1), so the new mean is $\frac{68}{8}=8.5$ (A1). The new median is the mean of the fourth and fifth values, $\frac{8+9}{2}=8.5$ (A1). The mean falls by $3.5$ while the median falls by only $0.5$, because the mean uses the size of every value and reacts to the outlier, while the median depends only on position (R1). This difference separates the two averages.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the idea of equal chance of selection. The (A1) requires a concrete mechanism, such as numbering the items and using a random generator, applied to this context. The (R1) is for naming the bias that convenience sampling introduces, and the final (A1) for linking random selection to representativeness. A candidate who only defines a random sample without applying it to masses earns at most two marks.

(b) Award (M1) for the sum $108$, (A1) for the mean $12$, and (A1) for the median $9$. The (R1) requires a statement about skew or tail direction; merely saying the mean is larger earns nothing unless it is linked to the shape.

(c) The (M1) is for either quartile position; the (A1) requires both quartiles correct. The third (A1) is for the interquartile range $5$, and the final (A1)(AG) for the boundary $19$ together with the explicit comparison $40>19$. A candidate who quotes $19$ but never compares it with $40$ forfeits the last mark.

(d) Award (M1) for the reduced total, (A1) for the new mean, and (A1) for the new median. The (R1) is for the positional-versus-magnitude explanation. Follow through from an incorrect outlier decision is allowed, provided the arithmetic is consistent.

69. Why the tallest bar is not the most crowded class

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.2-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.2 (presentation of data: frequency distributions, histograms, cumulative frequency, box-and-whisker plots) · difficulty 4

(a) With unequal widths a tall bar shows a wide class, not a crowded one, so the axis must show frequency per unit of width (M1). Frequency density is frequency divided by class width, so the five densities are $\frac{12}{2}=6$ (A1), $\frac{8}{2}=4$ and $\frac{40}{4}=10$ (A1), then $\frac{24}{4}=6$ and $\frac{16}{8}=2$ (A1). The tallest bar is the class $4\le x<8$, with density $10$, although its width is only four. Density, not frequency, is comparable across unequal widths.

(b) The total frequency is $100$, so the median is at position $50$ (M1). The cumulative frequencies are $12$, $20$, $60$, $84$ and $100$, so the fiftieth value falls in the class $4\le x<8$ (A1). Within that class, $30$ of the $40$ observations are needed (M1), so the median is $4+\frac{30}{40}\times 4=7$ (A1). This assumes an even spread in the class.

(c) The lower quartile lies at position $25$ (M1). The cumulative frequency reaches $20$ at $x=4$, so $5$ more come from the class of width $4$, giving $Q_1=4+\frac{5}{40}\times 4=4.5$ (A1). The upper quartile lies at position $75$, and the cumulative frequency reaches $60$ at $x=8$, so $Q_3=8+\frac{15}{24}\times 4=10.5$ (A1). The interquartile range is $10.5-4.5=6$ (A1). Each quartile uses the same interpolation as the median.

(d) The ninetieth percentile lies at position $90$ (M1). The cumulative frequency reaches $84$ at $x=12$, so $6$ observations come from the class $12\le x<20$ of width $8$ and frequency $16$, giving $P_{90}=12+\frac{6}{16}\times 8=15$ (A1)(A1). In context, about ninety per cent lie below $15$, so ten per cent exceed it (A1). The value is an estimate, again assuming an even spread in the top class.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the idea that unequal widths require a density rather than a frequency. The three (A1) marks are for the five densities, grouped so that any three correct values earn two marks and all five earn three; accept the densities written as fractions. A candidate who plots frequencies forfeits the reasoning mark but may still earn the (A1) marks for the numbers.

(b) Award (M1) for locating position $50$, (A1) for identifying the class $4\le x<8$, (M1) for the interpolation fraction, and (A1) for the value $7$. A candidate who reads $7$ directly from the cumulative curve earns the same marks, provided the position is stated.

(c) The (M1) is for the position $25$; the (A1) is for $4.5$. The second (A1) is for $10.5$, and the final (A1) for the interquartile range $6$. Follow through from an incorrect $Q_1$ or $Q_3$ is allowed when the subtraction is consistent.

(d) The (M1) is for the position $90$ and (A1)(A1) for the interpolation and the value $15$. The final (A1) is for a contextual interpretation in terms of the proportion above or below $15$; a bare number without context does not earn it.

70. The coding that leaves the mean alone but doubles the spread

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.3-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.3 (measures of central tendency and dispersion; effect of constant changes to the data) · difficulty 4

(a) The mean of $A$ is $\frac{4+5+6+7+8}{5}=6$ (M1)(A1). The deviations from the mean are $-2$, $-1$, $0$, $1$ and $2$, whose squares are $4$, $1$, $0$, $1$ and $4$ (M1). The variance is $\frac{10}{5}=2$, so the standard deviation is $\sqrt{2}=1.41$ to three significant figures (A1). The divisor is $5$ because these values are treated as the whole population.

(b) Coding with $b=2a-6$ gives $2$, $4$, $6$, $8$ and $10$, whose mean is $\frac{30}{5}=6$ (M1)(A1). The deviations are doubled, so each squared deviation is multiplied by $4$ and the variance becomes $4\times 2=8$ (M1), giving a standard deviation of $\sqrt{8}=2\sqrt{2}=2.83$ (A1). The mean changes by the whole transformation because every value is shifted and scaled, while the standard deviation ignores the shift of $-6$ and is scaled only by the factor $2$.

(c) Since the coding is $w=1.5x-4$, the mean satisfies $14=1.5\bar{x}-4$ (M1), so $1.5\bar{x}=18$ and $\bar{x}=12$ (A1). The standard deviation is unaffected by the subtraction of $4$, so $4.5=1.5\sigma_x$ (M1), giving $\sigma_x=\frac{4.5}{1.5}=3$ (A1). The inverse transformation is obtained by solving the coding rule rather than applying it again.

(d) Subtracting $4$ translates every value by the same amount, so all deviations from the mean are unchanged and the standard deviation is unchanged (M1)(A1). Multiplying by $1.5$ scales every deviation by $1.5$, so the variance is multiplied by $1.5^2$ and the standard deviation by $1.5$ (A1). A researcher might code data to remove an awkward constant, such as a large offset, so that the numbers are easier to handle later (R1). Only the multiplicative part of the coding changes the spread.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the sum and (A1) for the mean $6$. The second (M1) is for the squared deviations, which may be listed rather than summed. The (A1) is for the variance $2$ or the standard deviation $\sqrt{2}$; accept $1.41$ but do not accept a standard deviation quoted without the variance shown.

(b) The (M1)(A1) pair is for the coded values and their mean $6$. The second (M1) is for recognising that the variance is multiplied by $4$, and the final (A1) for $2\sqrt{2}$. A candidate who recomputes from the coded list earns full credit, but the explanation must still refer to the multiplier.

(c) The (M1) is for forming $14=1.5\bar{x}-4$, and the (A1) for $\bar{x}=12$. The second (M1) is for $4.5=1.5\sigma_x$, with the final (A1) for $\sigma_x=3$. Follow through from an incorrect coding equation is allowed if the algebra is consistent.

(d) The (M1)(A1) pair is for the translation argument, and the (A1) for the scaling of the standard deviation by $1.5$. The (R1) is for any valid reason for coding, such as simplifying large or awkward numbers. A candidate who claims the subtraction changes the spread forfeits the reasoning mark.

71. Six outcomes, three questions, and the effect of replacement

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.5-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.5 (concepts of trial, outcome, equally likely outcomes, sample space and event) · difficulty 4

(a) Choosing two different numbers from four gives $\binom{4}{2}=6$ pairs, namely $\{1,2\}$, $\{1,3\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{2,3\}$, $\{2,4\}$ and $\{3,4\}$ (M1)(A1). Because each pair is equally likely and order does not matter, each outcome has probability $\frac{1}{6}$ (A1). The sample space therefore contains six equally likely outcomes, and every event can be counted directly from this list (A1). The unordered convention avoids counting each pair twice.

(b) The larger number is exactly $3$ in the pairs $\{1,3\}$ and $\{2,3\}$ (M1)(A1). There are two such outcomes out of six, so the probability is $\frac{2}{6}=\frac{1}{3}$ (A1). The outcome $\{3,4\}$ is excluded because its larger number is $4$, not $3$ (R1). The count is a direct enumeration, so no multiplication rule is needed.

(c) The condition requires the larger number to be at least $3$ and the smaller number to be odd. The qualifying pairs are $\{1,3\}$, $\{1,4\}$ and $\{3,4\}$ (M1)(A1), giving three outcomes out of six, so the probability is $\frac{3}{6}=\frac{1}{2}$ (A1). The pair $\{2,3\}$ fails because its smaller number, $2$, is even (R1). Checking each pair against both conditions is the only reliable way to count this event.

(d) With replacement each of the two choices is made from four numbers, giving $4\times 4=16$ equally likely ordered outcomes (M1)(A1). The two numbers are equal in the four outcomes $(1,1)$, $(2,2)$, $(3,3)$ and $(4,4)$, so the probability is $\frac{4}{16}=\frac{1}{4}$ (A1). The space now contains ordered pairs and repeated values, so it is larger and its outcomes are different from before (R1). Replacement is what makes the two choices independent.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for recognising the count $\binom{4}{2}$ or for listing pairs systematically, and (A1) for all six pairs. The second (A1) is for the probability $\frac{1}{6}$, and the final (A1) for stating that the outcomes are equally likely. A candidate who lists ordered pairs must still identify the six unordered outcomes.

(b) The (M1) is for identifying the outcomes whose larger member is $3$, and the (A1) for the pair $\{1,3\}$ or $\{2,3\}$. The final (A1) requires both pairs and the value $\frac{1}{3}$. The (R1) is for explaining the exclusion of $\{3,4\}$; a candidate who counts three favourable outcomes earns at most one mark.

(c) The (M1)(A1) pair is for the qualifying pairs $\{1,3\}$, $\{1,4\}$ and $\{3,4\}$. The (A1) is for $\frac{1}{2}$. The (R1) is for testing at least one excluded pair against both conditions, such as $\{2,3\}$ failing the odd condition.

(d) Award (M1) for the product $4\times 4$, and (A1) for the sixteen ordered outcomes. The (A1) is for the four equal pairs and the probability $\frac{1}{4}$, and the (R1) for describing how replacement changes the sample space. Follow through from part (a) is allowed.

72. Two events that look dependent until the union is conditioned on

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.6-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.6 (use of Venn diagrams, tree diagrams, sample space diagrams and tables of outcomes; combined events, mutually exclusive events, conditional probability, independence) · difficulty 4

(a) The addition rule for two events gives $P(H\cup G)=P(H)+P(G)-P(H\cap G)$ (M1) $=0.6+0.45-0.25$ (A1) $=0.8$ (A1), so the probability that at least one of the two events occurs is $0.8$ (A1). The subtraction is needed because the outcomes lying in both events would otherwise be counted twice, so the rule is valid for any two events in the same sample space, whether or not those events are independent.

(b) The conditional probability formula gives $P(H\mid G)=\frac{P(H\cap G)}{P(G)}=\frac{0.25}{0.45}$ (M1) $=\frac{25}{45}=\frac{5}{9}$ (A1). Applying the same formula the other way round, $P(G\mid H)=\frac{P(H\cap G)}{P(H)}=\frac{0.25}{0.6}$ (M1) $=\frac{25}{60}=\frac{5}{12}$ (A1). Each conditional probability restricts attention to the conditioning event, so its denominator is the probability of that event alone, which is why the two fractions differ even though both use the same joint probability.

(c) Two events are independent precisely when $P(H\cap G)=P(H)P(G)$ (M1). Here $P(H)P(G)=0.6\times 0.45=0.27$ (A1), and this is not equal to the given value $0.25$ (R1). Hence $H$ and $G$ are not independent (A1), because the product of the separate probabilities fails to reproduce the joint probability.

(d) Conditioning on the union restricts the sample space to $H\cup G$ (M1), so the required probability is $\frac{P(H\cap G)}{P(H\cup G)}$ (A1) $=\frac{0.25}{0.8}$ (A1) $=\frac{25}{80}=\frac{5}{16}=0.3125$ (A1). This conditioning event is the union rather than a single event, so the quantity is different from either answer in part (b), and the calculation is correct because the definition of conditional probability applies to any event of positive probability.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for the addition rule with the intersection subtracted; the first (A1) is for substituting the three given probabilities, the second (A1) for the arithmetic, and the third (A1) for the final value $0.8$. A candidate who adds $0.6$ and $0.45$ and stops at $1.05$ earns only the (M1).

(b) Award (M1) for the conditional probability formula in each direction and (A1) for each exact fraction; decimals such as $0.5556$ do not earn the (A1). The two (M1) marks are independent, so a candidate who reverses the denominator for $P(G\mid H)$ still keeps the first pair.

(c) The (M1) is for stating the independence test, and the (A1) is for the product $0.27$. The (R1) requires an explicit comparison of $0.27$ with $0.25$, and the final (A1) is for the conclusion. A bare assertion of dependence without the product forfeits two marks.

(d) This is the discriminating part. The (M1) is for recognising that the conditioning event is the union, the (A1) for the quotient structure, the next (A1) for substituting $0.25$ and $0.8$, and the final (A1) for the exact value $\frac{5}{16}$ or its decimal $0.3125$. Follow through from an incorrect union is allowed provided the quotient is formed correctly.

73. The rearranged line that is not the regression of x on y

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.10-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.10 (equation of the regression line of x on y; use of the regression line for prediction) · difficulty 4

(a) The mean of $x$ is $\bar{x}=\frac{84}{12}=7$ (M1) and the mean of $y$ is $\bar{y}=\frac{60}{12}=5$ (A1). Then $S_{xx}=638-\frac{84^{2}}{12}=638-588=50$ (M1)(A1) and $S_{xy}=460-\frac{84\times 60}{12}=460-420=40$ (A1)(AG). These centred sums subtract the variation already accounted for by the means, so they measure spread about the mean rather than about the origin, which is why the printed values are correct.

(b) The gradient of the regression line of $y$ on $x$ is $b=\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}=\frac{40}{50}=0.8$ (M1)(A1), and the intercept is $a=\bar{y}-b\bar{x}=5-0.8\times 7=-0.6$ (M1)(A1). Hence $y=-0.6+0.8x$ (A1). The line passes through the point of means $(7,5)$, which is what determines the intercept once the gradient is known.

(c) The gradient of the regression line of $x$ on $y$ uses $S_{yy}$, and $S_{yy}=380-\frac{60^{2}}{12}=80$ (M1)(A1). Therefore the gradient is $d=\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}}=\frac{40}{80}=0.5$ (A1), and the intercept is $c=\bar{x}-d\bar{y}=7-0.5\times 5=4.5$ (M1), so $x=4.5+0.5y$ (A1). Exchanging the roles of the two variables replaces $S_{xx}$ by $S_{yy}$ in the denominator, which is why this gradient differs from the one in part (b).

(d) Rearranging the line from part (b) gives $x=\frac{y+0.6}{0.8}=1.25y+0.75$ (M1)(A1). This is not the regression line of $x$ on $y$, because that line minimises the squared deviations in $x$ and has gradient $\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}}=0.5$ (M1). The two lines coincide only when the correlation is exactly $\pm 1$, whereas here $r=\frac{40}{\sqrt{50\times 80}}=0.632$ (A1)(R1). Rearranging changes the subject of the formula but not which variable is treated as the response, so the student has reversed the equation without reversing the regression.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for either mean and the (A1) for both. Award (M1) for the centred-sum formula and (A1) for $S_{xx}=50$, then (A1) for $S_{xy}=40$ and the (AG) for the printed results. A candidate who uses $638/12$ without subtracting the mean term earns the method marks only.

(b) The (M1)(A1) pair is for the gradient $\frac{40}{50}=0.8$ and the (M1)(A1) pair for the intercept $5-0.8\times 7$. The final (A1) is for the assembled equation. Accept the line written as $y=0.8x-0.6$.

(c) The (M1)(A1) is for computing $S_{yy}=80$, and the (A1) for the gradient $0.5$. The (M1) is for the intercept using the point of means and the final (A1) for the equation $x=4.5+0.5y$. A candidate who uses $S_{xx}$ as the denominator here earns no credit for the gradient, because that is precisely the error the part is testing.

(d) Award (M1)(A1) for the rearrangement to $x=1.25y+0.75$. The (M1) is for identifying the true gradient $0.5$, the (A1) for the correlation $0.632$ and the (R1) for the explanation that rearranging does not reverse the regression. Follow through from earlier parts is allowed.

74. The inverse normal value that scales with the standard deviation

[16 marks]MATH-AHL4.12-001

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · SL 4.12 (standardization of normal variables, z-values; inverse normal calculations) · difficulty 4

(a) Standardising gives $z=\frac{138-150}{12}$ (M1) $=-1$ (A1), so $P(X<138)=P(Z<-1)$ (M1) $=0.158655$ (A1). Standardising expresses the value as a number of standard deviations below the mean, after which the standard normal distribution supplies the probability directly. The value $0.158655$ is the area of the standard normal curve to the left of minus one, and a value below the mean therefore gives a negative standardised score.

(b) The two standardised values are $z_{1}=\frac{144-150}{12}=-0.5$ (M1) and $z_{2}=\frac{168-150}{12}=1.5$ (A1). Hence $P(144<X<168)=P(-0.5<Z<1.5)$ (M1) $=0.933193-0.308539=0.624655$ (A1). Subtracting the two cumulative probabilities removes the lower tail, which is correct because the required region is bounded on both sides and the lower tail must not be counted twice.

(c) The standard normal value satisfying $P(Z<z)=0.90$ is $z=1.281552$ (M1)(A1). Reversing the standardisation gives $x=150+1.281552\times 12$ (M1) $=165.379$ (A1). The inverse normal returns the number of standard deviations above the mean, so multiplying by the standard deviation and adding the mean returns to the original scale of the measurement.

(d) The required standardised value is unchanged at $1.281552$ (M1), so the new value is $x=150+1.281552\times 15$ (A1) $=169.223$ (A1). The increase is exactly $1.281552\times 3=3.845$ (R1), since the answer moves by the fixed $z$-value multiplied by the change in the standard deviation. The same standardised position therefore corresponds to a larger distance from the mean when the spread is wider, so the new value of $x$ is greater than the value found in part (c). The standardised value is a property of the ninety per cent tail alone and is completely independent of the units in which $X$ is measured.

Markscheme notes

(a) The (M1) is for standardising with the mean and standard deviation; the (A1) is for $z=-1$. The second (M1) is for evaluating the standard normal probability and the final (A1) for $0.158655$. Accept four significant figures.

(b) Award (M1) for the first $z$-value and (A1) for both $-0.5$ and $1.5$. The (M1) is for expressing the interval probability as a difference of cumulative values and the (A1) for $0.624655$. A candidate who adds the two tail probabilities instead of subtracting earns the method mark only.

(c) The (M1)(A1) pair is for the inverse normal value $1.281552$ and the (M1) for reversing the standardisation with the mean and standard deviation. The (A1) is for $x=165.379$. Do not accept a $z$-value taken from a two-tailed table.

(d) The (M1) is for recognising that the $z$-value is unchanged. The (A1) is for the substitution with $15$ and the second (A1) for $169.223$. The (R1) is for explaining that the increase equals the $z$-value times the change in the standard deviation. A candidate who recomputes the $z$-value from scratch earns no credit for the first mark, because the point is that it is fixed.

75. The two-sided limit that disappears because the one-sided limits disagree

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.1-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.1 (introduction to the concept of a limit; one-sided limits and limits that do not exist) · difficulty 4

(a) Substituting $x=1$ gives $f(1)=\frac{0}{0}$, which is undefined because division by zero is not permitted (M1), so the function has no value at that point (A1) and cannot be evaluated by direct substitution (A1). A zero denominator means the algebraic expression has no numerical value at $x=1$, so the formula breaks down exactly where the limit is investigated (R1). The expression therefore fails precisely because the denominator vanishes there.

(b) For $x>1$ the quantity $x-1$ is strictly positive, so $|x-1|=x-1$ and $f(x)=\frac{x^{2}-1}{x-1}=\frac{(x-1)(x+1)}{x-1}=x+1$ (M1)(A1). As $x$ tends to $1$ from above, this linear expression tends to $2$, so the right-hand limit is $2$ (A1)(AG). The factor $x-1$ cancels legitimately because it is never zero on this side of $1$, leaving a well behaved linear function. This is why the one-sided limit exists even though the formula is undefined at the point.

(c) For $x<1$ the quantity $x-1$ is negative, so $|x-1|=-(x-1)$ and $f(x)=\frac{x^{2}-1}{-(x-1)}=-(x+1)$ (M1)(A1). As $x$ tends to $1$ from below, $-(x+1)$ tends to $-2$, so the left-hand limit is $-2$ (A1)(AG). The minus sign is forced by the absolute value on the negative side, because the denominator equals the opposite of the numerator factor. The left-hand limit therefore exists as a finite value distinct from the right-hand one.

(d) A two-sided limit exists only when the two one-sided limits agree (M1). Here the right-hand limit is $2$ and the left-hand limit is $-2$ (A1), and since $2\neq -2$ (A1), the two-sided limit does not exist (R1). The unequal one-sided limits are exactly what prevent the overall limit from existing, because a proper limit must approach the same value from both directions.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for recognising that substituting $x=1$ produces $0/0$ and that division by zero is forbidden, (A1) for stating the expression is undefined, (A1) for noting the function has no value at $x=1$, and (R1) for the conclusion that direct evaluation fails and says nothing about the surrounding limit.

(b) The (M1) is for using $|x-1|=x-1$ when $x>1$ and cancelling the common factor to reach $x+1$; the (A1) is for that simplified linear form. The second (A1) with (AG) is for the right-hand limit $2$, awarded only when the limit is explicitly evaluated as $x\to1^{+}$. Follow through from a different but consistent simplification is allowed.

(c) The (M1) is for the negative absolute value $|x-1|=-(x-1)$ when $x<1$ and the simplification to $-(x+1)$; the (A1) is for that form. The (A1)(AG) pair is for the left-hand limit $-2$. The minus sign is the most common omission and must be present for the mark.

(d) Award (M1) for the criterion that one-sided limits must agree, (A1) for quoting both limits $2$ and $-2$, (A1) for the inequality $2\neq-2$, and (R1) for concluding the two-sided limit does not exist.

76. The cubic whose derivative flips sign twice

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.2-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.2 (increasing and decreasing functions; interpretation of f'(x)) · difficulty 4

(a) Differentiating term by term using the power rule gives $f'(x)=3x^{2}-6x-9$ (M1), which factors as $3(x^{2}-2x-3)$ (A1) and then as $3(x-3)(x+1)$ because $-3$ and $1$ sum to $-2$ and multiply to $-3$ (A1)(AG). Expanding $3(x-3)(x+1)$ returns $3x^{2}-6x-9$, confirming the factorisation is correct (R1). The derivative is therefore confirmed in a fully factored form that exposes its roots directly. This factorised shape is what makes the later sign analysis straightforward.

(b) The derivative is zero at $x=-1$ and $x=3$, which split the real line into three intervals (M1), and its sign is positive outside these roots and negative between them (A1). Hence $f$ is increasing on $x<-1$ and on $x>3$, and decreasing on $-1<x<3$ (A1)(A1). The sign change is read directly from the factored form $3(x-3)(x+1)$ by testing the sign of each linear factor. The function therefore rises, then falls, then rises again.

(c) Substituting $x=-1$ into the original function gives $f(-1)=(-1)^{3}-3(1)-9(-1)+5=-1-3+9+5=10$ (M1)(A1), so the stationary point is $(-1,10)$ (A1). Since the derivative changes from positive to negative as $x$ passes $-1$, the point is a local maximum (R1). The stationary point is therefore a peak on the curve at that coordinate.

(d) Substituting $x=3$ gives $f(3)=27-27-27+5=-22$ (M1)(A1), so the stationary point is $(3,-22)$ (A1). Since the derivative changes from negative to positive as $x$ passes $3$, the point is a local minimum (R1). The stationary point is therefore a trough, and the cubic has completed its rise-fall-rise pattern. The steep drop of 32 units between the two turning points is a direct consequence of the positive leading coefficient and is easily understated.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the derivative $3x^{2}-6x-9$, (A1) for factoring out $3$ to reach $3(x^{2}-2x-3)$, and (A1)(AG) for the fully factored $3(x-3)(x+1)$. The (R1) is for verifying by expansion or by quoting the sum and product of the roots. A candidate who expands correctly but does not factor earns only the (M1).

(b) The (M1) is for the two roots $x=-1$ and $x=3$. The (A1) is for the correct sign pattern, and the two final (A1) marks are for the increasing intervals $x<-1$ and $x>3$ and the decreasing interval $-1<x<3$. Accept interval notation with or without the endpoints included.

(c) Award (M1) for substituting $x=-1$, (A1) for the $y$-value $10$, (A1) for the point $(-1,10)$, and (R1) for identifying it as a local maximum from the sign change of $f'$. Follow through from an incorrect derivative is allowed.

(d) Award (M1) for substituting $x=3$, (A1) for $-22$, (A1) for the point $(3,-22)$, and (R1) for local minimum. Follow through from an incorrect derivative is allowed provided the nature matches the sign change.

77. The derivative that keeps a negative power

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.3-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.3 (derivative of f(x)=ax^n is f'(x)=anx^{n-1}, n in Z; negative integer powers included) · difficulty 4

(a) Writing $\frac{2}{x^{3}}=2x^{-3}$, the power rule gives $\frac{d}{dx}(3x^{4})=12x^{3}$, $\frac{d}{dx}(-5x^{2})=-10x$ and $\frac{d}{dx}(2x^{-3})=-6x^{-4}$, while the constant $-7$ differentiates to zero (M1)(A1). Adding these three pieces yields $f'(x)=12x^{3}-10x-6x^{-4}$ (A1)(AG). The negative power remains because the index of $2x^{-3}$ drops from $-3$ to $-4$ when differentiated (R1). The derivative is therefore confirmed with the negative power retained rather than converted back to a fraction. This is the form that must be used for every later substitution.

(b) Substituting $x=1$ into the derivative gives $f'(1)=12(1)-10(1)-6(1)=12-10-6=-4$ (M1)(A1), so the gradient at $x=1$ is $-4$ (A1). The value is negative because the $-6$ term dominates the other two contributions at this point (R1). The gradient is therefore $-4$, meaning the curve is sloping downwards as it passes through $x=1$.

(c) Substituting $x=2$ gives $f'(2)=12(8)-10(2)-6(2^{-4})=96-20-\frac{6}{16}=76-0.375=75.625$ (M1)(A1), so the gradient at $x=2$ is $75.625$ (A1). The fractional subtraction must be carried through exactly, since $6/16=0.375$, to reach this precise decimal (R1). The gradient is therefore $75.625$, a steep positive slope that contrasts sharply with the negative slope at $x=1$.

(d) Substituting $x=-1$ gives $f'(-1)=12(-1)-10(-1)-6(1)=-12+10-6=-8$ (M1)(A1), so the gradient is $-8$ (A1). Because the gradient is negative, the function is decreasing at $x=-1$ (R1). The negative gradient confirms the function falls there, just as it does at $x=1$, although the magnitude of the slope is larger. The negative gradient at both $x=1$ and $x=-1$ shows the curve is sloping downwards at those two points.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for rewriting $2/x^{3}$ as $2x^{-3}$ and differentiating each term with the power rule, (A1) for the three differentiated pieces, and (A1)(AG) for the combined derivative $12x^{3}-10x-6x^{-4}$. The (R1) is for noting the negative index is retained. A candidate who writes $6/x^{4}$ instead of $-6x^{-4}$ loses the final mark. The negative index is the single most tested detail and must not be dropped.

(b) The (M1) is for substituting $x=1$, (A1) for $12-10-6$, and (A1) for $-4$; the (R1) is for the negative gradient conclusion and its meaning for the slope of the curve.

(c) Award (M1) for substituting $x=2$, (A1) for $96-20-6/16$, (A1) for $75.625$, and (R1) for noting the exact fractional subtraction. A candidate who rounds $6/16$ to $0.4$ earns the value mark only if the final answer is $75.6$. Exact arithmetic is expected at HL.

(d) Award (M1) for substituting $x=-1$, (A1) for $-12+10-6$, (A1) for $-8$, and (R1) for stating the function is decreasing because the gradient is negative. Follow through from an incorrect derivative is allowed.

78. The tangent and normal to a curve that is not a polynomial

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.4-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.4 (tangents and normals at a given point, and their equations) · difficulty 4

(a) Writing $\frac{3}{x}=3x^{-1}$, the derivative is $\frac{dy}{dx}=4x-(-3x^{-2})=4x+3x^{-2}=4x+\frac{3}{x^{2}}$ (M1)(A1)(A1)(AG). The reciprocal term contributes $3x^{-2}$ because differentiating $-3x^{-1}$ raises the index from $-1$ to $-2$ and keeps the minus sign from the original term (R1). The derivative is therefore confirmed as a sum of a polynomial part and a reciprocal part. This formula is the foundation for locating both the tangent and the normal.

(b) At $x=1$, the $y$-coordinate is $y=2(1)^{2}-\frac{3}{1}=2-3=-1$ (M1)(A1), so the point on the curve is $(1,-1)$; the gradient there is $\frac{dy}{dx}=4(1)+\frac{3}{1^{2}}=4+3=7$ (A1). The gradient is found by substituting $x=1$ into the derivative just established (R1). The point and the gradient are therefore $(1,-1)$ and $7$, both of which are needed for the two line equations.

(c) Using the point-gradient form $y-y_{1}=m(x-x_{1})$ with $m=7$ and $(1,-1)$ gives $y+1=7(x-1)$, which rearranges to $y=7x-8$ (M1)(A1). The tangent shares the curve's gradient at the point of contact, so its slope is exactly $7$ (A1). The equation is therefore $y=7x-8$, a line that just touches the curve at that single point (R1).

(d) The normal is perpendicular to the tangent, so its gradient is the negative reciprocal $-\frac{1}{7}$ (M1)(A1), and its equation is $y+1=-\frac{1}{7}(x-1)$, which simplifies to $y=-\frac{x}{7}-\frac{6}{7}$ (A1). The normal's gradient is the negative reciprocal of the tangent's gradient, so their product is $-1$ (R1). The equation is therefore $y=-\frac{x}{7}-\frac{6}{7}$, a line that cuts the curve at right angles at the point.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for rewriting $3/x$ as $3x^{-1}$ and differentiating, (A1) for the intermediate $4x+3x^{-2}$, and (A1)(AG) for the final $4x+3/x^{2}$. The (R1) is for explaining the sign of the reciprocal term. The sign must be positive for full marks; a candidate writing $-3/x^{2}$ loses the reasoning mark. The reciprocal contribution is the main trap and must be shown explicitly.

(b) The (M1) is for substituting $x=1$ into $y$, (A1) for $y=-1$, (A1) for the gradient $7$ from the derivative, and (R1) for linking the gradient to the derivative. The point is $(1,-1)$. Both coordinates are required before either line can be written.

(c) Award (M1) for the point-gradient form, (A1) for $y+1=7(x-1)$, (A1) for $y=7x-8$, and (R1) for stating the tangent shares the curve's gradient. Accept equivalent forms such as $7x-y-8=0$.

(d) Award (M1) for the perpendicular gradient $-1/7$, (A1) for using point-gradient, (A1) for $y=-x/7-6/7$, and (R1) for the negative reciprocal property. Follow through from an incorrect gradient in (b) is allowed. Candidates who leave the answer in unsimplified point-gradient form earn the first two marks only.

79. Rebuilding the function from its derivative and a single point

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.5-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.5 (introduction to integration as anti-differentiation of polynomial and exponential functions; the constant of integration) · difficulty 4

(a) Integrating $f'(x)=6x^{2}-4x$ term by term gives $f(x)=6\cdot\frac{x^{3}}{3}-4\cdot\frac{x^{2}}{2}+C=2x^{3}-2x^{2}+C$ (M1)(A1). The constant $C$ must be included because integration undoes differentiation only up to an additive constant (A1). The general form is therefore $f(x)=2x^{3}-2x^{2}+C$, representing a whole family of parallel curves (R1). The antiderivative is confirmed with the constant present, since any two antiderivatives differ only by a constant. This family picture is essential: without the condition in part (b) the exact curve cannot be singled out.

(b) Using the condition $f(1)=5$ gives $2(1)^{3}-2(1)^{2}+C=5$, so $2-2+C=5$ and therefore $C=5$ (M1)(A1). The single data point pins down the otherwise free constant uniquely (A1). The particular function is therefore $f(x)=2x^{3}-2x^{2}+5$ (R1), the one member of the family that passes through $(1,5)$.

(c) With $C=5$ fixed, substituting $x=2$ gives $f(2)=2(8)-2(4)+5=16-8+5=13$ (M1)(A1), so $f(2)=13$ (A1). This value is found by substituting into the fully determined function, not by differentiating again (R1). The value is therefore $13$, the height of the curve above the axis at that point.

(d) By the fundamental theorem of calculus, $\int_{1}^{2}f'(x)\,dx=f(2)-f(1)$ (M1), so this equals $13-5=8$ (A1). Direct integration confirms it: $\int_{1}^{2}(6x^{2}-4x)\,dx=[2x^{3}-2x^{2}]_{1}^{2}=(16-8)-(2-2)=8$ (A1), showing the two routes agree (R1). The two methods agree, so the integral is $8$, which is exactly the net change in $f$ across the interval. Both the rebuilt function and the direct integral give the same number, which is the essence of the fundamental theorem.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for integrating each term of $6x^{2}-4x$, (A1) for $2x^{3}-2x^{2}$, (A1) for the constant $C$, and (R1) for noting an antiderivative needs the constant because differentiation erases it. A candidate who omits $C$ loses the constant mark but may earn the others. The constant is what distinguishes the family of solutions.

(b) The (M1) is for substituting $x=1$ into the antiderivative, (A1) for $2-2+C=5$, (A1) for $C=5$, and (R1) for the idea that one point fixes the constant uniquely. Follow through from an incorrect antiderivative is allowed.

(c) Award (M1) for substituting $x=2$, (A1) for $16-8+5$, (A1) for $13$, and (R1) for the interpretation that this is a function value, not a derivative. A candidate using the wrong $C$ forfeits only the final value if the method is sound.

(d) Award (M1) for the fundamental theorem $f(2)-f(1)$, (A1) for $13-5=8$, (A1) for the direct integration giving $8$, and (R1) for confirming the two methods agree. Both routes must be shown for full credit. Partial credit follows through from earlier errors in the antiderivative.

80. The second derivative that changes sign at two points

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.7-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.7 (the second derivative; concavity and points of inflection) · difficulty 4

(a) Begin by differentiating the polynomial $f(x)=x^{4}-8x^{3}+18x^{2}$ once with respect to $x$ to obtain $f'(x)=4x^{3}-24x^{2}+36x$ (M1), then differentiate this result a second time to obtain $f''(x)=12x^{2}-48x+36$ (A1). The quadratic $x^{2}-4x+3$ factors as $(x-1)(x-3)$, so $f''(x)=12(x-1)(x-3)$ (A1)(AG). This confirms the given form, because multiplying the brackets back together restores $12x^{2}-48x+36$ exactly, leaving no remainder, and this step underpins every later part.

(b) Concavity can only change where the second derivative vanishes, so solve $12(x-1)(x-3)=0$ (M1). A product of real factors is zero precisely when one factor is zero, giving $x-1=0$ or $x-3=0$, thus $x=1$ and $x=3$ (A1). These two values are the only real roots of the quadratic, so they form the complete set of candidates that must be tested for a sign change before any conclusion is drawn.

(c) Choose one convenient test value inside each of the three intervals. At $x=0$ we have $f''(0)=36>0$, so the curve is convex on $x<1$ (M1). At $x=2$ we have $f''(2)=-12<0$, so it is concave on the middle interval $1<x<3$ (A1). At $x=4$ we have $f''(4)=36>0$, so it is convex again on $x>3$ (A1). The sign of $f''(x)$ alone decides the concavity on every open interval, and the integer test values keep the arithmetic exact and easy to verify.

(d) At $x=1$ the second derivative passes from positive to negative, and at $x=3$ it passes from negative to positive (M1), so each of these points is a point of inflection (A1)(AG). The function values at the two points are $f(1)=11$ and $f(3)=27$. A point of inflection is defined by a genuine change of sign in $f''(x)$, which occurs at both $x=1$ and $x=3$, and this is precisely why the classification is valid.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the first derivative $4x^{3}-24x^{2}+36x$ and (A1) for the second derivative $12x^{2}-48x+36$. The factorisation (A1)(AG) requires $12(x-1)(x-3)$ to be shown; accept the working written in either order. A candidate who expands the bracket incorrectly loses the final mark but may keep the two derivative marks.

(b) Award (M1) for setting $12(x-1)(x-3)=0$ and (A1) for both roots $x=1$ and $x=3$. The remark that these are the only candidates is part of the reasoning and need not be written, but follow through from a wrong quadratic is allowed provided the solving step is consistent with it.

(c) Award (M1) for a correct sign test giving convex on $x<1$, (A1) for concave on $1<x<3$, and (A1) for convex on $x>3$. The three test values need not be $0$, $2$, $4$ but the computed signs must be correct. A candidate who states the concavity from $f'$ rather than $f''$ forfeits all three marks.

(d) Award (M1) for identifying the sign change at each root and (A1)(AG) for both points of inflection. The function values $11$ and $27$ are optional supporting detail; the decisive fact is the change of sign, and a candidate who states a root without showing the sign change earns at most one mark.

81. The cubic with a maximum and a minimum found by the second derivative

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.8-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.8 (local maximum and minimum points; use of f'(x)=0 and the second derivative test) · difficulty 4

(a) Differentiate the expanded form $f(x)=x^{3}-3x+2$ to obtain $f'(x)=3x^{2}-3$ (M1). Factoring the result gives $3(x^{2}-1)=3(x-1)(x+1)$ (A1)(AG). This matches the required derivative exactly, because the constant term $2$ disappears when the function is differentiated and the remaining terms factor neatly. The factorised form is the most useful version of the derivative, because it displays the roots directly without any further algebra.

(b) Stationary points occur where the gradient is zero, so solve $3(x-1)(x+1)=0$ (M1). A product is zero when $x-1=0$ or $x+1=0$, giving the two $x$-coordinates $x=-1$ and $x=1$ (A1). These are the only real solutions, so they are the complete list of places where the curve could turn. Both $x$-coordinates must be found before any classification can begin, since the second derivative test is applied separately at each one.

(c) The second derivative here is the simple linear function $f''(x)=6x$ (M1). At $x=-1$ we have $f''(-1)=-6<0$, which means the curve is bending downwards, so the point is a local maximum (A1). At $x=1$ we have $f''(1)=6>0$, which means the curve is bending upwards, so the point is a local minimum (A1). This linear second derivative is unusually convenient, because its sign is fixed simply by the sign of $x$, and the sign of $f''$ alone decides the nature of each stationary point.

(d) Substitute the two $x$-values back into $f$. We have $f(-1)=(-1)^{3}-3(-1)+2=4$, so the local maximum is at $(-1,4)$ (M1)(A1). Also $f(1)=1^{3}-3(1)+2=0$, so the local minimum is at $(1,0)$ (A1). Recording both coordinates completes the description of the turning points and confirms the overall shape of the cubic, which is why the final coordinates are correct.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the derivative $3x^{2}-3$ and (A1)(AG) for the factorised form $3(x-1)(x+1)$. Accept differentiation of the product $(x-1)^{2}(x+2)$ if it reaches the same result. A candidate who writes $3x^{2}-3$ but fails to factor it earns only the method mark and not the accuracy mark.

(b) Award (M1) for setting the derivative equal to zero and (A1) for both roots $x=-1$ and $x=1$. Follow through from an incorrect derivative is allowed provided the equation is solved consistently with it. Both roots are required for the later classification marks in part (c).

(c) Award (M1) for stating $f''(x)=6x$, (A1) for the local maximum at $x=-1$ using the fact that $f''(-1)<0$, and (A1) for the local minimum at $x=1$ using $f''(1)>0$. The second derivative test must actually be applied; a candidate who classifies from a sketch earns no credit here.

(d) Award (M1) for substituting into $f$ and (A1) for $(-1,4)$, then (A1) for $(1,0)$. A candidate who gives only the $x$-values forfeits the coordinate marks, and the values $4$ and $0$ must be computed, not read from a graph.

82. The particle that stops twice and travels equal legs

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.9-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.9 (kinematic problems involving displacement s, velocity v and acceleration a) · difficulty 4

(a) Differentiate the displacement with respect to time to get the velocity $v(t)=3t^{2}-12t+9$ (M1), which factors as $3(t^{2}-4t+3)=3(t-1)(t-3)$ (A1)(AG). Differentiate once more to obtain the acceleration $a(t)=6t-12$ (A1). The velocity factorisation is exact, because expanding the bracket restores $3t^{2}-12t+9$, and this factored form makes the rest times immediate to read without solving a general quadratic.

(b) The particle is at rest when $v(t)=0$, so solve $3(t-1)(t-3)=0$ (M1). This gives $t-1=0$ or $t-3=0$, hence the two times $t=1$ and $t=3$ (A1). These are the only moments at which the velocity vanishes, so the direction of travel changes at both of these instants and the journey must be split there.

(c) Substitute each rest time into the displacement. At $t=1$ we have $s(1)=1-6+9=4$ (M1), and at $t=3$ we have $s(3)=27-54+27=0$ (A1). The displacements at the two stopping times are therefore $4$ m and $0$ m respectively, and the particle is momentarily stationary at each of these positions before it sets off again.

(d) Between $t=0$ and $t=4$ the particle moves from $s(0)=0$ out to $s(1)=4$, back to $s(3)=0$, then out again to $s(4)=4$ (M1). The three legs each have length $|4-0|=4$, $|0-4|=4$ and $|4-0|=4$ (A1), so the total distance is $4+4+4=12$ m (A1). Summing the absolute leg lengths is the only correct way to measure the path actually travelled, and the distinction between total distance and net displacement is the central idea tested here, so the total path length is the sum of three separate four-metre legs, which is why it equals $12$ and not the net displacement of $4$.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for the velocity $3t^{2}-12t+9$, (A1)(AG) for the factorised velocity $3(t-1)(t-3)$, and (A1) for the acceleration $a(t)=6t-12$. A candidate who differentiates only once and states the wrong acceleration loses the last mark but keeps the velocity marks if the differentiation is otherwise correct.

(b) Award (M1) for solving $v(t)=0$ and (A1) for both times $t=1$ and $t=3$. Follow through from an incorrect velocity factorisation is allowed if the solving is consistent with it. Both rest times are needed for the distance in part (d).

(c) Award (M1) for substituting into $s(t)$ and (A1) for the pair of displacements $4$ and $0$. The order is important: $s(1)=4$ and $s(3)=0$. A candidate who swaps the two values earns at most one mark.

(d) Award (M1) for identifying the four positional values $0$, $4$, $0$, $4$, (A1) for the three leg lengths each equal to $4$, and (A1) for the total $12$. A candidate who reports the net displacement $4$ instead of the total distance forfeits the final marks. The absolute values are essential to the method.

83. The area trapped between a parabola and a line

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.11-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.11 (definite integrals, including analytical approach to areas between curves) · difficulty 4
