so $\Delta > 0$ for $k < 2 - 2\sqrt{2}$ or $k > 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$, and the two roots coincide at $k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ (the root $k = 2 - 2\sqrt{2}$ gives a double root $t = \tfrac{k}{2} = 1 - \sqrt{2} < 0$ and so corresponds to no solution in $x$) (A1).

Strictly, the double root exists at both values of $k$; the two values are $2 - 2\sqrt{2} \approx -0.828$ and $2 + 2\sqrt{2} \approx 4.828$, and it is only the second that is positive.

(c) Since $t = 3^{x}$, the original equation has exactly one solution precisely when the quadratic in $t$ has exactly one positive root. Write the roots $t_{1}, t_{2}$, with
$$t_{1} + t_{2} = k, \qquad t_{1}t_{2} = k + 1 \;\; (M1).$$

Case $k < -1$. Then $k + 1 < 0$, so the product of the roots is negative and the roots have opposite signs: exactly one of them is positive, whatever the discriminant says. Since $k < -1 < 2 - 2\sqrt{2}$, the discriminant is indeed positive, so both roots are real. The original equation therefore has exactly one solution (A1).

For a concrete value take $k = -2$. Then $t^{2} + 2t - 1 = 0$ gives $t = -1 \pm \sqrt{2}$, of which only $t = \sqrt{2} - 1$ is positive, so
$$x = \log_{3}(\sqrt{2} - 1) \approx -0.802 \;\; (A1).$$

Case $k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2} \approx 4.828$. Here $\Delta = 0$ and the double root is $t = \tfrac{k}{2} = 1 + \sqrt{2} > 0$, so there is exactly one positive root and exactly one solution of the original equation, $x = \log_{3}(1 + \sqrt{2}) \approx 0.802$.

The remaining cases give no solution or two. For $2 - 2\sqrt{2} < k < 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ we have $\Delta < 0$ and there are no real roots at all. For $-1 < k < 0$ the product $k + 1$ is positive and the sum $k$ is negative, so both roots are negative and neither is admissible. For $k > 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ both roots are positive and there are two solutions. At $k = -1$ the roots are $0$ and $-1$, neither admissible, so there are none. At $k = 0$ the equation reads $t^{2} + 1 = 0$, with no real roots.

Hence the original equation has exactly one real solution if and only if
$$k < -1 \quad \text{or} \quad k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2}.$$

(d) The answer to (c) is not just $k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ because the discriminant answers a different question. It decides whether the two roots of the quadratic in $t$ are real, not whether they are *positive*, and $t = 3^{x}$ admits only positive values (A1).

The signs are read off the sum and the product. For $k < -1$ the product $t_{1}t_{2} = k + 1$ is negative, so the two roots have opposite signs (A1); one is positive and one is negative, and the negative one is simply discarded by the restriction. The discriminant is positive there too, so a candidate who checks only $\Delta \ge 0$ and then asks for equality will find $k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ and stop, having excluded an entire unbounded interval of valid answers. The discriminant is a necessary condition for the roots to be real; the product's sign is the condition that decides how many of them survive (A1).

In the middle range $-1 < k < 0$ the same reading runs the other way: the product is positive and the sum $k$ is negative, so both roots are negative and no solution survives at all. The sign pair $(\text{sum}, \text{product})$ is the complete description, and the discriminant is only one of its two ingredients.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for the substitution and the reduced quadratic, (A1) for $t > 0$, (A1) for the one-to-one correspondence between positive $t$ and real $x$. A candidate who notices $9^{x} = (3^{x})^{2}$ but writes the restriction as $t \ne 0$ scores the first two marks only.

(b) (M1) for the discriminant, (A1) for the two boundary values $2 \pm 2\sqrt{2}$, (A1) for the correct sign pattern of the quadratic inequality. The final (A1) also covers the statement that only $k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ gives a positive double root; a candidate who names $2 - 2\sqrt{2}$ as a second value where one solution occurs forfeits it, because $t = 1 - \sqrt{2}$ is negative.

(c) (M1) for writing down the sum and product, (A1) for the interval $k < -1$ justified by the sign of the product, (A1) for a worked value of $k$ less than $-1$ with its solution. The value $k = -2$ giving $x = \log_{3}(\sqrt{2} - 1)$ is the expected answer; any $k < -1$ with correct work is accepted. A candidate who gives only $k = 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ scores the first mark only.

(d) (A1) for distinguishing "real roots" from "positive roots", (A1) for the explicit use of the product $k + 1$, (A1) for the explicit use of the sum $k$ in at least one of the two cases. This is an explanation mark: the reasoning must be present, not merely the answer repeated.

Condonation: a candidate who handles the case $k = -1$ by observing that the roots are $0$ and $-1$ and neither is admissible scores full credit in (c) even if the interval is written as $-1 < k$ rather than $k \le -1$, provided the boundary behaviour is discussed. Forfeits: using $\Delta \ge 0$ as the whole condition, which produces the answer $k \ge 2 + 2\sqrt{2}$ with a spurious double count at the endpoint and no solution for $k < -1$ at all.

122. 4.14 Two unknown values of a discrete random variable recovered from the mean and the variance together, where either reading alone is satisfied by infinitely many pairs

[12 marks]MATH-AHL4.14-201

Topic 4: Statistics and probability · AHL 4.14 (variance of a discrete random variable) with AHL 4.11 (expected value) and SL 4.5 (probability of an event) · difficulty 4

(a) Using the definition $E(X) = \sum x\,P(X = x)$ (M1):
$$E(X) = 1 \cdot \tfrac{1}{2} + a \cdot \tfrac{1}{4} + b \cdot \tfrac{1}{4} = \frac{2 + a + b}{4} = 3,$$
so $2 + a + b = 12$ and $a + b = 10$ (A1).

One equation in two unknowns has infinitely many solutions in the positive integers, namely $(a, b) = (1, 9), (2, 8), (3, 7), (4, 6)$, and every one of them gives a distribution with mean $3$. The mean therefore fixes the *total* of the prizes and nothing about how that total is split. A second, independent reading of the distribution is needed, and the variance supplies it.

(b) The second moment is
$$E(X^{2}) = 1^{2} \cdot \tfrac{1}{2} + a^{2} \cdot \tfrac{1}{4} + b^{2} \cdot \tfrac{1}{4} = \frac{2 + a^{2} + b^{2}}{4} \;\; (M1).$$
Using $\operatorname{Var}(X) = E(X^{2}) - (E(X))^{2}$ with $\operatorname{Var}(X) = 6$ and $E(X) = 3$:
$$E(X^{2}) = 6 + 3^{2} = 15, \qquad \text{so} \qquad \frac{2 + a^{2} + b^{2}}{4} = 15, \qquad a^{2} + b^{2} = 58 \;\; (A1).$$

Now use $a + b = 10$. Squaring, $a^{2} + 2ab + b^{2} = 100$, so
$$2ab = 100 - 58 = 42, \qquad ab = 21 \;\; (M1).$$
The two numbers whose sum is $10$ and whose product is $21$ are the roots of
$$z^{2} - 10z + 21 = 0, \qquad (z - 3)(z - 7) = 0, \qquad z = 3 \text{ or } z = 7 \;\; (A1).$$

Since $a$ and $b$ are interchangeable in both the mean and the variance -- the distribution is symmetric between them -- the two roots are the two prizes, and the condition $a < b$ assigns $a = 3$ and $b = 7$.

Check: $E(X) = \dfrac{2 + 3 + 7}{4} = 3$; $E(X^{2}) = \dfrac{2 + 9 + 49}{4} = 15$; $\operatorname{Var}(X) = 15 - 9 = 6$. Both readings are satisfied.

(c) If $\operatorname{Var}(X) = 4$ instead, then $E(X^{2}) = 4 + 9 = 13$, so $\dfrac{2 + a^{2} + b^{2}}{4} = 13$ and $a^{2} + b^{2} = 50$ (M1). With $a + b = 10$ this gives $2ab = 100 - 50 = 50$, so $ab = 25$, and the quadratic is
$$z^{2} - 10z + 25 = 0, \qquad (z - 5)^{2} = 0 \;\; (A1).$$
Its only root is $z = 5$, so $a = b = 5$.

That is not merely a coincidence of the numbers: $\operatorname{Var}(X) = 4$ is the smallest variance attainable, because for fixed mean the variance is minimised when the distribution puts as much probability as possible at the mean. Here the mean $3$ lies between the value $1$ (probability $\tfrac{1}{2}$) and the equal prizes, and pushing both prizes to $5$ is exactly the point at which the two remaining outcomes coincide.

The stated form of the distribution requires $a < b$, and more fundamentally the question describes three prizes while $a = b$ collapses the distribution to the two values $1$ and $5$ (A1). So $\operatorname{Var}(X) = 4$ is inconsistent with the description, and the corrected value $6$ is the one that restores the third prize.

(d) By definition
$$P(X \le a) = P(X = 1) + P(X = a) = \tfrac{1}{2} + \tfrac{1}{4} = \tfrac{3}{4} \;\; (M1).$$
So the statement $P(X \le a) = \tfrac{3}{4}$ is not an extra restriction at all: it holds automatically for every choice of $a$ and $b$ with $a < b$, provided only that $a$ is the middle value in order. It does, however, identify $a$ as the upper quartile of the distribution and, since $a < b$, place $a$ strictly between the two larger values (A1).

What it leaves undetermined is the size of $a$, and this is the point worth stating: $P(X \le a) = \tfrac{3}{4}$ is a statement about probabilities, and probabilities are unchanged when the values are relabelled, so it carries no information about the numerical value of $a$ at all (A1). To determine $a$ one still needs one numerical reading of the distribution, and the mean is enough on its own here: given $a$ and $a + b = 10$ we get $b = 10 - a$, so any one of the three prizes, the mean, or the variance determines the rest. The auditor's statement is therefore *necessary but not sufficient*, which is why the organiser's mean and variance were both needed in part (b).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for forming the expectation with the stated probabilities, (A1) for $a + b = 10$ with the observation that one equation in two unknowns leaves a family of solutions. Listing the four admissible pairs is good practice but is not required for the mark.

(b) (M1) for $E(X^{2})$ in terms of $a^{2} + b^{2}$, (A1) for $a^{2} + b^{2} = 58$, (M1) for using $a + b = 10$ and squaring to obtain $ab = 21$, (A1) for $a = 3, b = 7$ from the quadratic. A candidate who guesses $(3,7)$ from the admissible pairs in (a) must still show the variance condition to earn the marks; the guess alone scores the (M1) for forming $E(X^{2})$ and nothing further, because the question asks for the determination, not for the answer.

(c) (M1) for converting $\operatorname{Var}(X) = 4$ into $a^{2} + b^{2} = 50$, (A1) for the degenerate quadratic and $a = b = 5$, (A1) for the reason the stated form excludes it. "Because the question says $a < b$" earns the third mark only if it is connected to the collapse from three prizes to two; a bare restatement of the inequality does not, because the deeper reason is that a repeated prize is not a third prize.

(d) (M1) for evaluating $P(X \le a)$ as $\tfrac{1}{2} + \tfrac{1}{4}$, (A1) for identifying the statement as automatic given the ordering, or as the upper-quartile condition, (A1) for explaining that a probability statement carries no information about the numerical value of $a$. A candidate who answers "it means $a = 3$" has confused the automatic inequality with a determination and scores zero on the third mark.

Condonation: in (b), a candidate who uses $\operatorname{Var}(X) = E(X^{2}) - 9$ correctly but who then solves the quadratic and assigns the roots the other way round -- $a = 7, b = 3$ -- loses nothing in (b) but must be consistent in (c); the condition $a < b$ is given in the stem. Follow-through: a candidate whose (a) is wrong and who continues with their own $a + b$ is credited fully in (b) where the method is correct.

123. 1.13 Loci in the complex plane: a circle and a half-line

[12 marks]MATH-AHL1.13-101

Topic 1: Number and algebra · AHL 1.13 (modulus-argument form; loci in the complex plane, |z - z0| = r and arg z = theta) · difficulty 4

(a) Put $z = x + iy$. Then $z - (3+2i) = (x-3) + i(y-2)$, and the condition $|z-(3+2i)| = 2$ says that $\sqrt{(x-3)^2 + (y-2)^2} = 2$. Squaring both sides gives $(x-3)^2 + (y-2)^2 = 4$. (M1) This is the equation of the circle with centre $3 + 2i$ and radius $2$. (A1)(AG)

(b) Every point of the half-line $\arg z = \frac{\pi}{4}$ has equal real and imaginary parts, so write $z = t(1+i)$ with $t > 0$. (M1) Substituting into the circle equation gives $(t-3)^2 + (t-2)^2 = 4$. (M1) Expanding, $2t^2 - 10t + 9 = 0$. (A1) The quadratic formula gives $t = \frac{10 \pm \sqrt{100-72}}{4} = \frac{10 \pm \sqrt{28}}{4} = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{7}}{2}$. For a point $z = t(1+i)$ the modulus is $|z| = t|1+i| = t\sqrt{2}$, so the two moduli are $\frac{(5+\sqrt{7})\sqrt{2}}{2} = \frac{5\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{14}}{2}$ and $\frac{(5-\sqrt{7})\sqrt{2}}{2} = \frac{5\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{14}}{2}$. (A1)(A1) Numerically these are $5.41$ and $1.66$, and the diagram confirms that both lie in the first quadrant.

(c) The two points are $z_1 = t_1(1+i)$ and $z_2 = t_2(1+i)$, and from the quadratic $t_1 - t_2 = \sqrt{7}$. (M1) Because both points lie on the same ray, $PQ = |z_1 - z_2| = (t_1-t_2)|1+i| = \sqrt{7}\,\sqrt{2} = \sqrt{14}$. (A1)

(d) The point of a circle closest to a given point lies on the line joining that point to the centre, so $W$ lies on the line from the origin through $3+2i$. (M1) The distance from the origin to the centre is $|3+2i| = \sqrt{13}$, and the origin is outside the circle because $\sqrt{13} > 2$, so $|W| = \sqrt{13} - 2$. (A1) Since $W$ lies on that line, $W = \frac{\sqrt{13}-2}{\sqrt{13}}(3+2i) = \frac{3(\sqrt{13}-2)}{\sqrt{13}} + \frac{2(\sqrt{13}-2)}{\sqrt{13}}i \approx 1.336 + 0.891i$. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) Award M1 for translating the modulus condition into an algebraic equation, either by writing $z = x+iy$ and squaring, or by quoting the circle form directly. Award A1 for centre $3+2i$ and radius $2$. Accept $(x-3)^2+(y-2)^2=4$ as an equivalent statement of the locus. (b) M1 for the parameterisation $z = t(1+i)$ or for any equivalent use of $y = x$ with $x > 0$; M1 for substituting into the circle equation; A1 for the reduced quadratic $2t^2-10t+9=0$; A1 for $t = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{7}}{2}$; A1 for both moduli in exact form. A candidate who obtains $t$ correctly but reports $\frac{5 \pm \sqrt{7}}{2}$ as $|z|$ has omitted the factor $\sqrt{2}$ and scores 4 of the 5 marks. Do not award the final A1 for decimal answers alone. (c) M1 for $PQ = (t_1-t_2)|1+i|$, A1 for $\sqrt{14}$. Follow through from the candidate's own $t_1, t_2$ provided the method is sound. (d) M1 for placing $W$ on the line joining the origin to the centre, A1 for $|W| = \sqrt{13}-2$, A1 for the exact complex form. Accept $1.34 + 0.89i$ only when the exact form is also given, since the question asks for exact values. Do not accept $W$ found by scaling the centre by any other factor.

124. Exact coordinates on the unit circle for an obtuse angle, the tangent of that angle, the chord between two symmetric points and the area of the sector

[11 marks]MATH-AHL3.6-501

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · SL 3.6 (the unit circle and the definition of sin, cos and tan for angles greater than 90 degrees) with SL 3.4 (the circle: radian measure and arc length) and SL 3.5 (the equation sin x = c and its solutions on a stated interval) · difficulty 4

(a) On the unit circle a point at angle $\theta$ has coordinates
$(\cos\theta, \sin\theta)$. The reference angle for $\dfrac{5\pi}{6}$ is
$\pi - \dfrac{5\pi}{6} = \dfrac{\pi}{6}$, and because $\dfrac{5\pi}{6}$ is in the second
quadrant its cosine is negative and its sine is positive:

$$\cos\left( \frac{5\pi}{6} \right) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \qquad
\sin\left( \frac{5\pi}{6} \right) = \frac{1}{2}.$$

Hence

$$P\left( -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \; \frac{1}{2} \right).$$ (A1)

For $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$, which is in the first quadrant, both coordinates are positive:

$$Q\left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \; \frac{1}{2} \right).$$ (A1)

The two points have the same $y$-coordinate, so the chord $PQ$ is horizontal, and $P$ and $Q$
are mirror images of one another in the $y$-axis.

(b) The tangent of an angle is the quotient of the sine and the cosine, which on the unit
circle is the quotient of the two coordinates:

$$\tan\left( \frac{5\pi}{6} \right)
= \frac{\sin(5\pi/6)}{\cos(5\pi/6)}
= \frac{\;1/2\;}{-\sqrt{3}/2}.$$ (M1)

Dividing by the fraction $-\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ multiplies by its reciprocal:

$$\tan\left( \frac{5\pi}{6} \right) = \frac{1}{2} \times \left( -\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \right)
= -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}.$$ (A1)

The value is negative, as it must be for an angle in the second quadrant, where the sine is
positive and the cosine is negative.

(c) The chord $PQ$ joins $P\left( -\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \dfrac{1}{2} \right)$ and
$Q\left( \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \dfrac{1}{2} \right)$. Because the $y$-coordinates are equal, the
chord is horizontal and its length is the difference of the $x$-coordinates:

$$PQ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - \left( -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right)
= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \sqrt{3}.$$ (M1)(A1)

Alternatively, the angle $POQ$ is

$$\frac{5\pi}{6} - \frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{4\pi}{6} = \frac{2\pi}{3},$$

and $OP$ and $OQ$ are both radii of the unit circle, so triangle $POQ$ is isosceles with two
sides of length $1$ and included angle $\dfrac{2\pi}{3}$. The chord is therefore the base of
that triangle:

$$PQ = 2(1)\sin\left( \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{2\pi}{3} \right)
= 2\sin\left( \frac{\pi}{3} \right) = 2 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \sqrt{3}.$$ (M1)(A1)

Both routes give $\sqrt{3}$, as required. (AG)

(d) The sector $POQ$ has radius $r = 1$ and angle $\theta = \dfrac{2\pi}{3}$, so its area is

$$A = \frac{1}{2} r^{2} \theta
= \frac{1}{2}(1)^{2} \left( \frac{2\pi}{3} \right)
= \frac{\pi}{3}.$$ (M1)(A1)(A1)

The area of the shaded sector is $\dfrac{\pi}{3}$ square units. Note that the chord length found
in part (c) is not needed here; the area of a sector depends only on the radius and the angle
between the radii, whereas the chord depends on the angle as well but is not required once the
angle is known.

Markscheme notes

(a) (A1) for $P\left( -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \frac{1}{2} \right)$, (A1) for $Q\left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \frac{1}{2} \right)$. Accept the coordinates in either order provided the labels match. An answer of $P\left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \frac{1}{2} \right)$ scores (A0) for the first mark: the cosine of an angle in the second quadrant is negative. (b) (M1) for forming the quotient of the coordinates, (A1) for $-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}$ or the equivalent $-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$. Accept $-0.577$ only if given to at least three significant figures. A positive value scores (M1)(A0). (c) (M1) for a valid method, either the horizontal difference of the two $x$-coordinates or the isosceles-triangle relation with the correct included angle, (A1) for reaching $\sqrt{3}$, (M1)(A1) for the second valid method or (AG) where the student completes the algebra to the stated result. Do not award the method mark for merely asserting that the chord is $\sqrt{3}$. (d) (M1) for using $A = \frac{1}{2}r^{2}\theta$ with $r = 1$, (A1) for the correct angle $\frac{2\pi}{3}$, (A1) for $\frac{\pi}{3}$. Award follow-through from an incorrect angle in (c) only if the angle used is consistent with the coordinates given in (a).

125. 3.13 The closest point of a line segment is an endpoint when the foot of the perpendicular falls outside it

[6 marks]MATH-3.13-401

Topic 3: Geometry and trigonometry · SL 3.13 The definition of the scalar product of two vectors; SL 3.12 Concept of a vector, position vectors and displacement vectors; 3.1 The distance between two points in three dimensions · difficulty 4

(a) $\overrightarrow{AB} = 2\mathbf{i} + 4\mathbf{j} + 2\mathbf{k}$ and $\overrightarrow{AP} = 7\mathbf{i} + 3\mathbf{j} + 3\mathbf{k}$, so $\overrightarrow{AP}\cdot\overrightarrow{AB} = 14 + 12 + 6 = 32$ and $|\overrightarrow{AB}|^{2} = 4 + 16 + 4 = 24$ (M1). The closest point of the line is $\mathbf{a} + t\,\overrightarrow{AB}$ with $t = \dfrac{\overrightarrow{AP}\cdot\overrightarrow{AB}}{|\overrightarrow{AB}|^{2}} = \dfrac{32}{24} = \dfrac{4}{3}$, and since $\tfrac{4}{3} > 1$ that point lies beyond $B$ and therefore outside the segment $AB$ (A1).

(b) Because the closest point of the *line* is outside the segment, the closest point of the segment is its nearest endpoint, namely $B$ (M1), and the distance is $|\overrightarrow{BP}| = |5\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k}| = \sqrt{25 + 1 + 1} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3} \approx 5.20$ (A1).

(c) $\sqrt{73/3}$ is the perpendicular distance from $P$ to the infinite line through $A$ and $B$: $|\overrightarrow{AP}|^{2} = 49 + 9 + 9 = 67$, and $67 - \dfrac{32^{2}}{24} = 67 - \dfrac{128}{3} = \dfrac{73}{3}$ (M1). It is not the answer to (b) because the foot of that perpendicular is not a point of the segment. A segment is a bounded set, and the closest point of a bounded set need not be where the perpendicular falls: it can be, and here is, an endpoint. The perpendicular distance is smaller because it is measured to a point the segment does not contain, so it is a lower bound that the segment cannot attain (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for the two ingredients of the projection, $\overrightarrow{AP}\cdot\overrightarrow{AB} = 32$ and $|\overrightarrow{AB}|^{2} = 24$, however they are obtained; (A1) for $t = \tfrac{4}{3}$ together with the observation that the point corresponding to $t > 1$ lies beyond $B$. The comparison with $1$ is the mark: a candidate who computes $t = \tfrac{4}{3}$ and says nothing about what it means has done the arithmetic and missed the part.

(b) (M1) for recognising that the endpoint is now the closest point; (A1) for $\sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3}$. Accept $5.20$.

(c) (M1) for identifying the value as the distance to the line and reproducing the calculation that produces it; (A1) for the point that the segment is bounded so the minimum over the segment is attained at an endpoint. Do not credit 'it is wrong because the foot is outside' without the consequence, which is that the smaller number is unattainable.

126. 5.11 The moment of a region found from its symmetry, without ever knowing the function

[6 marks]MATH-5.11-401

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.11 Definite integrals, including the analytical approach; SL 5.5 Introduction to integration as anti-differentiation; 2.14 Odd and even functions · difficulty 4

(a) With $x = 4 - u$ we have $\mathrm{d}x = -\mathrm{d}u$ and the limits swap, so $I = \displaystyle\int_{0}^{4} (4 - u)\,f(4 - u)\,\mathrm{d}u$ (M1). By the symmetry, $f(4 - u) = f(2 + (2 - u)) = f(2 - (2 - u)) = f(u)$, so

$$I = \int_{0}^{4} (4 - u)\,f(u)\,\mathrm{d}u = 4\int_{0}^{4} f(u)\,\mathrm{d}u - \int_{0}^{4} u\,f(u)\,\mathrm{d}u = 4 \times 12 - I = 48 - I \quad (A1).$$

(b) $2I = 48$, so $I = 24$ (M1) (A1). This is $2 \times 12$: the integral of $x\,f(x)$ over the region is the abscissa of the axis of symmetry multiplied by the area, which is what a centroid argument would also give, since the centroid of a region with a vertical axis of symmetry lies on that axis.

(c) The substitution $x = 4 - u$ reflects the interval $[0,\,4]$ about the line $x = 2$, and that reflection maps the interval onto itself (M1). Reflecting $[0,\,4]$ about the line $x = 3$ instead sends it to $[2,\,6]$, which is not the interval the integral runs over, so the substituted integral cannot be renamed as the original one and the symmetry of $g$ produces no relation between the integral and itself. A symmetry is only useful here if it is a symmetry of the interval as well as of the function (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for carrying out the substitution correctly, including the interchange of limits; (A1) for using $f(4 - u) = f(u)$ and separating the integral into $4\int f - I$. The separation is the mark: a candidate who writes the substituted integral and stops has not yet produced an equation in $I$.

(b) (M1) for $2I = 48$; (A1) for $I = 24$.

(c) (M1) for noticing that the reflection is of the interval and not only of the function; (A1) for the conclusion. Credit a candidate who makes the point by remarking that $x = 6 - u$ would be the substitution matching an axis at $x = 3$ and that it turns $[0,\,4]$ into $[2,\,6]$. Watch for the converse error, which is to claim the method still works; it does not, and a candidate who thinks it does has not noticed that the limits moved.

127. 5.17 The area between a sideways parabola and a chord, integrated with respect to y

[6 marks]MATH-AHL5.17-401

Topic 5: Calculus · AHL 5.17 Area of the region enclosed by a curve and the y-axis; SL 5.11 Definite integrals, including the analytical approach; SL 2.2 Concept of a function, domain, range and graph · difficulty 4

(a) A point on both curves satisfies $y^{2} = 3 - 2y$, so $y^{2} + 2y - 3 = 0$ (M1). Factorising, $(y + 3)(y - 1) = 0$, so $y = -3$ or $y = 1$. Substituting back gives $x = y^{2} = 9$ when $y = -3$ and $x = 1$ when $y = 1$, so the two points are $(9,\,-3)$ and $(1,\,1)$ (A1).

(b) For every $y$ between $-3$ and $1$ the line lies to the right of the curve, because $3 - 2y - y^{2} = -(y + 3)(y - 1)$ is positive on that interval (M1). A horizontal strip at height $y$ therefore has width $(3 - 2y) - y^{2}$ and height $dy$, and the area of $R$ is the sum of those strips:

$$A = \int_{-3}^{1}\left(3 - 2y - y^{2}\right)dy = \left[3y - y^{2} - \tfrac{1}{3}y^{3}\right]_{-3}^{1} \;(M1).$$

At the upper limit, $3 - 1 - \tfrac{1}{3} = \tfrac{5}{3}$. At the lower limit, $-9 - 9 + 9 = -9$. Subtracting,

$$A = \tfrac{5}{3} - (-9) = \tfrac{32}{3} \approx 10.67 \;(A1).$$

So the enclosed area is $\tfrac{32}{3}$ square units, which is a little over ten and a half. Notice that the antiderivative is a cubic whose value at the lower limit is negative: the region straddles the $x$-axis, and the integral handles that without any adjustment because the strips are measured horizontally and each one keeps its own sign of $x$, not of $y$.

(c) Integrating with respect to $x$ means slicing the region vertically, and the top of a vertical slice is not the same curve all the way across (M1). To the left of the point where the two boundaries cross, the slice runs from the lower branch of the parabola to the upper branch, so its height is $2\sqrt{x}$; to the right of that point the upper branch of the parabola lies above the line, so the height becomes $\dfrac{3 - x}{2} + \sqrt{x}$ and the two curves have exchanged roles as the upper boundary. The change happens where $y^{2} = 3 - 2y$ has its positive root, that is at $y = 1$, and there $x = 1$; this is the dashed vertical line drawn in the figure, and it is the only vertical slice for which the two descriptions agree, since it runs from $y = -1$ to $y = 1$ (A1). A candidate working in $x$ must therefore write

$$A = \int_{0}^{1} 2\sqrt{x}\,dx + \int_{1}^{9}\left(\frac{3 - x}{2} + \sqrt{x}\right)dx = \tfrac{4}{3} + \tfrac{28}{3} = \tfrac{32}{3},$$

which is the same area at roughly twice the work: two integrals, two antiderivatives, and a split point that has to be found before either integral can be set up. Investing in the coordinate that matches the shape of the region is what removes the split.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for forming $y^{2} = 3 - 2y$ or the equivalent and reducing it to $y^{2} + 2y - 3 = 0$; (A1) for both points $(9,\,-3)$ and $(1,\,1)$ with the substitution shown. Accept a candidate who solves for $x$ first, provided both coordinates of both points are stated. A candidate who gives the roots $y = -3$ and $y = 1$ but never forms the coordinates scores the first mark only.

(b) (M1) for setting up the integral with the line minus the curve over $y$ from $-3$ to $1$, with the sign of $3 - 2y - y^{2}$ justified or evidently checked; (M1) for the correct antiderivative $3y - y^{2} - \tfrac{1}{3}y^{3}$; (A1) for $\tfrac{32}{3}$ or $10.7$ to three significant figures. Three fields; credit two, and the displayed integral already earns the first. Do not accept an area given as negative, and do not accept $\tfrac{27}{3}$ or $9$ from substituting the limits in the wrong order.

(c) (M1) for the observation that the upper boundary changes from the upper branch of the parabola to the straight line; (A1) for $x = 1$, correctly located as the point where the interval of $y$ is the largest. Award the second mark for $x = 1$ alone, but only if the change of upper boundary has been stated. A candidate who says the region must be split 'because it is curved' has not identified what changes and scores nothing.

128. 5.11 Two unbounded regions, one of which encloses a finite area, decided by the exponent

[6 marks]MATH-5.11-402

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.11 Definite integrals, including the analytical approach; SL 5.10 Indefinite integral of x to the power n (n rational); AHL 5.13 The evaluation of limits · difficulty 4

(a) Writing $x^{-2}$ and integrating, $$\int_{1}^{t} x^{-2}\,dx = \left[-x^{-1}\right]_{1}^{t} = -\frac{1}{t} + 1 = 1 - \frac{1}{t} \;(M1)(A1).$$

As $t$ increases without bound the term $\dfrac{1}{t}$ tends to $0$, so the partial area increases towards the value $1$. The shaded region is unbounded in extent but encloses exactly one square unit: to reach an area of $0.99$ the region must be extended to $t = 100$, to reach $0.999$ to $t = 1000$, and no finite $t$ ever achieves the full unit (A1). Each doubling of the extent adds less than half of what the previous doubling added, and the total therefore settles.

(b) Writing $x^{-1/2}$ and integrating, $$\int_{1}^{t} x^{-1/2}\,dx = \left[2x^{1/2}\right]_{1}^{t} = 2\sqrt{t} - 2 \;(M1)(A1).$$

Here the partial area increases without limit as $t$ increases, because $2\sqrt{t} - 2$ does: extending the region to $t = 100$ gives an area of $18$, to $t = 10\,000$ an area of $198$, and to pass an area of $200$ the region must be taken out to $t = 10\,201$. There is no number that the area approaches from below, so the shaded region in the right-hand panel has no finite area (A1). The distinction between the two panels is therefore not that one region is longer than the other -- both are equally long -- but that one of them has a measurable area and the other does not.

(c) Both integrands are powers, $x^{-p}$, and both antiderivatives are powers, $x^{1-p}$ divided by $1-p$ (M1). What decides the outcome is the sign of the exponent $1 - p$ in the antiderivative. When $p > 1$ that exponent is negative, so the antiderivative is a negative power of $t$ and it tends to $0$ as $t$ grows; the area then settles at the finite value $\dfrac{1}{p-1}$, which for $p = 2$ is $1$, and for $p = 1.5$ would be $2$. When $p \leq 1$ the exponent is positive or zero, so the antiderivative grows with $t$ and there is no finite limit; for $p = \tfrac{1}{2}$ it grows like $\sqrt{t}$ (A1). The property that decides, then, is how fast the integrand decays: a finite tail requires the integrand to fall away faster than $\dfrac{1}{x}$, and the two panels differ only in that their exponents are $2$ and $\tfrac{1}{2}$, one above the threshold and one below it.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for integrating $x^{-2}$ rather than $x^{2}$, or for any correct antiderivative of $x^{-2}$; (A1) for the evaluation $1 - \dfrac{1}{t}$ with both limits used correctly; (A1) for the limit $1$, with the behaviour of $\dfrac{1}{t}$ stated. Three fields; credit two, and a candidate who writes down $1 - \dfrac{1}{t}$ and then states the area as $1$ without a word about the limit earns two of the three.

(b) (M1) for integrating $x^{-1/2}$; (A1) for $2\sqrt{t} - 2$; (A1) for the conclusion that the area is unbounded, with the reason that the expression grows without limit rather than settling. The conclusion is the mark that matters: an answer of '$\infty$' with no statement about the growth is worth the first two fields only.

(c) (M1) for comparing the antiderivatives of $x^{-p}$ and identifying the sign of $1 - p$ as the deciding feature; (A1) for expressing the criterion as $p > 1$, equivalently that the integrand must decay faster than $\dfrac{1}{x}$, and for observing that the two panels sit on opposite sides of that threshold. Do not accept a criterion stated in terms of the curves 'getting closer to the axis', since both of them do.

129. The indefinite integral that needs a logarithm and a cosine

[16 marks]MATH-AHL5.10-001

Topic 5: Calculus · SL 5.10 (indefinite integral of x^n, n in Q, sin x, cos x, 1/x, e^x) · difficulty 3

(a) Integrate the expression term by term, because the integral of a sum is the sum of the separate integrals. The power term gives $\int 3x^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=x^{3}$ (M1). The reciprocal term gives $\int -\frac{2}{x}\,\mathrm{d}x=-2\ln|x|$ (A1), where the absolute value is required so the logarithm is defined. The exponential term gives $\int 6\mathrm{e}^{x}\,\mathrm{d}x=6\mathrm{e}^{x}$ and the sine term gives $\int -4\sin x\,\mathrm{d}x=4\cos x$ (A1), since the derivative of $\cos x$ is $-\sin x$. No term affects another, so the four rules can be applied in any order, and adding the constant of integration gives the general antiderivative $F(x)=x^{3}-2\ln|x|+6\mathrm{e}^{x}+4\cos x+C$ (A1)(AG). Each standard rule is applied to one term independently, so the final result is the sum of four antiderivatives.

(b) Differentiate the answer to check it. We have $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(x^{3})=3x^{2}$ (M1), $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(-2\ln|x|)=-\frac{2}{x}$ (A1), $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(6\mathrm{e}^{x})=6\mathrm{e}^{x}$ and $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(4\cos x)=-4\sin x$ (A1). Adding these derivatives gives exactly the original integrand $3x^{2}-\frac{2}{x}+6\mathrm{e}^{x}-4\sin x$ (A1)(AG). This reverse check is valuable, because it catches any single sign slip instantly, and the derivative undoes the integration term by term, which is why the check confirms the antiderivative is correct.

(c) With $C=0$ and $x=1$, substitute to obtain $F(1)=1-2\ln 1+6\mathrm{e}^{1}+4\cos 1$ (M1). Because $\ln 1=0$, this simplifies to $1+6\mathrm{e}+4\cos 1$, which evaluates to $19.470900$ to six decimal places (A1). The logarithm contributes nothing at $x=1$, so the value is dominated by the exponential and the cosine term, and those two terms together account for almost the whole magnitude of the result.

(d) The condition $F(1)=7$ gives $19.470900+C=7$ (M1), so the constant is $C=7-19.470900=-12.470900$ (A1)(AG). This constant shifts the whole antiderivative downwards so that its graph passes through the point $(1,7)$. Choosing the constant in this way fixes a unique member of the family of antiderivatives, and the negative sign is required because the value without the constant already exceeds $7$, which is why $C$ must be negative.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for integrating the power term to $x^{3}$ and (A1) for $-2\ln|x|$; the absolute value bars are required for full credit. Award (A1) for $6\mathrm{e}^{x}$ and (A1) for $4\cos x$, noting the sign change that comes from integrating $-4\sin x$. The final (A1)(AG) is for the combined antiderivative $x^{3}-2\ln|x|+6\mathrm{e}^{x}+4\cos x+C$. A candidate who writes $\ln x$ without bars earns the term mark only, not full credit.

(b) Award (M1) for the derivative of the power term and (A1) for $-2/x$ from the logarithm. Award (A1) for $6\mathrm{e}^{x}$ and (A1) for $-4\sin x$, with (A1)(AG) for recovery of the original integrand. The confirmation must show the derivative equals the given integrand term by term.

(c) Award (M1) for substituting $x=1$ with $C=0$ and (A1) for the numerical value $19.470900$ to six decimal places. Accept $19.47$ if rounded, but the exact form $1+6\mathrm{e}+4\cos 1$ is preferred in the markscheme.

(d) Award (M1) for forming $19.470900+C=7$ and (A1)(AG) for $C=-12.470900$. Follow through from an incorrect part (c) value is allowed provided the subtraction is consistent with it. A candidate who misses the negative sign earns no credit for the final answer.

130. 2.15 The solution set of 2^x >= x^2, where the two exact intersections x = 2 and x = 4 are the only ones a natural window shows and the third intersection is at a negative value of x that the window excludes

[12 marks]MATH-AHL2.15-201

Topic 2: Functions · AHL 2.15 (solutions of g(x) >= f(x), both graphically and analytically) with SL 2.9 (exponential functions and their graphs) and SL 2.10 (solving equations graphically and analytically) · difficulty 3

(a) The diagram is drawn on $0 \le x \le 5$ and shows the two curves meeting at $(2,\,4)$ and $(4,\,16)$, since $2^{2} = 4 = 2^{2}$ and $2^{4} = 16 = 4^{2}$ (A1). Reading the sign of the difference between the curves off the diagram, the solid exponential curve lies above the dashed parabola from $x = 0$ up to $x = 2$, the parabola lies above the exponential curve between the two crossings, and the exponential curve is again above from $x = 4$ onwards (M1). For example at $x = 1$ the values are $2$ and $1$, at $x = 3$ they are $8$ and $9$, and at $x = 5$ they are $32$ and $25$ (A1). So on this interval the inequality holds for
$$0 \le x \le 2 \quad\text{and}\quad 4 \le x \le 5,$$
and the diagram shows exactly two intersections.
(b) The diagram begins at $x = 0$, so it cannot say anything about the curve for $x < 0$. On the negative side the two curves behave in opposite ways: $y = 2^{x}$ falls towards zero as $x$ becomes more negative, while $y = x^{2}$ grows without bound (M1). Immediately to the left of the origin the exponential curve is still the larger of the two, because $2^{0} = 1 > 0 = 0^{2}$, but far to the left the parabola is much the larger, because $2^{-10} = 0.00098$ against $100$ (A1). A curve that starts above the other and finishes below it must cross it at least once in between, so there is at least one intersection with a negative $x$-coordinate, and the diagram is drawn on an interval that excludes it (A1). The complete solution set therefore cannot be read off the diagram.
(c) Define $f(x) = 2^{x} - x^{2}$ for $x < 0$. At $x = -1$,
$$f(-1) = 2^{-1} - 1 = -0.5 < 0,$$
while at $x = -0.5$,
$$f(-0.5) = 2^{-0.5} - 0.25 = 0.70711 - 0.25 = +0.45711 > 0,$$
so a root lies between $-1$ and $-0.5$ (M1). Repeated bisection, or a numerical solver, narrows that bracket to
$$x = -0.7666646960\ldots \qquad\text{so}\qquad x \approx -0.7667 \;(A1),$$
correct to four decimal places. To see that there is only one negative root, differentiate:
$$f'(x) = 2^{x}\ln 2 - 2x .$$
For $x < 0$ the first term is positive because $2^{x} > 0$ and $\ln 2 > 0$, and the second term $-2x$ is positive as well. Hence $f'(x) > 0$ for every $x < 0$, so $f$ is strictly increasing on the whole negative half-line and can change sign there exactly once (A1). Since $f(x) \to -\infty$ as $x \to -\infty$ while $f(0) = 1 > 0$, that one sign change does occur, and the root is unique.
(d) Combining the three intervals, $2^{x} \ge x^{2}$ exactly when
$$-0.7667 \le x \le 2 \quad\text{or}\quad x \ge 4 \;(A1).$$
It remains to show that there is no intersection beyond $x = 4$. Consider the ratio $g(x) = \dfrac{2^{x}}{x^{2}}$ for $x \ge 5$. Differentiating,
$$g'(x) = \frac{2^{x}\,(x\ln 2 - 2)}{x^{3}},$$
and for $x \ge 5$ the factor $x\ln 2 - 2$ is at least $5(0.69315) - 2 = 1.4657 > 0$, so $g$ is strictly increasing on $x \ge 5$ (M1). At $x = 5$ the ratio is $32/25 = 1.28 > 1$, so $g(x) > 1$ for every $x \ge 5$, which means $2^{x} > x^{2}$ throughout (A1). The curves therefore do not meet again, and the largest intersection is the one at $x = 4$ (A1). The complete solution set is
$$\{x \in \mathbb{R} : -0.7667 \le x \le 2\} \cup \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x \ge 4\},$$
with $-0.7667$ given to four decimal places.

Markscheme notes

(a) (A1) for the two intersection points $(2,\,4)$ and $(4,\,16)$ read from the diagram, (M1) for comparing the two curves between and outside the crossings, (A1) for the answer $0 \le x \le 2$ together with $4 \le x \le 5$. A candidate who states only the two points without giving the intervals scores (A1) and nothing more, because the question asks for the set of values of $x$ and not for the intersections. Accept $4 \le x$ written without the upper limit of $5$ provided the candidate has made clear that the diagram only extends to $x = 5$.
(b) (M1) for the observation that the diagram begins at $x = 0$ and therefore cannot rule out anything to the left of the $y$-axis, (A1) for the correct behaviour of the two curves as $x \to -\infty$, namely $2^{x} \to 0$ and $x^{2} \to +\infty$, (A1) for the intermediate value argument that the two must therefore cross on the negative side. An answer that merely says 'the graph is not wide enough' without naming the behaviour of either curve scores (M1) only. Do not accept 'the diagram is inaccurate' or 'the scale is too small' as a substitute: the diagram is accurate on the interval it covers, and the defect is the interval, not the drawing.
(c) (M1) for a correct bracket containing the negative root, for example by evaluating $f$ at $-1$ and at $-0.5$ and observing the sign change, (A1) for $-0.7667$ to four decimal places. Accept $-0.7666$ or $-0.76666$ as intermediate working but the final answer must be rounded to four decimal places; condone $-0.7667$ obtained from any valid numerical method, including a graph plotter or a Newton iteration. (A1) for the uniqueness argument, which requires the derivative and the observation that both of its terms are positive for $x < 0$. A candidate who asserts uniqueness because 'the graphs cross only once' has used the conclusion it is meant to prove and scores no mark for it.
(d) (A1) for the correct solution set, with both the closed interval and the half-line, (M1) for a valid argument that no intersection lies beyond $x = 4$, (A1) for completing that argument, and (A1) for the statement of the set in set notation. The ratio argument is not the only acceptable one: a candidate may instead show that $2^{x} > x^{2}$ at $x = 5$ and that the difference is increasing thereafter, or may argue from the exponential eventually dominating any polynomial. Any of these earns the two marks. Follow-through: a candidate whose negative root from (c) is wrong but whose set is written consistently with it keeps the marks in (d). Forfeits: answering $0 \le x \le 2$ or $x \ge 4$, which discards the negative interval; answering $-0.7667 \le x \le 2$ only, which discards the half-line; and writing $x \ge 4$ as $4 < x$, which loses the endpoint.
